Completed means the initial curated cross-reference pass: the source verse and every listed target passage were read in the pinned Saints-Translation English records, and each retained pair has a specific explanation. It does not mean every possible biblical connection has been exhausted, or independent scholarly approval.
Reference edition: Saints Holy Bible 70-book edition, pinned Saints-Translation English records. Greek-based source numbering is retained.

Psalms 1:1
Blessed is the man who did not walk in the counsel of the ungodly, and did not stand in the way of sinners, and did not sit in the seat of pestilent men;
  Psalms 25:4-5 — Refusal of the company of evildoers: The blessed person refuses the counsel, way and seat of the ungodly. Psalm 25 likewise refuses to sit with an empty council or the ungodly and rejects an assembly of evildoers; the shared concern is chosen association with wrongdoing.

Psalms 1:2
but his will is in the Lord’s law, and on His law he will meditate day and night.
  Joshua 1:8 — Meditation on the law day and night: Both texts describe meditation on the law day and night. Joshua connects this discipline with doing what is written and prospering, while the psalm says the blessed person's will is in the Lord's law.

Psalms 1:3
He will be like the tree planted beside the outlets of waters, which will give its fruit in its season, and whose leaf will not fall off; and everything he does will be prospered.
  Jeremiah 17:7-8 — Watered tree and continuing fruitfulness: The psalm compares the blessed person to a tree beside waters, bearing seasonal fruit without losing its leaf. Jeremiah compares the person trusting the Lord to a tree beside waters that continues bearing fruit through heat and drought.

Psalms 1:4
Not so the ungodly, not so, but like chaff which the wind flings away from the face of the earth.
  Job 21:18 — Wind-scattered plant matter and the wicked: The psalm pictures the ungodly as chaff flung away by wind. In Job's discussion of the wicked, they are compared with straw before wind and dust swept by a storm; the shared image is instability and dispersal, within different arguments about their fate.

Psalms 1:5
Therefore the ungodly will not rise in judgment, nor sinners in the council of the righteous;
  Matthew 13:49-50 — Exclusion of the evil from the righteous in judgment: The psalm says the ungodly will not rise in judgment and excludes sinners from the righteous council. Jesus' explanation of the net separates the evil from the righteous at the age's consummation. This compares exclusion from the righteous company without turning the psalm's wording into a separate claim about resurrection.

Psalms 1:6
for the Lord knows the way of the righteous, and the way of the ungodly will perish.
  Psalms 36:18-20 — Known righteous ways and the perishing of sinners: Psalm 1 contrasts the Lord's knowledge of the righteous way with the perishing of the ungodly way. Psalm 36 says the Lord knows the ways of the blameless and contrasts their enduring inheritance with sinners who perish.

Psalms 2:1
Why did nations rage, and peoples meditate on empty things?
  Acts 4:25 — Quotation of the raging nations: The gathered believers quote the question about raging nations and peoples devising empty things, attributing it to God's speech through David. Their surrounding prayer applies the quoted opposition to the gathering against Jesus.

Psalms 2:2
The kings of the earth stood ready, and the rulers gathered together, against the Lord and against His anointed. Diapsalma.
  Acts 4:26-28 — Quotation applied to opposition against Jesus: Acts quotes the kings and rulers gathering against the Lord and His Christ, then names Herod, Pontius Pilate, Gentiles and Israel's peoples as gathering against God's anointed Servant Jesus. The target itself supplies that application.

Psalms 2:3
“Let us tear apart their bonds and cast their yoke away from us.”
  Jeremiah 5:5 — Rebellion pictured as broken yoke and bonds: The rebellious speakers propose tearing bonds and casting away a yoke. Jeremiah describes those who recognized the Lord's way nevertheless breaking the yoke and tearing the bonds together. The images of rejected restraint match, although Jeremiah addresses a different group.

Psalms 2:4
The One dwelling in heaven will laugh at them, and the Lord will sneer at them.
  Psalms 58:9 — The Lord's laughter at hostile nations: The heavenly Lord laughs and sneers at the rebels in Psalm 2. Psalm 58 likewise says the Lord will laugh them to scorn and reduce nations to nothing, presenting divine contempt for threatening opponents rather than fear of their power.

Psalms 2:5
Then He will speak to them in His anger and trouble them in His fury:
  Psalms 109:5-6 — Divine anger confronting kings and nations: The Lord's angry speech troubles the rulers who oppose Him in Psalm 2. Psalm 109 describes the Lord crushing kings on the day of His anger and judging among nations. The comparison concerns royal opposition subjected to divine anger, without identifying a single narrated battle.

Psalms 2:6
“But I was appointed king by Him on Zion, His holy mountain,
  2 Samuel 5:7-12 — Zion and kingship established by the Lord: The appointed king speaks of Zion as God's holy mountain. The narrative joins David's capture and residence in Zion with his recognition that the Lord prepared him as king. This supplies a concrete Davidic royal setting without identifying the psalm with that precise occasion.

Psalms 2:7
announcing the Lord’s decree: The Lord said to me, ‘You are My son; today I have begotten you.
  Acts 13:32-33 — Explicit citation of the second psalm: Acts explicitly names the second psalm and quotes the decree about the Son begotten today in its announcement that God fulfilled the fathers' promise by raising Jesus. The reference and its application are stated in the target itself.
  Hebrews 1:5 — Sonship decree in the comparison with angels: Hebrews quotes the Lord's declaration of sonship and begetting today while asking which angel ever received such speech. The royal decree is used in the target's argument concerning the Son's distinction from angels.
  Hebrews 5:5 — Sonship decree and the calling of Christ: Hebrews quotes the decree about sonship and begetting today when explaining that Christ did not glorify Himself to become high priest. Its context joins the quoted declaration to divine calling, rather than self-assumed honor.

Psalms 2:8
Ask of Me, and I will give you nations as your inheritance, and the ends of the earth as your possession.
  Daniel 7:14 — Granted authority over nations: The son is promised nations as an inheritance and the earth's ends as a possession. Daniel's vision describes authority given to the figure of the preceding verse and worship by all the earth's nations. Both portray worldwide dominion as granted, without forcing the two passages' figures into an independently established identity.

Psalms 2:9
You will shepherd them with an iron rod; you will crush them like a potter’s vessel.’”
  Isaiah 30:14 — Judgment as the shattering of pottery: The nations are to be crushed like a potter's vessel. Isaiah compares the coming collapse to an earthen vessel shattered beyond useful fragments. The particular comparison is the destructive pottery image; Isaiah's warning concerns its own disobedient audience.

Psalms 2:10
And now, kings, understand; be instructed, all who judge the earth.
  Wisdom of Solomon 6:1-3 — Kings and judges summoned to understand: Both texts directly call kings to understand and the earth's judges to receive instruction. Wisdom explains that their authority comes from the Lord and that He will examine their actions, developing the accountability implicit in the psalm's warning.

Psalms 2:11
Serve the Lord with fear, and exult in Him with trembling.
  Hebrews 12:28-29 — Service to God with reverent fear: The psalm commands service to the Lord with fear and rejoicing with trembling. Hebrews urges acceptable service with reverence and awe, adjoining the warning that God is a consuming fire. Both join worship with serious awareness of divine judgment.

Psalms 2:12
Take hold of instruction, lest the Lord become angry and you perish from a righteous way. When His fury burns quickly, blessed are all who have trusted in Him.
  Proverbs 1:29-33 — Instruction accepted or rejected and contrasting outcomes: The psalm commands taking hold of instruction, warning of anger and perishing while blessing those who trust. Wisdom's speech contrasts refusal of counsel and fear of the Lord with the hopeful, secure dwelling of the one who listens. The connection preserves the psalm's actual instruction wording.

Psalms 3:1
A psalm of David, when he was fleeing from the presence of Absalom his son.
  2 Samuel 15:13-16 — Named historical setting of flight from Absalom: The title places David's psalm during flight from his son Absalom. The narrative reports Israel's support for Absalom and David's command to flee Jerusalem, followed by his departure with his household; it directly supplies the setting named by the title.

Psalms 3:2
Lord, why have those who afflict me multiplied? Many rise up against me.
  2 Samuel 15:12-13 — Increasing opposition in Absalom's conspiracy: The speaker laments multiplied afflicters and many rising against him. In the Absalom setting specified by the title, the narrative expressly says the conspiracy strengthened and the people with Absalom increased, before the report that Israel's hearts followed him.

Psalms 3:3
Many say to my soul, “There is no salvation for him in his God.” Diapsalma.
  2 Samuel 16:7-12 — Hostile interpretation of David's calamity: The psalm reports claims that God offers the speaker no salvation. During David's flight, Shimei interprets his calamity and the transfer of the kingdom to Absalom as the Lord's repayment, while David still hopes for the Lord's favor. This compares the hostile theological accusation without identifying the psalm's many speakers solely with Shimei.

Psalms 3:4
But Thou, Lord, art my helper, my glory and the One who lifts my head.
  Psalms 26:5-6 — Divine help and the raising of the head: Psalm 3 calls the Lord its helper and the one who lifts the speaker's head. Psalm 26 describes God's shelter and the raising of the speaker's head above enemies, making lifted head imagery an expression of protection and restored standing.

Psalms 3:5
With my voice I cried to the Lord, and He heard me from His holy mountain. Diapsalma.
  Psalms 17:7 — The cry heard from the holy dwelling: The speaker's voiced cry is heard from the Lord's holy mountain. Psalm 17 likewise recounts a distressed cry whose voice God hears from His holy temple. Both locate the answering of the afflicted worshipper's voice at God's holy dwelling.

Psalms 3:6
I lay down and slept; I was awakened, for the Lord will help me.
  Psalms 4:9 — Trust expressed through lying down and sleeping: Psalm 3 recounts lying down, sleeping and awakening because of the Lord's help. Psalm 4 resolves to lie down and sleep in peace because the Lord grants hopeful dwelling. Sleep becomes a concrete expression of dependence on divine protection.

Psalms 3:7
I will not fear tens of thousands of people who together attack me all around.
  Psalms 26:3 — Fearlessness before an encircling force: Psalm 3 refuses fear of tens of thousands attacking on every side. Psalm 26 similarly refuses fear even if an army deploys and war rises against the speaker. The shared affirmation specifically concerns military encirclement or attack.

Psalms 3:8
Rise, Lord! Save me, my God! For Thou didst strike all who were my enemies without cause; Thou didst crush sinners’ teeth.
  Psalms 57:7 — God breaking the teeth of enemies: The appeal for rescue recalls God crushing sinners' teeth. Psalm 57 says God shattered their teeth and crushed lions' grinders. Both use broken teeth to represent the disabling of threatening wicked power.

Psalms 3:9
Salvation belongs to the Lord, and Thy blessing is upon Thy people.
  Jonah 2:10 — Deliverance assigned to the Lord: Psalm 3 declares that salvation belongs to the Lord and speaks of His blessing upon His people. Jonah's vow of praise and thanksgiving ends by assigning deliverance to the Lord. The concluding attribution places rescue with God rather than the endangered worshipper.

Psalms 4:2
When I called, the God of my righteousness heard me. In affliction Thou madest room for me. Have compassion on me, and hear my prayer.
  Psalms 117:5 — Room granted in response to a distressed call: The speaker recalls being heard and given room in affliction. Psalm 117 similarly says that a call made in affliction was answered with room to breathe. The repeated contrast of constriction and enlarged space describes answered distress.

Psalms 4:3
Sons of men, how long will you be dull of heart? Why do you love vanity and seek falsehood? Diapsalma.
  Jonah 2:9 — Vanity and falsehood as misdirected attachment: The psalm challenges love of vanity and pursuit of falsehood. Jonah contrasts those guarding vain and false things with his own thanksgiving, saying they abandoned their mercy. Both reject attachment to vanity and falsehood as a spiritual misdirection.

Psalms 4:5
Be angry, and do not sin; speak in your hearts, and upon your beds be pierced with remorse. Diapsalma.
  Ephesians 4:26-27 — Reuse of the command about anger without sin: Ephesians repeats the psalm's command to be angry and not sin, adding that anger must not outlast the sunset or give room to the devil. The psalm's own continuation directs inward speech and remorse on the bed; the applications remain distinct.

Psalms 4:6
Offer a sacrifice of righteousness, and hope in the Lord.
  Psalms 50:19-21 — Righteous sacrifice and the worshipper's inner response: Psalm 4 calls for righteous sacrifice after its instruction about inward remorse. Psalm 50 joins the broken and humbled heart with anticipated delight in a sacrifice of righteousness. The comparison concerns rightly offered sacrifice in a setting of inward correction.

Psalms 4:7
Many say, “Who will show us good things?” The light of Thy face was marked upon us, Lord.
  Numbers 6:25-26 — The favorable light of the Lord's face: The psalm answers the demand for good things with the light of the Lord's face marked upon the people. The priestly blessing asks that His face shine upon Israel with mercy and be lifted toward them in peace. Both express divine favor through the face's light.

Psalms 4:8
Thou didst put gladness in my heart; from the season of their grain and wine and oil, they were increased.
  Deuteronomy 7:13 — Grain, wine and oil accompanying increase: The psalm places God-given gladness alongside the statement that from the season of their grain, wine and oil, they were increased. Deuteronomy promises to multiply the people and bless their grain, wine and oil. The same agricultural triad accompanies increase, without resolving the psalm's pronoun or importing a greater-than-harvest comparison.

Psalms 4:9
In peace I will both lie down and sleep, for Thou, Lord, madest me dwell alone in hope.
  Psalms 3:6 — Peaceful sleep grounded in divine help: The speaker will lie down and sleep in peace because of the Lord's hopeful provision. Psalm 3 recounts lying down, sleeping and awakening because the Lord helps. Both connect ordinary vulnerable sleep with trust in God's care.

Psalms 5:4
In the morning Thou wilt hear my voice; in the morning I will stand before Thee and watch.
  Psalms 87:14 — Morning prayer reaching the Lord: Psalm 5 expects the Lord to hear in the morning while the speaker stands and watches. Psalm 87 likewise says that the morning prayer will reach the Lord first. The parallel concerns the deliberate presentation of prayer at morning.

Psalms 5:5
For Thou art not a God who desires lawlessness, nor will an evildoer sojourn with Thee.
  Psalms 14:1-3 — Moral conditions of dwelling with God: Psalm 5 denies that an evildoer can sojourn with God. Psalm 14 asks who may sojourn in God's dwelling and answers with blameless conduct, righteousness, truthful speech and refusal of harm. The two formulations give negative and positive accounts of access to the holy dwelling.

Psalms 5:6
The lawless will not remain before Thine eyes; Thou didst hate all who work lawlessness.
  Psalms 100:3-7 — Lawlessness excluded from sight and dwelling: Psalm 5 says the lawless cannot remain before God's eyes. In Psalm 100 the Davidic speaker refuses lawlessness before his eyes and says an unjust speaker did not prosper before them. The comparison joins moral exclusion and the eyes image while distinguishing God from the human householder who addresses Him.

Psalms 5:7
Thou wilt destroy all who speak falsehood; the Lord abhors a man of bloodshed and deceit.
  Proverbs 6:16-19 — Falsehood and bloodshed among what the Lord hates: Psalm 5 joins destruction of false speakers with the Lord's abhorrence of bloodshed and deceit. Proverbs' description of what the Lord hates includes an unjust tongue, hands shedding righteous blood and a witness kindling falsehoods. The paired speech and bloodshed offenses give the connection its particular content.

Psalms 5:8
But I, through the multitude of Thy mercy, will enter Thy house; I will bow toward Thy holy temple in fear of Thee.
  Psalms 137:2 — Bowing toward the holy temple in response to mercy: Psalm 5 anticipates entering God's house through abundant mercy and bowing toward His holy temple. Psalm 137 likewise bows toward the holy temple and thanks God's name for mercy and truth. Worship directed toward the temple is expressly joined to mercy in both.

Psalms 5:9
Lord, guide me in Thy righteousness because of my enemies; direct Thy way straight before me.
  Psalms 26:11 — Guidance on the upright way because of enemies: Psalm 5 asks for guidance in righteousness and a straight divine way because of enemies. Psalm 26 asks for instruction in God's way and guidance on an upright path for the same stated reason. Both requests link moral direction to hostile pressure.

Psalms 5:10
For there is no truth in their mouth; their heart is vain. Their throat is an open tomb; with their tongues they practiced deceit.
  Romans 3:13 — Quotation of the open tomb and deceitful tongues: Romans repeats the throat as an opened tomb and the tongues practicing deceit in its collection of scriptural accusations of sin. The psalm supplies these particular images of corrupt speech; Romans applies the collected testimony to its wider argument about Jews and Greeks under sin.

Psalms 5:11
Judge them, God! Let them fall away from their counsels. According to the multitude of their ungodly deeds, drive them out, for they provoked Thee bitterly, Lord.
  2 Samuel 15:31 — Prayer that hostile counsel fail: Psalm 5 asks that opponents fall away from their counsels. David's response to Ahithophel's joining Absalom is a prayer that the Lord scatter his counsel. The narrative illustrates a particular prayer against hostile planning without fixing Psalm 5 to that event.

Psalms 5:12
Let all who hope in Thee rejoice; they will exult for ever, and Thou wilt dwell among them. All who love Thy name will boast in Thee.
  Psalms 32:21-22 — Hope in the divine name expressed as rejoicing: Psalm 5 joins the rejoicing of those hoping in God with the boasting of those loving His name. Psalm 32 likewise joins the heart's rejoicing in Him with hope in His holy name and a prayer for mercy. The combined name, hope and joy language connects the worshippers' response.

Psalms 6:1
For the end, among hymns, concerning the eighth. A psalm of David.
  Psalms 11:1 — Shared heading concerning the eighth: Both headings identify a Davidic psalm for the end and specify concerning the eighth; Psalm 6 additionally mentions hymns. The connection records the distinctive repeated heading without deciding whether the eighth denotes an instrument, musical arrangement or another referent.

Psalms 6:2
Lord, do not rebuke me in Thy fury, nor discipline me in Thine anger.
  Psalms 37:2 — Repeated plea against rebuke in divine anger: Psalm 37 repeats the complete plea not to be rebuked in God's fury or disciplined in His anger. The identical paired petitions connect the laments without making their surrounding illness descriptions a single historical incident.

Psalms 6:3
Have mercy on me, Lord, for I am weak; heal me, Lord, for my bones were troubled,
  Psalms 37:4 — Troubled bones and the need for healing: Psalm 6 asks for mercy and healing because the speaker is weak and the bones are troubled. Psalm 37 speaks of no healing in the flesh and no peace for the bones, there explicitly relating the condition to anger and sins. The physical language matches while the fuller explanations remain distinct.

Psalms 6:4
and my soul was greatly troubled. And Thou, Lord—how long?
  John 12:27-28 — The troubled soul addressed to God: The psalm's greatly troubled soul turns to the Lord with a question of delay. Jesus likewise speaks of His troubled soul and addresses the Father concerning His hour and the Father's name. This compares the voiced anguish and prayer, without identifying the psalmist's occasion with Jesus' hour.

Psalms 6:6
For in death there is no one who remembers Thee; and in Hades, who will give Thee thanks?
  Isaiah 38:18-19 — Death and Hades contrasted with living praise: Psalm 6 declares that in death no one remembers God and asks who will thank Him in Hades. Hezekiah's prayer says those in Hades do not praise and contrasts them with the living who bless God. Both use that contrast within a plea or thanksgiving concerning endangered life.

Psalms 6:7
I grew weary with my groaning; every night I will bathe my bed, with my tears I will wet my resting place.
  Psalms 41:4 — Tears occupying the sufferer's nights: Psalm 6 pictures the bed and resting place soaked by tears every night. Psalm 41 makes tears the speaker's bread day and night amid questioning about God. Both render prolonged grief through bodily images of tears filling ordinary daily life.

Psalms 6:8
My eye was troubled from anger; I grew old among all my enemies.
  Psalms 30:10-12 — Eye troubled with anger amid enemies: Psalm 6 describes an eye troubled from anger and aging among enemies. Psalm 30 likewise describes the eye troubled with anger, then failing years, groaning and reproach among enemies. The unusual eye-and-anger expression is accompanied by closely related affliction language.

Psalms 6:9
Stand away from me, all who work lawlessness, for the Lord heard the voice of my weeping.
  Matthew 7:23 — Dismissal of workers of lawlessness: The psalm tells all workers of lawlessness to stand away because the Lord has heard the weeping. Jesus' judgment saying likewise dismisses those practicing lawlessness. The shared dismissal is preserved while its grounds differ: answered lament here and Jesus' refusal to recognize the claimants there.

Psalms 7:3
lest he seize my soul like a lion, with no one to redeem or save.
  Psalms 16:12-13 — A lion-like enemy and the appeal for rescue: Psalm 7 fears seizure of the soul like a lion when no rescuer is present. Psalm 16 describes enemies taking the speaker up like a hunting lion and immediately asks the Lord to arise and rescue the soul. The predator image and rescue request form the particular parallel.

Psalms 7:4
Lord my God, if I did this, if there is injustice in my hands,
  Job 31:5-8 — Conditional innocence oath before divine judgment: Psalm 7 posits possible wrongdoing in a conditional oath that leads to a self-directed curse in its following verses. Job likewise sets out possible misconduct, appeals to God's righteous weighing and accepts loss if guilty. The comparison is the conditional oath form, not an identification of the offenses or speakers.

Psalms 7:5
if I repaid those who repay me evils, then may I fall away from my enemies empty,
  1 Samuel 24:18-20 — Repayment to an enemy: conditional oath and narrative: The psalm puts repaying those who repay the speaker evils under its conditional self-curse. The Saul narrative explicitly distinguishes David's repayment with good things from Saul's evil repayment. The comparison concerns reciprocal response without adding an object to the psalm's first repaid.

Psalms 7:7
Rise, Lord, in Thine anger; be exalted within the boundaries of my enemies. Awake, Lord my God, in the ordinance which Thou didst command,
  Numbers 10:34 — Arise invocation against enemies: Psalm 7 calls on the Lord to rise in anger within the enemies' boundaries. Moses' invocation when the ark sets out likewise calls on the Lord to arise and scatter His enemies. Both invoke divine action against opposition, in distinct prayer and wilderness settings.

Psalms 7:9
The Lord will judge peoples. Judge me, Lord, according to my righteousness and according to my innocence upon me.
  Psalms 25:1-2 — Request for judgment according to innocence: Psalm 7 asks the Lord to judge according to the speaker's righteousness and innocence. Psalm 25 likewise requests judgment on the ground of an innocent walk and invites testing of kidneys and heart. The comparison concerns a personal appeal for examination and vindication.

Psalms 7:10
Let sinners’ wickedness come to an end, and Thou wilt direct the righteous one, God who tests hearts and kidneys.
  Jeremiah 11:20 — Righteous judgment testing hearts and kidneys: Psalm 7 names God as the tester of hearts and kidneys while asking an end to wickedness and direction for the righteous. Jeremiah likewise addresses the just judge who tests kidneys and hearts while entrusting his claim against plotters to Him. Both link inward testing with just adjudication.

Psalms 7:12
God is a righteous judge, strong and patient, not bringing wrath every day.
  Exodus 34:6-7 — Divine patience alongside justice: Psalm 7 calls God a righteous, strong and patient judge who does not bring wrath every day. The proclamation in Exodus calls Him slow to anger and merciful while also preserving righteousness and not cleansing the guilty. Patience is joined to, rather than substituted for, judgment.

Psalms 7:13
If you do not return, He will polish His sword; He bent His bow and prepared it,
  Deuteronomy 32:41 — Divine sword prepared for judgment: Psalm 7 warns of God's polished sword if the addressed person does not return. The song in Deuteronomy describes God sharpening His sword as lightning and taking hold of judgment to repay enemies. Both picture threatened judgment as divine preparation of a blade.

Psalms 7:14
and in it He prepared instruments of death; He fashioned His arrows for those being burned.
  Deuteronomy 32:42 — Divine arrows as instruments of deadly judgment: Psalm 7 describes prepared instruments of death and arrows fashioned for those being burned. Deuteronomy makes God's arrows drunk with blood in vengeance upon enemies. The connection is the deadly divine archery image, without changing Psalm 7 into a statement that its arrows themselves burn.

Psalms 7:15
Behold, he was in labor with injustice; he conceived trouble and gave birth to lawlessness.
  Job 15:35 — Wrongdoing pictured as conception and birth: The psalm portrays labor with injustice, conception of trouble and birth of lawlessness. Eliphaz pictures conception of pains and a belly bearing deceit. Both give wrongdoing a gestational image; this comparison does not endorse Eliphaz's application of his speech to Job.

Psalms 7:16
He dug a pit and hollowed it out, and he will fall into the hole he made.
  Proverbs 26:27 — The digger falling into his own pit: Psalm 7 says the one who excavates a pit will fall into the hole he made. Proverbs likewise says the person digging a pit for a neighbor falls into it. The trap reverses upon its maker in both formulations.

Psalms 7:17
His trouble will return upon his head, and his injustice will descend upon his crown.
  Esther 9:24-25 — Attempted harm returning upon its author: The psalm says trouble returns upon the wrongdoer's head and injustice descends upon his crown. Esther's festival explanation says the evils Haman attempted against the Jews came upon him. The narrative illustrates reversal of intended harm without claiming that it uses the psalm's head imagery.

Psalms 8:1
For the end, concerning the winepresses. A psalm of David.
  Psalms 80:1 — Shared heading concerning the winepresses: Both psalms are marked for the end and concerning the winepresses, while one is Davidic and the other is for Asaph. The distinctive heading is shared without equating authors or settling what winepresses denotes in these headings.

Psalms 8:2
Lord our Lord, how wondrous is Thy name in all the earth, for Thy magnificence was raised above the heavens.
  Psalms 112:3-4 — Worldwide praise and glory above the heavens: Psalm 8 joins the Lord's wondrous name throughout the earth with magnificence above the heavens. Psalm 112 calls for praise of His name across the sun's course and declares Him above nations with glory above the heavens. Both move from worldwide praise to heavenly exaltation.

Psalms 8:3
From the mouth of infants and nursing children Thou didst prepare praise, because of Thine enemies, to bring down the enemy and the avenger.
  Matthew 21:15-16 — Explicit quotation defending children's praise: Jesus answers objections to children praising Him in the temple by quoting the preparation of praise from infants and nursing children. Matthew therefore provides an explicit use of this psalmic statement in defense of the children's acclamation.

Psalms 8:4
For I will see the heavens, works of Thy fingers, the moon and stars which Thou didst found:
  Genesis 1:14-18 — The heavenly lights as God's creative work: The psalm looks at the heavens, moon and stars as works of God's fingers that He founded. Genesis recounts God's making and placing of the heavenly lights and stars. The connection concerns the same created celestial objects, with poetic finger imagery specific to the psalm.

Psalms 8:5
what is a human being, that Thou rememberest him, or a son of man, that Thou visitest him?
  Hebrews 2:6 — Quotation of the question about humanity: Hebrews quotes the question asking what a human being or son of man is that God remembers and cares for him. The citation introduces the target's discussion of subjection and of Jesus in the following verses; that later interpretation is not supplied merely by the phrase son of man alone.
  Job 7:17-18 — Human smallness and divine attention in contrasting prayers: Psalm 8 asks why God remembers and visits a human being in a context of bestowed honor. Job similarly asks what a human being is that God magnifies and visits him, but his surrounding plea seeks relief from scrutiny. The shared question serves contrasting responses to divine attention.

Psalms 8:6
Thou madest him a little lower than angels; Thou didst crown him with glory and honor,
  Hebrews 2:7-9 — Quotation of lower-than-angels status and crowning: Hebrews quotes being made a little lower than angels and crowned with glory and honor, then applies that language to Jesus and His suffering of death. The actual angels wording is retained in both passages, while the target supplies its christological use.

Psalms 8:7
and didst appoint him over the works of Thy hands; Thou didst subject everything beneath his feet:
  Hebrews 2:8 — Quotation and exposition of subjection beneath the feet: Hebrews quotes all things subjected beneath the feet and explains the comprehensiveness of that subjection while acknowledging it is not yet wholly seen. The psalm's grant over God's handiwork is therefore directly reused and interpreted in the target.
  1 Corinthians 15:27-28 — All-things subjection in the resurrection argument: Paul invokes the subjection of all things beneath His feet, explicitly excepts the One who subjects them and describes the Son's eventual subjection. The psalmic wording is used within a particular account of Christ's reign and its consummation.

Psalms 8:8
all sheep and cattle, and also the animals of the plain,
  Genesis 1:26 — Human rule including cattle and land creatures: The psalm lists sheep, cattle and animals of the plain under the human figure's granted rule. Genesis' creation declaration gives humankind rule including cattle and the earth's creatures. The shared content is the grant over animal life, not a claim that the two lists have identical wording.

Psalms 8:9
the birds of heaven and the fish of the sea, those that travel along the paths of seas.
  Genesis 1:28 — Human rule over sea fish and heavenly birds: Psalm 8 includes birds of heaven and fish of the sea among what is subjected to humanity. Genesis' blessing explicitly commands rule over fish of the sea and birds of heaven. The two named classes correspond directly.

Psalms 8:10
Lord our Lord, how wondrous is Thy name in all the earth!
  Psalms 8:2 — same-chapter opening and closing acclamation: The psalm ends by repeating the address Lord our Lord and the acclamation that His name is wondrous throughout the earth from its opening praise. This repeated frame encloses the reflection on heavenly creation and the place granted to humankind.

Psalms 9:2
I will give thanks unto Thee, Lord, with my whole heart; I will recount all Thy wonders.
  Psalms 104:1-2 — Thanksgiving joined to recounting all God's wonders: Psalm 9 promises wholehearted thanks and recounting all God's wonders. Psalm 104 similarly joins thanksgiving with the command to recount all His wonders. The particular link is the combination of grateful worship and comprehensive rehearsal of divine deeds.

Psalms 9:4
When my enemy turns back, they will grow weak and perish before Thee,
  Psalms 55:10 — Enemies turning back in response to God: Psalm 9 describes the enemy turning back and opponents weakening before God. Psalm 55 expects enemies to turn backward on the day the speaker calls upon God. The shared reversal is specifically the retreat of enemies before divine help.

Psalms 9:6
Thou didst rebuke nations, and the ungodly one perished; Thou didst erase their name for ever and for the age of the age.
  Deuteronomy 9:14 — Judgment expressed as erasure of a people's name: Psalm 9 recalls rebuked nations and an erased name. In Deuteronomy the Lord threatens to destroy idolatrous Israel and blot out its name from under heaven. The erasure image corresponds, but the threatened Israel and the psalm's nations are distinct, and Moses' subsequent intercession matters to the narrative.

Psalms 9:8
And the Lord remains for ever; He prepared His throne for judgment,
  Daniel 7:9-10 — The divine throne prepared for judgment: The psalm says the enduring Lord has prepared His throne for judgment. Daniel sees thrones set in place, an Ancient of Days seated and a court assembled with opened books. Both join divine enthronement with the exercise of judgment, while Daniel supplies visionary details absent from the psalm.

Psalms 9:9
and He will judge the inhabited world in righteousness; He will judge peoples with uprightness.
  Acts 17:31 — Judging the inhabited world in righteousness: The psalm declares that God will judge the inhabited world in righteousness. Paul's speech uses the same formulation and specifies a fixed day and an appointed Man whose resurrection gives assurance. The common judgment language is linked without importing those added specifications into the psalm.

Psalms 9:10
And the Lord became a refuge for the needy, a helper at fitting times in distress.
  Psalms 45:2 — God as refuge and helper in affliction: Psalm 9 describes the Lord as refuge for the needy and timely helper in distress. Psalm 45 likewise calls God refuge, strength and helper in overwhelming afflictions. The combined refuge-and-helper formulation connects the descriptions of protective aid.

Psalms 9:12
Sing praise to the Lord, who dwells in Zion; announce His deeds among the nations,
  Psalms 104:1 — Announcing the Lord's deeds among nations: Psalm 9 calls for praise to the Lord dwelling in Zion and proclamation of His deeds among nations. Psalm 104 likewise calls for thanksgiving and announcing His works among nations. The parallel concerns public proclamation of divine acts beyond the worshipping community.

Psalms 9:13
for He who calls bloodshed to account remembered them; He did not forget the cry of the needy.
  Genesis 4:10-11 — God hearing bloodshed and calling it to account: Psalm 9 says the One who calls bloodshed to account remembers the needy cry. God tells Cain that Abel's blood cries from the ground and pronounces a curse in response. The narrative supplies a concrete instance of shed blood being heard and answered by divine judgment.

Psalms 9:14
Have mercy on me, Lord; see my humiliation from my enemies, Thou who raisest me from the gates of death,
  Isaiah 38:10 — Endangered life at the gates of death: The psalm addresses God as the One raising the speaker from death's gates. Hezekiah describes his threatened remaining years at Hades' gates before recounting restoration. Both use a gateway to death's realm for mortal danger; their surrounding prayers move toward life and praise.

Psalms 9:15
so that I may proclaim all Thy praises in the gates of daughter Zion; I will exult in Thy salvation.
  Isaiah 38:19-20 — Preserved life and praise at God's dwelling: The psalm asks for mercy from the Lord who raises from death's gates so the speaker may praise at daughter Zion's gates. Hezekiah contrasts living praise with Hades and promises continual blessing before God's house. Both connect life preserved from death with worship at the holy place.

Psalms 9:16
Nations became fixed in the destruction they brought about; in this trap they concealed, their foot was caught.
  Psalms 34:7-8 — A concealed trap catching those who laid it: Psalm 9 says nations become caught by the destruction and trap they prepared. Psalm 34 asks that the concealed snare catch its makers and that they fall into it. The connection is the precise reversal of a hidden trap upon its authors.

Psalms 9:17
The Lord is known as He executes judgments; the sinner was caught in the works of his own hands. A song of Diapsalma.
  Esther 7:9-10 — The sinner caught by his own prepared instrument: Psalm 9 presents the sinner caught in the works of his own hands as a manifestation of judgment. Haman is hung on the tree he prepared for Mordecai. This is a concrete narrative analogy of a deadly preparation returning upon its maker, not an identification of the psalm's sinner.

Psalms 9:18
Let sinners turn back to Hades, all nations that forget God.
  Job 8:13 — Destruction associated with forgetting God: The psalm consigns sinners and nations forgetting God to Hades. Bildad describes the end of those who forget the Lord and the perishing of the ungodly person's hope. Both connect forgetting God with a ruinous end, without treating Bildad's speech as a verdict on Job or making its imagery identical to Hades.

Psalms 9:19
For the poor will not be forgotten utterly; the endurance of the needy will not perish for ever.
  Psalms 9:32 — same-chapter remembrance contrasted with the oppressor's claim: The declaration that the poor will not be utterly forgotten directly counters the later oppressor's assertion that God has forgotten and turned away His face. The contrast distinguishes the psalm's assurance from the wicked person's quoted claim.

Psalms 9:20
Arise, Lord; do not let a human being grow strong; let nations be judged before Thee.
  2 Chronicles 14:10 — Prayer that human strength not prevail before God: Psalm 9 asks the Lord to arise so that a human being does not grow strong and nations face judgment. Asa similarly asks that no human being prevail against God while confronting a great force. Both subordinate threatening human strength to divine intervention, in different settings.

Psalms 9:22
Why, Lord, hast Thou stood far away, overlooking us at fitting times in distress?
  Psalms 9:10 — same-chapter timely help questioned in the lament: The question why the Lord stands far away at fitting times in distress recalls the earlier description of Him as helper at fitting times in distress. The repeated phrasing creates a deliberate tension between confessed refuge and experienced absence within this one psalm.

Psalms 9:24
For the sinner is praised for the desires of his soul, and the one who acts unjustly is blessed.
  Proverbs 28:4 — Moral inversion in praising wrongdoing: Psalm 9 describes the sinner praised for desire and the unjust person blessed. Proverbs describes those abandoning the law as praising impiety. Both identify praise directed toward wrongdoing as a distortion of moral judgment.

Psalms 9:25
The sinner provoked the Lord. According to the abundance of his anger, he will not seek out; God is not before him.
  Psalms 35:2-5 — The sinner acting without God before his view: Psalm 9 says God is not before the sinner. Psalm 35 describes the lawless person's inward resolve to sin with no fear of God before his eyes, followed by deceit and planned lawlessness. The parallel concerns practical disregard of God, without replacing either text with an explicit denial that God exists.

Psalms 9:27
For he said in his heart, “I will certainly not be shaken, from generation to generation, without adversity.”
  Psalms 29:7-8 — The unshaken boast exposed by a contrasting experience: The sinner claims he will never be shaken or encounter adversity. Psalm 29 recalls the speaker's own prosperous claim never to be shaken, then God's turned face and resulting trouble. The repeated boast is compared while distinguishing the wicked speaker from the worshipper recalling failed confidence.

Psalms 9:28
His mouth is full of cursing, bitterness, and deceit; under his tongue are toil and trouble.
  Romans 3:14 — Quotation of the mouth's cursing and bitterness: Romans repeats the mouth full of cursing and bitterness in its scriptural indictment of sin. Psalm 9 supplies those features within the fuller description that also names deceit and trouble under the tongue; Romans' shorter citation does not reproduce every detail.

Psalms 9:29
He sits in ambush with rich people, to kill an innocent person in hiding places; his eyes look toward the needy.
  Proverbs 1:11-13 — Concealed plotting against an innocent person: Psalm 9 depicts ambush with rich people and a plan to kill an innocent person in hiding. Proverbs voices a criminal invitation to shared bloodshed, unjust concealment of a righteous man and seizure of property. Both expose collective hidden violence, without attributing every proposed motive to the psalm's ambushers.

Psalms 9:30
He lurks in hiding like a lion in its enclosure; he lurks to seize a poor man, to seize a poor man as he drags him away.
  Proverbs 28:15 — The predator image for oppression of the needy: The psalm compares the lurking captor of a poor man with a lion in its enclosure. Proverbs compares one tyrannizing over a needy nation with a hungry lion and thirsty wolf. The lion image makes oppression of the vulnerable predatory in both texts.

Psalms 9:32
For he said in his heart, “God has forgotten; He turned His face away so as never to see.”
  Psalms 93:6-9 — The oppressor's claim that God does not see: Psalm 9 quotes the assertion that God has forgotten and turned away His face forever. Psalm 93 places the claim that the Lord will not see after murder of vulnerable people, then rebuts it through the Creator of the eye. Both expose an oppressive confidence in divine inattention.

Psalms 9:34
For what reason did the ungodly provoke God? For he said in his heart, “He will not call to account.”
  Psalms 9:13 — same-chapter denial of accountability contradicted by divine remembrance: The ungodly person's claim that God will not call to account conflicts with the earlier statement that He calls bloodshed to account and remembers the needy. The intrapsalm comparison separates quoted denial from the psalm's own assertion of justice.

Psalms 9:35
Thou seest, for Thou perceivest trouble and anger, to hand them over into Thy hands. To Thee, therefore, the poor man has been left; Thou wast helping the orphan.
  Deuteronomy 10:18 — God's care and judgment for the orphan: Psalm 9 answers the claim of divine inattention by affirming God's perception and help for the orphan. Deuteronomy describes God as executing judgment for the sojourner, orphan and widow. Both place protection of the orphan within God's active justice.

Psalms 9:36
Crush the arm of the sinful and evil man; his sin will be sought out, and he will certainly not be found because of it.
  Psalms 36:17 — The breaking of the sinner's arm: Psalm 9 asks for the sinful and evil man's arm to be crushed. Psalm 36 says sinners' arms will be shattered while the Lord sustains the righteous. Both use the disabled arm to portray the collapse of wicked power.

Psalms 9:37
The Lord will reign for ever and for the age of the age; you will perish, nations, from His land.
  Exodus 15:17-18 — Everlasting divine reign and the land of inheritance: Psalm 9 joins the Lord's endless reign with the nations' removal from His land. The sea song moves from the request to plant the people in God's mountain of inheritance to the declaration of His everlasting reign. Both connect divine kingship with the place belonging to Him, without equating their historical settings.

Psalms 9:39
to judge for the orphan and the lowly, so that a human being on the earth may no longer boast greatly.
  Deuteronomy 24:17-18 — Justice for the orphan against oppression: Psalm 9 describes judgment for orphan and lowly so that human boasting ceases. Deuteronomy prohibits perverting judgment for the orphan and other vulnerable people and recalls Israel's redeemed slavery. The command gives a concrete human obligation corresponding to the psalm's appeal for protective justice.

Psalms 10:2
For behold, sinners bent a bow; they prepared arrows for a quiver, to shoot down in the moonless dark the upright in heart.
  Psalms 63:4-5 — Hidden archery against an upright victim: Psalm 10 depicts sinners preparing bow and arrows to strike the upright in moonless darkness. Psalm 63 likewise depicts a stretched bow and shooting a blameless person in hiding, adjoining sharpened tongues. Both portray concealed attack against uprightness, without deciding that every weapon in Psalm 10 is figurative speech.

Psalms 10:4
The Lord is in His holy temple; the Lord’s throne is in heaven. His eyes look toward the needy; His eyelids examine the sons of men.
  Psalms 32:13-15 — Divine observation of humanity from the heavenly dwelling: Psalm 10 places the Lord's throne in heaven and describes His eyes examining human beings. Psalm 32 likewise describes Him looking from heaven and His prepared dwelling upon all humanity and understanding their deeds. Heavenly dwelling is joined to intimate human scrutiny in both.

Psalms 10:5
The Lord examines the righteous and the ungodly; but the one who loves injustice hates his own soul.
  Proverbs 29:24 — Participation in wrongdoing as hostility to one's own life: Psalm 10 says the lover of injustice hates his own soul. Proverbs says the person sharing with a thief hates his own life. Both describe allegiance to wrongdoing as self-directed harm, with theft the target's specific case.

Psalms 10:6
He will rain traps upon sinners; fire, sulfur, and a storm wind are the portion of their cup.
  Genesis 19:24-25 — Fire and sulfur as divine judgment: Psalm 10 first describes God raining traps upon sinners, then names fire, sulfur and a storm wind as their cup's portion. Genesis narrates the Lord raining sulfur and fire upon Sodom and Gomorrah. Fire and sulfur belong to divine judgment in both, while their exact grammatical relation to the rain differs and the psalm does not name the cities.

Psalms 11:2
Save me, Lord, for the holy one is gone; for truths have become few among the sons of men.
  Micah 7:2 — Loss of faithful people: The psalm laments the disappearance of the holy one and the scarcity of truth among people. Micah likewise laments the loss of the devout person and the absence of upright conduct; both describe a social crisis of faithfulness.

Psalms 11:3
Each spoke empty things to his neighbor; deceitful lips spoke with a heart and a heart.
  Jeremiah 9:7 — Deceit toward a neighbor: The psalm joins empty speech to a neighbor with deceitful lips and a heart-and-heart formulation. Jeremiah makes the outward and inward discrepancy explicit: peaceful words to a neighbor conceal enmity within.

Psalms 11:4
May the Lord destroy all deceitful lips and a boastful tongue—
  Psalms 30:19 — Judgment upon deceitful and arrogant lips: Both prayers seek an end to deceitful lips associated with arrogant speech. Psalm 11 asks for their destruction, while Psalm 30 asks that they become speechless and specifies their attacks on the righteous.

Psalms 11:5
those who said, “We will magnify our tongue; our lips are our own; who is our lord?”
  James 3:5-8 — The boasting and unrestrained tongue: The speakers magnify their tongue and claim that their lips have no lord. James describes the tongue boasting great things and being untamable by humans; the texts expose dangerous claims and effects of speech rather than endorsing its independence.

Psalms 11:6
“Because of the misery of the poor and because of the groaning of the needy, now I will arise,” says the Lord. “I will set him in salvation; I will speak openly in him.”
  Exodus 3:7-8 — Divine action in response to oppression and cries: The Lord announces that He will arise because of poor people's misery and needy people's groaning. In Exodus He explicitly connects seeing Israel's affliction and hearing its cry with coming down to rescue it; the shared response pattern occurs in different settings.

Psalms 11:7
The sayings of the Lord are pure sayings, silver tried by fire, tested in the earth, purified sevenfold.
  Proverbs 30:5 — Divine words refined by fire: The Lord's sayings are compared with silver tested by fire and purified sevenfold. Proverbs likewise calls all God's words refined by fire, giving a particular counterpart to the psalm's refining image.

Psalms 12:2
How long, Lord, wilt Thou forget me utterly? How long wilt Thou turn Thy face away from me?
  Psalms 43:25 — The hidden face and apparent forgetting: Both laments combine God's turning His face away with forgetting the afflicted speaker or community. Psalm 12 asks how long this will continue, while Psalm 43 asks why it occurs.

Psalms 12:4
Look; hear me, Lord my God. Give light to my eyes, lest I fall asleep into death,
  John 11:11-14 — Death described as sleep: The prayer asks for light to the eyes lest the speaker fall asleep into death. Jesus calls Lazarus asleep, and the narrative explicitly explains that He means Lazarus's death; the shared image is death as sleep, in distinct situations.

Psalms 12:5
lest my enemy say, “I prevailed against him.” Those who afflict me will exult if I am shaken.
  Psalms 34:24-26 — Preventing an enemy's triumphant boast: The speaker fears an enemy saying that he prevailed and afflicters rejoicing at a fall. Psalm 34 similarly asks that enemies not rejoice or claim to have swallowed the speaker, making hostile boasting the specific shared concern.

Psalms 12:6
But I have hoped in Thy mercy; my heart will exult in Thy salvation. I will sing to the Lord, who has benefited me, and I will sing praise to the name of the Lord Most High.
  Psalms 9:3 — Exultation and praise to the Most High's name: The closing promise to exult and sing praise to the Lord Most High's name corresponds to Psalm 9's explicit sequence of rejoicing, exulting and praising the Most High's name. Psalm 12 additionally grounds its response in mercy and salvation.

Psalms 13:1
For the end. A psalm of David. A fool said in his heart, “There is no God.” They acted corruptly and became abominable in their practices; there is no one who shows kindness; there is not even one.
  Psalms 52:2 — Parallel fool and corruption stanza: Both passages place the denial of God in a fool's heart and follow it with corruption, abominable conduct and the absence of kindness or good action. Psalm 13 includes the title in its actual source unit; Psalm 52 gives the parallel statement after its separate title.

Psalms 13:2
The Lord looked down from heaven upon the sons of men, to see whether there is anyone who understands or seeks God.
  Psalms 52:3 — God looks from heaven for understanding and seeking: Both passages picture God looking or bending down from heaven toward humanity to see whether anyone understands or seeks Him. This is a close correspondence of the action, viewpoint and two objects of inquiry.

Psalms 13:3
All turned aside; together they became useless; there is no one who shows kindness, not even one. Their throat is an open tomb; with their tongues they practiced deceit. The venom of asps is under their lips; their mouth is full of cursing and bitterness. Their feet are swift to shed blood; brokenness and misery are in their ways, and they did not know a way of peace. There is no fear of God before their eyes.
  Psalms 52:4 — Parallel opening indictment: The opening of this expanded source unit closely matches Psalm 52's turning aside, becoming useless and absence of anyone doing good. Psalm 13 continues with a much longer catalogue that is absent from the short target unit.
  Romans 3:12-18 — Extended shared indictment text: The complete source unit's sequence of turning aside, the open tomb, asp venom, cursing, bloodshed, misery in one's ways, ignorance of peace and absence of fear of God corresponds to the catalogue in Romans. Romans places it within an indictment of Jews and Greeks under sin; this comparison does not decide a direction of textual dependence.

Psalms 13:4
Will all those who practice lawlessness not know? Those who devour my people as a meal of bread did not call upon the Lord.
  Psalms 52:5 — People devoured like bread: Both passages portray lawless people consuming God's people as a meal of bread and failing to call upon God. The shared devouring simile and neglected invocation make this a particular parallel.

Psalms 13:5
There they cowered in fear where there was no fear, for God is with a righteous generation.
  Psalms 52:6 — Fear where there was no fear: The distinctive fear-where-no-fear phrase occurs in both passages. Psalm 13 recounts their cowering and says God is with a righteous generation; Psalm 52 anticipates great fear and adds the scattering of people-pleasers' bones, so their explanations remain distinct.

Psalms 13:7
Who will give Israel’s salvation from Zion? When the Lord turns back His people’s captivity, let Jacob exult and let Israel rejoice.
  Psalms 52:7 — Salvation from Zion and returned captivity: Both endings ask who will give Israel salvation from Zion, then join the Lord's reversal of captivity with Jacob's exultation and Israel's joy. Psalm 13 exhorts them to rejoice, while Psalm 52 states that they will do so.

Psalms 14:1
A psalm of David. Lord, who will sojourn in Thy dwelling, and who will encamp on Thy holy mountain?
  Psalms 23:3 — Who may enter the holy mountain: Both psalms introduce qualifications for approaching the Lord with a question about His mountain and holy dwelling or place. The shared entrance question supplies the setting for their ensuing descriptions of conduct.

Psalms 14:2
One who walks blamelessly and works righteousness, speaking truth in his heart,
  Psalms 23:4 — Moral qualifications for the holy place: The answer concerning blameless conduct, righteousness and truth in the heart corresponds to the other entrance psalm's innocent hands and pure heart. Both make inward and outward integrity relevant to the preceding question about God's holy place.

Psalms 14:3
who did not act deceitfully with his tongue, nor do evil to his neighbor, and did not take up reproach against those nearest him.
  Leviticus 19:16 — Deceit and harm to one's neighbor: The psalm excludes deceitful speech and evil against a neighbor. Leviticus prohibits going deceitfully among the people and conspiring against a neighbor's blood, providing a specific legal counterpart to the neighboring-person ethics of the entrance psalm.

Psalms 14:4
One who acts wickedly is held in contempt before him, but he honors those who fear the Lord; one who swears to his neighbor and does not set it aside.
  Numbers 30:3 — Keeping one's sworn word: The psalm commends one who swears to his neighbor and does not set it aside. Numbers requires a person who swears an oath not to profane his word but to do what comes from his mouth; this comparison concerns the source unit's oath-keeping clause.

Psalms 14:5
He did not lend his silver at interest and did not take gifts against innocent people. The one who does these things will never be shaken.
  Exodus 22:24 — Refusal to charge interest on a loan: The worshiper's refusal to lend silver at interest corresponds to the law against placing interest upon a loan to a poor brother. The psalm gives a personal qualification, while Exodus specifies the protected borrower.
  Deuteronomy 27:25 — Gifts used against innocent people: The psalm rejects taking gifts against innocent people. Deuteronomy curses the person who takes a bribe to kill an innocent life, supplying a particular counterpart to the combination of payment and harm to the innocent.

Psalms 15:2
I said to the Lord, “Thou art my Lord, for Thou hast no need of my good things.”
  Acts 17:24-25 — God does not need human provision: The speaker addresses the Lord as having no need of his good things. Paul similarly says that the Creator is not served by human hands as though He needed anything, because He Himself gives life and everything.

Psalms 15:5
The Lord is the portion of my inheritance and my cup; Thou art the One who restores my inheritance to me.
  Numbers 18:20 — The Lord as portion and inheritance: The psalm calls the Lord the portion of the speaker's inheritance and cup, and addresses Him as the One who restores the inheritance. In Numbers the Lord explicitly tells Aaron that He is Aaron's portion and inheritance; the shared portion language does not assign Aaron's land restriction to the psalmist.

Psalms 15:6
Measuring cords fell for me among the best places; indeed, my inheritance is the best for me.
  Joshua 17:4-6 — Measured allotment and inheritance: The psalm describes measuring cords falling in excellent places for an inheritance. Joshua recounts an inheritance awarded to daughters and a measured allotment that fell to them; the concrete allotment language illuminates the psalm's image without identifying the properties.

Psalms 15:8
I kept seeing the Lord before me continually, because He is at my right hand, so that I may not be shaken.
  Acts 2:25 — Explicit quotation of the Lord at the right hand: Peter introduces David's words concerning Jesus and quotes the Lord continually before the speaker and at his right hand so that he is not shaken. The actual target identifies the application; the shared words are explicitly presented as David's saying.

Psalms 15:9
Therefore my heart rejoiced, and my tongue exulted; indeed, even my flesh will dwell in hope,
  Acts 2:26 — Explicit quotation of joy and the flesh's hope: Peter's quotation repeats the rejoicing heart, exulting tongue and flesh dwelling in hope. The surrounding speech applies the quoted passage to Jesus' resurrection.

Psalms 15:10
for Thou wilt not abandon my soul to Hades, nor let Thy holy one see corruption.
  Acts 2:27 — Explicit quotation of Hades and corruption: Peter's quotation repeats the refusal to abandon the soul to Hades or allow the Holy One to see decay. His subsequent explanation in Acts 2:29-32 distinguishes David's death from Jesus' resurrection.
  Acts 13:35-37 — Quotation that the Holy One will not see decay: Paul quotes the holy-one and decay clause and immediately distinguishes David, who saw decay, from the One God raised, who did not. This target explicitly states the resurrection application of the quoted words.

Psalms 15:11
Thou madest known to me ways of life; Thou wilt fill me with gladness in Thy presence; pleasures are at Thy right hand for ever.
  Acts 2:28 — Explicit quotation of life's paths and gladness: Peter's quotation repeats God's making known life's paths and filling the speaker with gladness in His presence. The psalm additionally speaks of pleasures at God's right hand for ever, which the quoted target does not repeat.

Psalms 16:1
A prayer of David. Hear my righteousness, Lord; attend to my petition; give ear to my prayer, not from deceitful lips.
  Psalms 65:18-20 — Prayer heard in relation to integrity: The speaker asks God to hear a prayer that does not come from deceitful lips. Psalm 65 joins a conditional appeal that the Lord not hear if injustice was regarded in the heart with the statement that God did hear the petition; both relate prayer and integrity.

Psalms 16:2
May my judgment come forth from Thy presence; let my eyes see upright things.
  Psalms 7:9-11 — Judgment from God and uprightness: The speaker seeks judgment from God's presence and upright things before his own eyes. Psalm 7 asks the Lord to judge according to the speaker's righteousness and names God as examiner of hearts and savior of the upright.

Psalms 16:3
Thou didst test my heart; Thou didst visit by night; Thou didst try me by fire, and no injustice was found in me,
  Psalms 25:2 — The heart tested by fire: The speaker reports God's testing his heart and trying him by fire. Psalm 25 asks the Lord to test the speaker and try his kidneys and heart by fire; the correspondence preserves the difference between a reported test and a requested one.

Psalms 16:5
Set my steps firmly in Thy paths, so that my steps may not be shaken.
  Psalms 118:133 — Steps directed by God: The prayer asks for steps to be firmly set in God's paths so that they are not shaken. Psalm 118 asks God to direct the speaker's steps according to His saying; both use directed steps for faithful conduct.

Psalms 16:7
Make Thy mercies wonderful, Thou who savest those hoping in Thee from those who oppose Thy right hand.
  Exodus 15:6 — Divine rescue and the opposing enemies of God's right hand: The psalm addresses God as rescuing those who hope in Him from opponents of His right hand. The sea song celebrates that same divine right-hand image as strong and shattering enemies, without identifying the opponents or rescues as one event.

Psalms 16:8
Guard me as the pupil of an eye; in the shelter of Thy wings Thou wilt shelter me,
  Deuteronomy 32:10 — Guarding as the pupil of an eye: The psalm asks to be guarded as the pupil of an eye. The song in Deuteronomy uses the same protective image for the Lord's keeping Jacob in the wilderness.
  Ruth 2:12 — Shelter under God's wings: The psalm asks to be guarded and says that God will shelter the speaker under His wings. Boaz blesses Ruth as one who has come to trust under the Lord's wings; the repeated shelter image is applied to distinct persons.

Psalms 16:12
They took me up like a lion ready for the hunt and like a young lion dwelling in hiding places.
  Psalms 9:30 — A concealed lion as the image of an attacker: The source places a lion ready for the hunt and a young lion in hiding places in its description of hostile action. Psalm 9 explicitly compares a lurking attacker with a lion in its enclosure seeking to seize a poor man; the connection concerns the concealed hunting-lion image.

Psalms 17:1
For the end. Of the Lord’s servant David, who spoke the words of this song to the Lord on the day the Lord rescued him from the hand of all his enemies and from the hand of Saul.
  2 Samuel 22:1 — Parallel forms of David’s rescue song: Both headings assign the song to David and locate it at the Lord's rescue from all his enemies and Saul. The psalm adds its liturgical heading and description of David as the Lord's servant.

Psalms 17:2
And he said: I will love Thee, Lord, my strength.
  Deuteronomy 6:5 — Confessed love and the command to love the Lord: David's song opens with his declaration that he will love the Lord. Deuteronomy commands love for the Lord with the whole heart, soul and strength; the comparison relates the expressed devotion to that explicit command, while the psalm calls the Lord himself its speaker's strength.

Psalms 17:3
The Lord is my firm foundation, my refuge, and my rescuer; my God is my helper, and I will hope in Him; my protector, the horn of my salvation, my supporter.
  2 Samuel 22:2-3 — Parallel forms of David’s rescue song: Both song openings accumulate refuge, rescue and protection titles for the Lord, including the distinctive horn of salvation. The psalm's single unit corresponds to two Samuel units and retains its own foundation and supporter wording.

Psalms 17:4
Praising, I will call upon the Lord, and I will be saved from my enemies.
  2 Samuel 22:4 — Parallel forms of David’s rescue song: Both lines join calling upon the praised Lord with being saved from enemies. The psalm says praising I will call, while Samuel describes the Lord as worthy of praise.

Psalms 17:5
The birth pangs of death encompassed me, and torrents of lawlessness threw me into turmoil.
  Acts 2:24 — The pangs of death and divine rescue: The song describes the birth pangs of death surrounding its speaker. Peter says God raised Jesus by releasing Him from the pangs of death; the shared death-pangs image occurs in David's rescue song and an explicit resurrection announcement.
  2 Samuel 22:5 — Parallel forms of David’s rescue song: Both lines combine surrounding death imagery with torrents of lawlessness. The psalm names birth pangs of death and turmoil; Samuel names crushings of death and being stunned.

Psalms 17:6
The birth pangs of Hades surrounded me; the traps of death overtook me.
  2 Samuel 22:6 — Parallel forms of David’s rescue song: The corresponding lines depict encirclement and threatening death. The psalm specifically names Hades' birth pangs and death's traps, whereas Samuel names death's birth pangs and harsh assaults.

Psalms 17:7
And when I was afflicted, I called upon the Lord and cried out to my God. He heard my voice from His holy temple, and my cry before Him will enter His ears.
  2 Samuel 22:7 — Parallel forms of David’s rescue song: Both lines connect distress, calling to God, His temple and the cry entering His ears. The psalm recounts a past call and hearing before anticipating the cry's entry; Samuel uses future forms for calling and hearing.

Psalms 17:8
And the earth was shaken and began to tremble; the foundations of the mountains were troubled and shaken because God was angry with them.
  2 Samuel 22:8 — Parallel forms of David’s rescue song: Both lines describe the earth shaking because God is angry, accompanied by disturbed foundations. The psalm names the mountains' foundations, while Samuel names heaven's foundations and their being torn apart.

Psalms 17:9
Smoke rose in His anger, and fire from His face set things ablaze; coals were kindled from Him.
  2 Samuel 22:9 — Parallel forms of David’s rescue song: Both lines join divine anger, rising smoke, fire and kindled coals. The psalm's fire comes from God's face and sets things ablaze; Samuel places it at His mouth and says it will devour.

Psalms 17:10
And He bent heaven and came down, and gloom was beneath His feet.
  2 Samuel 22:10 — Parallel forms of David’s rescue song: Both forms of the song depict God bending heaven, coming down and having darkness beneath His feet. The corresponding actions and position of the darkness establish the close parallel.

Psalms 17:11
And He mounted cherubim and flew; He flew on the wings of winds.
  2 Samuel 22:11 — Parallel forms of David’s rescue song: Both lines combine cherubim, flight and the wind's wings. The psalm says God flew on those wings, while Samuel says He appeared upon them after stating that He sat on cherubim and flew.

Psalms 17:12
And He made darkness His hiding place; His tent was around Him, dark water in clouds of the air.
  2 Samuel 22:12 — Parallel forms of David’s rescue song: Both lines make darkness God's surrounding hiding place and tent, with dark waters and clouds of air. The shared arrangement of tent, darkness, water and cloud is particular to this corresponding divine-appearance portrait.

Psalms 17:13
From the far-shining radiance before Him, the clouds passed— hail and coals of fire.
  2 Samuel 22:13 — Parallel forms of David’s rescue song: Both lines associate radiance before God with coals of fire. The psalm also names passing clouds and hail, while Samuel specifically says that coals were kindled from the brightness.

Psalms 17:14
And the Lord thundered from heaven, and the Most High gave His voice.
  2 Samuel 22:14 — Parallel forms of David’s rescue song: Both lines pair the Lord's thunder from heaven with the Most High's giving His voice. Their parallel phrasing explicitly links heavenly thunder and the divine voice.

Psalms 17:15
And He sent out arrows and scattered them; He multiplied lightning flashes and threw them into turmoil.
  2 Samuel 22:15 — Parallel forms of David’s rescue song: Both lines depict divine arrows scattering opponents and lightning throwing them into confusion. The psalm additionally speaks of multiplying lightning flashes.

Psalms 17:16
And the springs of the waters appeared, and the foundations of the inhabited world were uncovered at Thy rebuke, Lord, at the blast of the spirit of Thine anger.
  2 Samuel 22:16 — Parallel forms of David’s rescue song: Both lines uncover the inhabited world's foundations at the Lord's rebuke and the blast of His anger. The psalm names springs of waters and the spirit of that anger; Samuel names sea outlets and its breath.

Psalms 17:17
He sent from on high and took me; He took me to Himself out of many waters.
  2 Samuel 22:17 — Parallel forms of David’s rescue song: Both lines describe action from on high that takes the speaker out of many waters. The psalm says God sent and took him to Himself; Samuel says He reached and drew him out.

Psalms 17:18
He will rescue me from my powerful enemies and from those who hate me, for they grew stronger than I.
  2 Samuel 22:18 — Parallel forms of David’s rescue song: Both lines name powerful enemies and haters who had grown stronger than the speaker. The psalm anticipates rescue with will rescue, whereas Samuel reports that He rescued him.

Psalms 17:19
They overtook me in the day of my affliction, and the Lord became my support,
  2 Samuel 22:19 — Parallel forms of David’s rescue song: Both lines put an enemy encounter in the speaker's day of affliction or distress and state that the Lord became his support. The combination of timing and divine support supplies the close correspondence.

Psalms 17:20
and He brought me out into a spacious place. He will rescue me because He desired me. He will rescue me from my powerful enemies and from those who hate me.
  2 Samuel 22:20 — Parallel forms of David’s rescue song: Both lines recount being brought into a spacious place and connect rescue with God's desire or delight. The psalm anticipates rescue and adds powerful enemies and haters, whereas Samuel reports completed rescue in this unit.

Psalms 17:21
And the Lord will repay me according to my righteousness, and according to the purity of my hands He will repay me,
  2 Samuel 22:21 — Parallel forms of David’s rescue song: Both lines connect repayment with the speaker's righteousness and clean hands. The psalm uses will repay; Samuel reports repaid. These remain the song speaker's claims, not an independent judgment about every act in David's life.

Psalms 17:22
because I kept the Lord’s ways and did not act impiously by turning from my God,
  2 Samuel 22:22 — Parallel forms of David’s rescue song: Both lines explain the preceding claim by keeping the Lord's ways and not acting impiously through departure from God. Their matching statement concerns the speaker's declared conduct.

Psalms 17:23
because all His judgments are before me, and I did not put His ordinances away from me.
  2 Samuel 22:23 — Parallel forms of David’s rescue song: Both lines place God's judgments before the speaker and deny putting away or turning from His ordinances. The psalm says the judgments are before him; Samuel says they were.

Psalms 17:24
And I will be blameless with Him and guard myself from my lawlessness.
  2 Samuel 22:24 — Parallel forms of David’s rescue song: Both lines anticipate blamelessness toward God and guarding against the speaker's own lawlessness. The shared future orientation is preserved rather than converted into a claim of completed lifelong sinlessness.

Psalms 17:25
And the Lord will repay me according to my righteousness and according to the purity of my hands before His eyes.
  2 Samuel 22:25 — Parallel forms of David’s rescue song: Both lines anticipate the Lord's repayment according to righteousness and the purity or cleanness of the speaker's hands before His eyes. This corresponds to the later, future-form statement in each song.

Psalms 17:26
With a holy one Thou wilt show Thyself holy, and with an innocent man Thou wilt be innocent,
  2 Samuel 22:26 — Parallel forms of David’s rescue song: Both lines pair God's showing Himself holy toward the holy with a second correspondence toward an upright person. The psalm calls the second person innocent, while Samuel uses complete; those actual terms remain distinct.

Psalms 17:27
and with a chosen one Thou wilt be chosen, and with a twisted one Thou wilt twist.
  2 Samuel 22:27 — Parallel forms of David’s rescue song: Both lines pair chosen with chosen and twisted or crooked with a corresponding divine response. This particular two-part formulation supplies the parallel without substituting a different description of the persons.

Psalms 17:28
For Thou wilt save a lowly people and humble the eyes of the arrogant.
  2 Samuel 22:28 — Parallel forms of David’s rescue song: Both lines combine saving a lowly or poor people with lowering arrogant or haughty eyes. The same reversal of the lowly and proud appears in the corresponding place in the songs.

Psalms 17:29
For Thou wilt light my lamp, Lord; my God, Thou wilt light my darkness.
  2 Samuel 22:29 — Parallel forms of David’s rescue song: Both lines join the Lord with a lamp and the lighting of the speaker's darkness. The psalm says the Lord will light his lamp; Samuel directly calls the Lord his lamp before anticipating light in darkness.

Psalms 17:30
For by Thee I will be rescued from a raiding band, and by my God I will cross over a wall.
  2 Samuel 22:30 — Parallel forms of David’s rescue song: Both lines say that the speaker will pass over a wall by his God. The first clauses differ: the psalm anticipates rescue from a raiding band, while Samuel says the speaker will run lightly armed.

Psalms 17:31
My God’s way is blameless; the Lord’s sayings are tried by fire; He is a protector of all who hope in Him.
  2 Samuel 22:31 — Parallel forms of David’s rescue song: Both lines combine God's blameless way, His word tested or refined by fire, and protection for those who rely on Him. These three linked assertions correspond despite the different titles and wording for strength and protection.

Psalms 17:32
For who is God except the Lord? And who is God except our God?
  2 Samuel 22:32 — Parallel forms of David’s rescue song: Both lines use two questions to exclude rivals to the Lord and our God. The psalm repeats God, while Samuel asks about the mighty and a Creator; their actual predicates are not made identical.

Psalms 17:33
God girds me with power and made my way blameless,
  2 Samuel 22:33 — Parallel forms of David’s rescue song: Both lines attribute the speaker's power and blameless way to God. The psalm uses girding and making the way blameless, while Samuel uses strengthening and shaking the way free.

Psalms 17:34
He sets my feet firmly like a deer’s and makes me stand upon the heights,
  2 Samuel 22:34 — Parallel forms of David’s rescue song: Both lines compare the speaker's feet with a deer's and place him upon heights. The paired foot-and-height image forms a close correspondence within the rescue songs.

Psalms 17:35
training my hands for war; and Thou madest my arms a bronze bow.
  2 Samuel 22:35 — Parallel forms of David’s rescue song: Both lines describe God training hands for war and then mention an arm and bronze bow. The psalm says God made the arms a bronze bow; Samuel says He broke a bronze bow with the speaker's arm, so the actions are distinct.

Psalms 17:36
And Thou gavest me the protection of my salvation; Thy right hand supported me, and Thy discipline set me upright completely, and Thy discipline itself will teach me.
  2 Samuel 22:36 — Parallel forms of David’s rescue song: Both lines recount God giving the protection or defence of salvation. The psalm adds past right-hand support and discipline that set the speaker upright, followed by future teaching; Samuel instead names God's heeding that made him great.

Psalms 17:37
Thou didst widen my steps beneath me, and my footprints did not grow weak.
  2 Samuel 22:37 — Parallel forms of David’s rescue song: Both lines associate room for the speaker's steps with secure movement. The psalm says the footprints did not grow weak, while Samuel says the legs did not shake.

Psalms 17:38
I will pursue my enemies and overtake them, and I will not turn back until they fail.
  2 Samuel 22:38 — Parallel forms of David’s rescue song: Both lines anticipate pursuing enemies and refusing to turn back before their defeat is complete. The psalm speaks of overtaking and their failing; Samuel speaks of wiping them out and finishing them.

Psalms 17:39
I will press them down, and they will certainly be unable to stand; they will fall beneath my feet.
  2 Samuel 22:39 — Parallel forms of David’s rescue song: Both lines anticipate enemies being pressed or crushed down, unable to stand or rise, and falling beneath the speaker's feet. The shared sequence describes their defeat in each song.

Psalms 17:40
And Thou didst gird me with power for war; Thou didst fetter all those who rose against me beneath me,
  2 Samuel 22:40 — Parallel forms of David’s rescue song: Both lines place the speaker's war strength and opponents beneath him under God's action. The psalm reports being girded and opponents fettered; Samuel anticipates strengthening and bending attackers beneath him.

Psalms 17:41
and Thou madest my enemies turn their backs to me, and Thou didst destroy those who hated me.
  2 Samuel 22:41 — Parallel forms of David’s rescue song: Both lines recount God giving the speaker the backs of his enemies and destroying or putting to death those who hated him. The back-turning defeat image and the identified haters correspond.

Psalms 17:42
They cried out, and there was no one to save; they cried to the Lord, and He did not hear them.
  2 Samuel 22:42 — Parallel forms of David’s rescue song: Both lines depict opponents crying with no helper and the Lord not listening. The psalm reports their cries in the past; Samuel anticipates their crying while retaining the statement that He did not listen.

Psalms 17:43
And I will grind them fine like dust before the wind; I will smooth them away like mud in the streets.
  2 Samuel 22:43 — Parallel forms of David’s rescue song: Both lines compare the defeat of enemies with fine dust and street mud. The psalm anticipates grinding and smoothing them away, with dust before the wind; Samuel recounts grinding and crushing, with dust of the earth.

Psalms 17:44
Thou wilt rescue me from the people’s disputes; Thou wilt appoint me head of nations; a people I did not know served me.
