But my soul will exult in the Lord; it will delight in His salvation.
  Luke 1:46-47 — salvation praise parallel: The soul's exultation in the Lord and delight in His salvation corresponds to Mary's soul magnifying the Lord and her spirit rejoicing in God her Savior.

Psalms 34:10
All my bones will say, “Lord, who is like Thee, rescuing the poor from the hand of those stronger than he is, and the poor and needy from those plundering him?”
  Jeremiah 20:13 — deliverance praise parallel: Praise for the Lord who rescues the poor from stronger plunderers corresponds to Jeremiah's invitation to praise the Lord for rescuing a poor person's life from evildoers' hands.

Psalms 34:11
Unjust witnesses rose up; they questioned me about what I did not know.
  Mark 14:55-59 — unjust testimony parallel: The psalmist faces unjust witnesses questioning him about what he did not know. Mark describes false witnesses against Jesus whose accusations disagree, a particular narrative comparison for hostile judicial testimony.

Psalms 34:12
They repaid me evils for good things, and childlessness to my soul.
  Proverbs 17:13 — ethical warning parallel: The complaint that enemies repaid good with evil corresponds to Proverbs' warning about the person who repays evil instead of good. The proverb supplies the ethical judgment on the conduct lamented here.

Psalms 34:13
But when they troubled me, I put on sackcloth and humbled my soul with fasting; and my prayer will return to my bosom.
  Psalms 68:11-12 — lament practice parallel: Both speakers humble or bow down their souls with fasting and put on sackcloth amid reproach. The comparison preserves the actual source's when they troubled me wording.

Psalms 34:14
As for a neighbor, as for our brother, so I sought to please; like one mourning and downcast, so I humbled myself.
  Romans 15:2 — Pleasing a neighbor: conduct and instruction: The psalm joins its neighbor and brother comparison with the statement that the speaker sought to please. Paul instructs each person to please a neighbor for that neighbor's good and edification; the connection concerns this stated conduct, without supplying a cause for the psalmist's mourning.

Psalms 34:16
They tested me and mocked me with mockery; they gnashed their teeth at me.
  Acts 7:54 — hostility gesture parallel: The enemies' gnashing teeth at the speaker has a narrative counterpart in Stephen's enraged hearers grinding their teeth at him. Both depict fierce hostility toward the person addressing or facing them.

Psalms 34:17
Lord, when wilt Thou look upon this? Restore my soul from their wickedness, my only life from lions.
  Psalms 21:21-22 — literary parallel: Both pleas ask for rescue of the speaker's soul or only life and employ lions as an image of the threatening power. Psalm21 distributes the only-one and lion images across two adjacent units.

Psalms 34:18
I will thank Thee, Lord, in a great assembly; among a weighty people I will praise Thee.
  Psalms 21:23 — assembly praise parallel: The promise to thank and praise the Lord in a great assembly corresponds to the promise to recount His name to brothers and sing hymns in the midst of the assembly.

Psalms 34:19
Let those unjustly hostile to me not rejoice over me, those who hate me without cause and signal with their eyes.
  John 15:25 — fulfillment wording parallel: Jesus applies the scriptural words about being hated without cause to His rejection. That same unprovoked hatred is named here; the comparison preserves John's fulfillment claim without asserting an exclusive source identification.

Psalms 34:20
For to me they spoke peaceful things, but in anger they devised deceits.
  Psalms 27:3 — deceptive peace parallel: Peaceful speech conceals deceit in this verse. Psalm27 similarly describes people speaking peace to neighbors while harboring evils in their hearts, exposing the opposition between their words and purposes.

Psalms 34:21
They opened their mouth wide against me; they said, “Well done! Well done! Our eyes have seen!”
  Lamentations 2:16 — mocking speech parallel: The enemies open their mouths and claim that their eyes have seen the desired disaster. Jerusalem's enemies likewise open their mouths, celebrate swallowing her, and say that they found and saw the awaited day.

Psalms 34:23
Awake, Lord, and attend to my judgment, my God and my Lord, to my case.
  Psalms 43:24-27 — awakening petition parallel: The request that God awake and attend to the case corresponds to Psalm43's repeated call for the Lord to awake, arise and help an afflicted people. Both use awakening language within petitions for intervention.

Psalms 34:24
Judge me according to Thy righteousness, Lord my God; let them not rejoice over me.
  Psalms 7:9 — judgment petition comparison: Both speakers ask the Lord to judge their case according to righteousness. Here the appeal names God's righteousness; Psalm7 names the petitioner's righteousness and innocence, a significant difference in the stated ground.

Psalms 34:25
Let them not say in their hearts, “Well done! Well done for our soul!” Nor let them say, “We have swallowed him.”
  Lamentations 2:16 — victory taunt parallel: The feared enemy boast, We have swallowed him, closely corresponds to the enemies' boast over Jerusalem, We have swallowed her. The object changes from an individual to the city.

Psalms 34:26
May those rejoicing at my troubles be ashamed and abashed together; let those boasting against me wear shame and humiliation.
  Psalms 108:29 — shame clothing imagery: The request that hostile boasters wear shame and humiliation closely matches the request that slanderers wear humiliation and wrap themselves in shame like a cloak.

Psalms 34:27
May those who desire my righteousness exult and rejoice; let them always say, “May the Lord be magnified”— those who desire His servant’s peace.
  Psalms 39:17 — literary parallel: Both lines invite a supportive group to rejoice and continually say that the Lord be magnified. Here they desire the servant's righteousness and peace; Psalm39 identifies seekers of God and lovers of His salvation.

Psalms 34:28
My tongue will meditate on Thy righteousness, on Thy praise all day long.
  Psalms 70:24 — literary parallel: Both speakers promise that their tongue will meditate on God's righteousness all day. Psalm70 adds the shame of enemies, while this verse pairs righteousness with continual praise.

Psalms 35:1
For the end. Of the Lord’s servant, of David.
  2 Samuel 7:5 — named servant correspondence: The heading specifically calls David the Lord's servant. The Lord's message through Nathan addresses David as My servant, supplying the same named relationship without dating this psalm to that message.

Psalms 35:2
The lawless person speaks within himself of sinning; there is no fear of God before his eyes.
  Romans 3:18 — quotation: Romans closes its scriptural indictment with no fear of God before their eyes, matching this description of the lawless person's lack of fear before his eyes.

Psalms 35:4
The words of his mouth are lawlessness and deceit; he was unwilling to understand so as to do good.
  Romans 3:13-14 — wicked speech parallel: The psalm identifies lawlessness and deceit in the wicked person's mouth. Romans' indictment similarly locates deceit in tongues and cursing and bitterness in the mouth, giving concrete forms of corrupt speech.

Psalms 35:5
He devised lawlessness upon his bed; he stood by every way that was not good, and he was not offended by wickedness.
  Micah 2:1 — evil planning parallel: Both passages place the devising of evil upon beds. Micah expressly places the carrying out of the plans at daybreak.

Psalms 35:6
Lord, Thy mercy is in heaven, and Thy truth reaches the clouds.
  Psalms 56:11 — literary parallel: Both lines pair God's mercy reaching heaven with His truth reaching the clouds, repeating the two attributes and their corresponding heights.

Psalms 35:7
Thy righteousness is like mountains of God; Thy judgments are a great deep. Thou wilt save humans and beasts, Lord.
  Jonah 4:11 — human and animal preservation parallel: The Lord's saving humans and beasts corresponds to His question about sparing Nineveh with its many people and much livestock. Both expressly include animal life alongside human life in divine preservation.

Psalms 35:8
How Thou hast multiplied Thy mercy, God! The sons of men will hope beneath the shelter of Thy wings.
  Psalms 90:4 — refuge imagery parallel: Hope beneath God's wings is repeated in Psalm90, where divine overshadowing and protective truth accompany the assurance that the addressee will hope under His wings.

Psalms 35:9
They will be intoxicated from the richness of Thy house; Thou wilt give them the torrent of Thy delight to drink.
  Ezekiel 47:1-9 — sanctuary water imagery: The abundance of God's house and torrent offered to drink have a related sanctuary image in Ezekiel: water flows from the house, grows into an uncrossable torrent, and gives life wherever it comes. The vision and praise poem retain different settings.

Psalms 35:10
For with Thee is a fountain of life; in Thy light we will see light.
  John 1:4-9 — life and light correspondence: The psalm joins life originating with God and seeing by His light. John's prologue likewise joins life in the divine Word with light for humankind and identifies the true Light as illuminating every person.

Psalms 35:11
Extend Thy mercy to those who know Thee, and Thy righteousness to the upright in heart.
  Jeremiah 9:23 — knowledge and divine character parallel: The plea for mercy toward those knowing God and righteousness for the upright corresponds to Jeremiah's account of knowing the Lord who performs mercy, judgment and righteousness on earth.

Psalms 35:13
There the workers of lawlessness fell; they were expelled and will certainly not be able to stand.
  Proverbs 24:16 — contrasting downfall parallel: The workers of lawlessness fall and cannot stand here. Proverbs contrasts the righteous person's repeated rising with the impious growing weak amid evils, distinguishing the outcomes of downfall.

Psalms 36:1
Of David. Do not be provoked to envy by those doing evil, nor envy those committing lawlessness.
  Proverbs 24:19 — wisdom instruction parallel: Both instructions explicitly forbid envying people who do evil. Proverbs says not to rejoice over evildoers, while the psalm says not to be provoked to envy; the shared prohibition of envy is retained precisely.

Psalms 36:2
For like grass they will quickly dry up, and like green plants they will quickly fall away.
  Isaiah 40:6-8 — grass mortality imagery: The rapid passing of evildoers is compared to grass drying and plants falling. Isaiah uses withered grass and fallen flowers for all human flesh, contrasting this mortality with God's enduring word.

Psalms 36:3
Hope in the Lord and show kindness; settle in the land, and you will be pastured on its wealth.
  Deuteronomy 30:16 — land and obedience promise parallel: Trust and kindness accompany dwelling in a richly supplied land here. Deuteronomy similarly joins loving and obeying the Lord with life and blessing in the land being inherited.

Psalms 36:4
Delight yourself in the Lord, and He will give you the requests of your heart.
  1 John 5:14-15 — desire and prayer comparison: The person delighting in the Lord receives the heart's requests; John describes confidence that God hears requests made according to His will. John's explicit qualification by God's will is preserved.

Psalms 36:5
Reveal your way to the Lord; hope in Him, and He will act.
  Psalms 61:9 — trust and disclosure parallel: Revealing one's way to the Lord and hoping for His action corresponds to Psalm61's invitation to hope in God and pour out one's heart before Him as helper.

Psalms 36:6
He will bring out your righteousness like light, and your judgment like noon.
  Isaiah 58:8-10 — righteousness and light imagery: The promised emergence of righteousness like light and judgment like noon corresponds to Isaiah's righteousness going before the faithful and darkness becoming like noon after practical mercy toward the oppressed.

Psalms 36:7
Submit to the Lord and beseech Him; do not be provoked to envy by one prospering in his way, by a person committing lawless deeds.
  Psalms 72:3 — temptation and instruction contrast: The prohibition of envy at a lawless person's prosperity addresses the very temptation Asaph confesses when he envied lawless people on seeing sinners' peace.

Psalms 36:8
Cease from anger and abandon wrath; do not be provoked to envy so as to act wickedly.
  James 1:19-20 — ethical instruction parallel: Abandoning anger and wrath so as not to act wickedly corresponds to James' instruction to be slow to anger because human anger does not accomplish God's righteousness.

Psalms 36:9
For those doing evil will be destroyed, but those who wait for the Lord will inherit the land.
  Isaiah 57:13 — land inheritance promise: Those waiting for the Lord inherit the land in contrast to evildoers' destruction. Isaiah similarly contrasts helpless objects carried away by wind with people holding fast to God acquiring land and inheriting His holy mountain.

Psalms 36:10
A little longer, and the sinner will certainly not exist; you will seek his place and certainly not find it.
  Job 20:7-9 — disappearing wicked imagery: The sinner disappears and his place cannot be found; Zophar likewise pictures the ungodly vanishing so completely that observers ask where he is and his place no longer perceives him. The comparison concerns that image within Zophar's speech.

Psalms 36:11
But the meek will inherit the land and delight themselves in abundant peace.
  Matthew 5:5 — quotation parallel: Jesus' beatitude repeats the meek inheriting the earth, corresponding to the meek inheriting the land here. The psalm also adds their delight in abundant peace.

Psalms 36:12
The sinner will watch the righteous closely and gnash his teeth at him.
  Acts 7:54 — hostility gesture parallel: The sinner gnashing teeth at the righteous person is illustrated by Stephen's hearers angrily grinding their teeth at him before the assault narrated in Acts.

Psalms 36:13
But the Lord will laugh him to scorn, for He foresees that his day will come.
  Psalms 2:4 — divine derision parallel: The Lord laughing at the plotting sinner corresponds to the One dwelling in heaven laughing at gathered hostile rulers. Both place God's derision over threatening human plans.

Psalms 36:14
The sinners drew a sword and stretched their bow, to cast down the poor and needy, to slaughter the upright in heart.
  Proverbs 1:11-12 — unjust violence parallel: The sinners prepare weapons to slaughter the upright and needy. The gang's invitation in Proverbs likewise proposes bloodshed and the unjust destruction of a righteous person.

Psalms 36:15
May their sword enter their own heart, and may their bows be shattered.
  Proverbs 1:18-19 — self-destructive violence parallel: The enemy's sword is requested to enter his own heart. Proverbs similarly says that murderous lawless people store evils for themselves and take away their own life, reversing the intended benefit of their violence.

Psalms 36:16
Better is a little for the righteous than the great wealth of sinners.
  Proverbs 15:16 — little and wealth comparison: Both sayings prefer a small portion joined to a right relationship with God over great wealth without it. This psalm names righteousness versus sinners; Proverbs names fear of the Lord versus fearlessness.

Psalms 36:17
For the arms of sinners will be shattered, but the Lord sustains the righteous.
  Psalms 74:11 — opposed power imagery: Shattered sinners' arms are contrasted with sustained righteous people. Psalm74 likewise contrasts broken sinners' horns with raised righteous horns, using a different bodily image for opposed outcomes of strength.

Psalms 36:18
The Lord knows the ways of the blameless, and their inheritance will last for ever.
  Psalms 1:6 — divine knowledge parallel: The Lord knowing the ways of the blameless closely corresponds to His knowing the way of the righteous in Psalm1, where the opposing ungodly way perishes.
  1 Peter 1:4-5 — enduring inheritance comparison: The enduring inheritance of the blameless has a counterpart in Peter's imperishable, unfading inheritance kept in heaven for people guarded by God's power. The earthly land context of the psalm and Peter's heavenly location remain distinct.

Psalms 36:19
They will not be put to shame in an evil time, and in days of famine they will be filled.
  1 Kings 17:14-16 — famine provision illustration: The promise that the righteous will be filled during famine is illustrated by the Lord sustaining Elijah, the widow and her children through flour and oil that do not run out before the rains.

Psalms 36:20
For sinners will perish; the Lord’s enemies, as soon as they are glorified and exalted, vanished away like smoke—they vanished.
  Psalms 67:3 — smoke judgment imagery: The Lord's enemies vanish like smoke here; Psalm67 likewise requests that sinners vanish as smoke vanishes before God. Both apply the same disappearing-smoke image to hostile wickedness.

Psalms 36:21
The sinner borrows and will not repay, but the righteous person has compassion and gives.
  Proverbs 21:26 — righteous generosity parallel: The psalm contrasts the sinner who fails to repay with the righteous person who has compassion and gives. Proverbs similarly contrasts the impious person's desires with the righteous person's unstinting mercy and compassion.

Psalms 36:22
For those blessing him will inherit the land, but those cursing him will be destroyed.
  Genesis 12:3 — blessing and curse pattern: Both passages attach opposing outcomes to blessing and cursing another: inheritance versus destruction here, and God's blessing or curse toward those responding to Abram in Genesis. The psalm's pronoun him is not resolved into Abram's identity.

Psalms 36:23
A person’s steps are directed by the Lord, and He will desire his way.
  Proverbs 20:24 — divine direction parallel: Both statements explicitly say that a person's steps are directed by the Lord. The psalm follows this with the Lord desiring his way; Proverbs follows it with a question about human understanding.

Psalms 36:24
When he falls, he will not be dashed down, for the Lord supports his hand.
  Proverbs 24:16 — righteous recovery parallel: The righteous person can fall without being dashed down because the Lord supports him; Proverbs likewise says that the righteous can fall seven times and rise, distinguishing a fall from final ruin.

Psalms 36:25
I was young and indeed have grown old, and I have not seen a righteous person abandoned or his offspring seeking loaves.
  Psalms 111:1-3 — household provision parallel: The speaker's observation concerns a righteous person not being abandoned and his offspring not seeking bread. Psalm111 similarly associates fear of the Lord with blessed offspring and a supplied household, expressing provision across generations.

Psalms 36:26
All day long he shows mercy and lends, and his offspring will be a blessing.
  Psalms 111:5 — compassionate lending parallel: The righteous person's continual mercy and lending correspond to Psalm111's kind person who has compassion and lends. Both join generosity of disposition with the concrete act of lending.

Psalms 36:27
Turn aside from evil and do good, and dwell for age upon age.
  1 Peter 3:11 — shared ethical wording: The command to turn aside from evil and do good appears in Peter's quoted instruction as well. This psalm continues with enduring dwelling, while Peter's sentence continues with seeking peace.

Psalms 36:28
For the Lord loves judgment and will not abandon His holy ones; they will be guarded for ever. But the lawless will be driven out, and the offspring of the ungodly will be destroyed.
  1 Samuel 12:22-25 — covenant preservation comparison: The Lord will not abandon His holy ones, while the lawless are driven out. Samuel similarly assures Israel that the Lord will not reject His people and then warns them against continuing in evil.

Psalms 36:29
But the righteous will inherit the land and dwell upon it for age upon age.
  Isaiah 60:21 — land inheritance promise: Both passages explicitly join righteous people, inheritance of the land, and enduring possession: the righteous dwell there age upon age here, and Isaiah's people inherit it for ever.

Psalms 36:30
The mouth of the righteous will meditate on wisdom, and his tongue will speak judgment.
  Proverbs 10:31 — righteous speech parallel: The righteous mouth meditating on wisdom corresponds to the righteous mouth dripping wisdom in Proverbs. Both identify wisdom as the characteristic product of righteous speech.

Psalms 36:31
The law of his God is in his heart, and his steps will not be tripped up.
  Psalms 118:11 — internalized word and conduct: God's law in the heart accompanies steps that do not stumble. Psalm118 likewise places God's sayings in the heart with the stated purpose of avoiding sin, linking inward retention to conduct.

Psalms 36:32
The sinner observes the righteous and seeks to put him to death.
  Wisdom of Solomon 2:12-20 — righteous persecution parallel: The sinner watches the righteous to kill him. Wisdom develops the same hostile plan: opponents lie in wait for the righteous, resent his different life, and resolve to condemn him to a shameful death.

Psalms 36:33
But the Lord will certainly not abandon him into his hands, nor condemn him when he comes to judgment with him.
  2 Timothy 4:16-18 — divine support in judgment: The Lord will not abandon the righteous into the sinner's hands when judgment comes. Paul recounts being deserted at his defense while the Lord stood beside him, and looks toward final rescue into the heavenly kingdom.

Psalms 36:34
Wait for the Lord and keep His way; He will exalt you to inherit the land; you will see when sinners are destroyed.
  Isaiah 57:13 — trust and inheritance parallel: Waiting for the Lord and keeping His way lead to inheritance of the land here. Isaiah likewise promises land and God's holy mountain to those holding fast to Him while exposing the helplessness of rival refuges.

Psalms 36:35
I saw an ungodly person raised high and lifting himself like the cedars of Lebanon.
  Ezekiel 31:3-12 — towering tree power imagery: The ungodly person's apparent greatness is likened to Lebanon's cedars. Ezekiel depicts Assyria as a tall Lebanese cypress before its destruction: different tree names, but the common image of imposing power destined to fall.

Psalms 36:36
I passed by, and behold, he was gone; I sought him, but his place could not be found.
  Job 20:8-9 — vanished place imagery: The speaker can no longer find the ungodly person's place. Zophar's image likewise describes the wicked as an unfindable dream whose former place no longer perceives him.

Psalms 36:37
Guard innocence and look upon uprightness, for a peaceful person has a remnant.
  Proverbs 2:21 — uprightness and continuance parallel: The instruction to guard innocence and look upon uprightness accompanies a remnant for the peaceful person. Proverbs similarly promises that good, innocent and upright people will remain in the land.

Psalms 36:38
But the lawless will be destroyed together; the remnants of the ungodly will be destroyed.
  Proverbs 2:22 — lawless removal parallel: The destruction of the lawless and ungodly remnants corresponds to Proverbs' statement that ungodly ways perish from the land and the lawless are driven out of it.

Psalms 36:39
But the salvation of the righteous is from the Lord, and He is their defender in a time of affliction.
  Psalms 9:10 — refuge in distress parallel: The Lord is the righteous person's defender in a time of affliction here; Psalm9 calls Him the needy person's refuge and helper at fitting times in distress.

Psalms 36:40
The Lord will help them and rescue them; He will remove them from sinners and save them, because they have hoped in Him.
  Psalms 90:14 — causal rescue promise: The Lord rescues and saves because they hoped in Him. Psalm90 voices the same causal assurance from God: because the person hoped in Him, He will rescue and shelter him.

Psalms 37:1
A psalm of David, for remembrance concerning the Sabbath.
  Psalms 91:1 — liturgical heading parallel: The title explicitly associates this psalm with remembrance concerning the Sabbath. Psalm91's heading likewise identifies a psalm-song for the Sabbath day, preserving a particular worship designation rather than only shared authorship.

Psalms 37:2
Lord, do not rebuke me in Thy fury, nor discipline me in Thine anger.
  Psalms 6:2 — verbal repetition: The entire petition against rebuke in fury and discipline in anger repeats the wording of Psalm6:2, including the two parallel divine actions and emotional terms.

Psalms 37:3
For Thine arrows became lodged in me, and Thou didst press Thy hand upon me.
  Job 6:4 — divine arrow imagery: The Lord's arrows lodged in the speaker correspond to Job's description of the Lord's arrows in his body. Both portray suffering through arrows from God; their surrounding accounts of personal culpability differ.

Psalms 37:4
There is no healing in my flesh because of Thine anger; there is no peace for my bones because of my sins.
  Psalms 31:3-5 — penitential bodily distress: The speaker connects bodily and bone distress with sin and God's anger. Psalm 31 likewise places aging bones and God's heavy hand before an explicit confession of sin and forgiveness.

Psalms 37:5
For my lawless deeds rose above my head; like a heavy load they weighed upon me.
  Ezra 9:6 — overwhelming guilt imagery: Lawless deeds rise above the speaker's head here. Ezra's confession similarly says the community's lawless deeds have multiplied above their heads and their offenses grown to heaven, using height to express overwhelming guilt.

Psalms 37:6
My bruises stank and rotted because of my foolishness.
  Isaiah 1:4-6 — unhealed wound imagery: The bruises that stink and rot because of folly correspond to Isaiah's wounded, bruised and inflamed portrayal of a sinful nation without dressings. The comparison concerns bodily imagery in confessions or accusations of wrongdoing.

Psalms 37:7
I suffered misery and was bent down completely; all day long I walked about downcast.
  Psalms 41:10 — downcast walking parallel: Both speakers describe walking downcast while suffering. Here the speaker is bent down in misery; Psalm41 asks why he walks downcast under an enemy's oppression.

Psalms 37:8
For my loins were filled with mockings, and there is no healing in my flesh.
  Psalms 37:4 — same-chapter literary repetition: The declaration that there is no healing in the speaker's flesh repeats the same phrase from verse 4. The earlier line names God's anger and sins; this line adds loins filled with mockings, preserving the source's unusual wording within the repeated bodily lament.

Psalms 37:9
I was afflicted and humbled exceedingly; I roared from the groaning of my heart.
  Psalms 6:7 — groaning lament parallel: The deeply humbled speaker roars from the groaning of his heart. Psalm6 similarly describes weariness through groaning, extending the lament into nights of tears upon the bed.

Psalms 37:10
Lord, all my desire is before Thee, and my groaning was not hidden from Thee.
  Romans 8:26-27 — inner prayer comparison: The speaker's desires and groaning are fully before God. Romans likewise connects groanings beyond words with the One searching hearts; there the Spirit is explicitly the intercessor helping human weakness.

Psalms 37:11
My heart was troubled; my strength abandoned me; and the light of my eyes—even that is not with me.
  Psalms 12:4 — eye light and failing life: Loss of strength and the eyes' light corresponds to Psalm12's plea for God to give light to the eyes lest the speaker sleep into death. One laments the loss, and the other asks its reversal.

Psalms 37:12
My friends and neighbors approached and stood opposite me, and those nearest me stood far away.
  Job 19:13-15 — social isolation parallel: The psalm places friends and neighbors opposite the sufferer and those nearest him far away. Job likewise describes withdrawn brothers, merciless friends and neighbors treating him as a foreigner; their precise behaviors differ.
  Luke 23:49 — distant acquaintances correspondence: The close acquaintances standing far away have a narrative counterpart in Jesus' acquaintances and the women watching from a distance. Luke describes these people remaining as witnesses to the suffering scene.

Psalms 37:13
Those seeking my soul used violence; those seeking evils for me spoke vain things and meditated on deceptions all day long.
  Mark 14:1 — deceptive lethal pursuit: Those seeking the speaker's life use violence and meditate on deceptions. Mark likewise describes leaders seeking a way to seize Jesus by deceit and kill Him, a specific narrative parallel for the combination of deception and lethal intent.

Psalms 37:14
But like a deaf person I did not hear, like one unable to speak who does not open his mouth.
  Isaiah 53:7 — silent sufferer parallel: The sufferer becomes like one unable to speak who does not open his mouth. Isaiah's afflicted servant is likewise described twice as not opening his mouth, with the silence of a lamb before shearers.
  Matthew 27:12-14 — narrative silence parallel: The speaker depicts his silence amid persecution by comparison with a person unable to speak. Matthew describes Jesus giving no answer to the accusations of the chief priests and elders, despite Pilate's question about their testimony.

Psalms 37:15
I became like a person who does not hear and has no rebukes in his mouth.
  1 Peter 2:23 — nonretaliatory speech parallel: The speaker has no rebukes in his mouth despite hostility. Peter describes Christ not returning reviling or threats when abused and suffering, while entrusting His cause to the just Judge.

Psalms 37:17
For I said, “Let my enemies never rejoice over me!” When my feet were shaken, they boasted against me.
  Psalms 12:5 — enemy triumph parallel: Both passages connect the speaker's being shaken with enemies exulting over him and seek to prevent that outcome. Psalm12 supplies the corresponding boast that the enemy has prevailed.

Psalms 37:19
For I will announce my lawlessness and be concerned about my sin.
  Psalms 31:5 — confession parallel: The announcement of lawlessness corresponds to Psalm31's explicit confession of lawlessness to the Lord without concealing sin. Psalm31 also records the resulting forgiveness.

Psalms 37:20
But my enemies live and have become stronger than I, and those hating me unjustly have multiplied.
  Psalms 68:5 — hostile numbers and strength: The enemies who unjustly hate the speaker multiply and grow stronger here. Psalm68 likewise describes numerous causeless haters and unjustly pursuing enemies becoming strong.

Psalms 37:21
Those repaying evils for good things slandered me because I pursued righteousness, and they cast me, the beloved, away like an abhorred corpse.
  Proverbs 17:13 — ethical warning parallel: The complaint of evil repaid for good corresponds to Proverbs' warning that evils will remain in the house of one who repays evil instead of good. The psalm also supplies its own slander and rejected-corpse imagery.

Psalms 37:23
Attend to my aid, Lord of my salvation.
  Psalms 69:2 — verbal petition parallel: The appeal for the Lord of salvation to attend to the speaker's aid corresponds to Psalm69's adjoining request for the Lord to save and for God to attend to his help.

Psalms 38:1
For the end. For Jeduthun. A song of David.
  Psalms 61:1 — named liturgical heading parallel: Both headings combine the for-the-end designation, the name Jeduthun, and David's song or psalm. The shared named recipient makes the title comparison more specific than Davidic authorship alone.

Psalms 38:2
I said, “I will guard my ways so as not to sin with my tongue.” I placed a guard upon my mouth when the sinner stood against me.
  Psalms 140:3 — guarded mouth imagery: The speaker places a guard on his mouth to avoid sinning with his tongue. Psalm140 explicitly asks the Lord to set a guard for the mouth and a restraining door around the lips.

Psalms 38:3
I became mute and was humbled and kept silent from good things, and my pain was renewed.
  Psalms 31:3 — silence and distress comparison: Silence is followed by renewed pain here; Psalm31 similarly associates keeping silent with bones growing old amid continual crying. The local contexts differ: restraint before the sinner here and confession of sin in Psalm31.

Psalms 38:4
My heart grew hot within me, and in my meditation a fire will be kindled. I spoke with my tongue:
  Jeremiah 20:9 — inner fire and speech: The speaker's heart grew hot, and a fire will be kindled in his meditation, as the psalm moves from silence to speech. Jeremiah says he will no longer name the Lord or speak in His name, but describes a burning fire in his bones that he cannot bear.

Psalms 38:5
“Make known to me, Lord, my limit and the number of my days, what it is, so that I may know what I lack.
  Job 14:5 — bounded lifetime parallel: The prayer asks God to reveal life's limit and number of days. Job states that a person's months are numbered with God and a divinely set time cannot be exceeded, supplying the same combination of counting and a boundary.

Psalms 38:6
Behold, Thou madest my days handbreadths, and my existence is as nothing before Thee. Yet all things are vanity—every living person. Diapsalma.
  Psalms 143:4 — fleeting human life imagery: The speaker's short days and human vanity correspond to Psalm143's human being likened to futility and days passing like a shadow. Both use a brief span and insubstantial imagery for human life.

Psalms 38:7
Indeed a person passes through as an image; yet they are troubled in vain. He stores treasure and does not know for whom he will gather it.
  Luke 12:18-21 — uncertain treasure recipient: The person gathers treasure without knowing for whom he gathers it. Jesus' rich fool stores goods for years but is told his life is demanded that very night, facing God's question about whose the prepared possessions will be.

Psalms 38:8
And now, what is my expectation? Is it not the Lord? And my existence comes from Thee.
  Acts 17:25-28 — dependent existence parallel: The confession that the speaker's existence comes from God corresponds to Paul's proclamation that God gives everyone life and breath and that in Him people live, move and exist.

Psalms 38:9
Rescue me from all my lawless deeds; Thou hast given me as a reproach to a fool.
  Psalms 129:8 — release from lawlessness parallel: The personal plea for rescue from all lawless deeds corresponds to the promise that the Lord will redeem Israel from all its lawless deeds. The scope changes from the individual to Israel while retaining comprehensive release from wrongdoing.

Psalms 38:10
I became mute and did not open my mouth, because Thou art the One who made me.
  Psalms 37:14 — silent mouth wording: Becoming mute and not opening the mouth repeats the comparison with a person unable to speak who does not open his mouth. This verse explicitly attributes the restraint to God as maker.

Psalms 38:11
Remove Thy scourges from me; from the strength of Thy hand I wasted away.
  Job 13:21 — withdrawn hand petition: The plea to remove scourges and the wasting force of God's hand corresponds to Job's request that God keep His hand away and not overwhelm him with terror.

Psalms 38:12
With rebukes for lawlessness Thou didst discipline a person and melt his soul like a spider. Yet every person is troubled in vain. Diapsalma.
  Proverbs 3:11-12 — divine correction comparison: The Lord's rebukes and discipline for lawlessness correspond to the instruction about receiving the Lord's discipline and correction. Proverbs explicitly supplies a loving father-and-son framework, while the psalm stresses human frailty.

Psalms 38:13
Hear my prayer, Lord, and give ear to my supplication; do not remain silent at my tears. For I am a sojourner with Thee and a temporary resident like all my fathers.
  1 Chronicles 29:15 — sojourner confession parallel: Both prayers call the worshippers sojourners or resident strangers before God like all their fathers. Chronicles also compares earthly days to a shadow, matching this psalm's context of brief life.

Psalms 38:14
Give me relief, that I may be refreshed, before I depart and no longer exist.”
  Job 10:20-22 — respite before departure: Both speakers ask for a little relief before an irreversible departure from mortal life. The psalm says before I depart and no longer exist; Job speaks of going without return to a dark land.

Psalms 39:3
He brought me up from a pit of misery and from muddy slime; He set my feet upon a rock and directed my steps.
  Psalms 68:3 — mud and footing imagery: Being lifted from muddy slime and set on a rock reverses the situation in Psalm68, where the sufferer is stuck in deep mud without firm ground. The shared image joins mire with the presence or absence of stable footing.

Psalms 39:4
He put a new song into my mouth, a hymn to our God. Many will see and fear and hope in the Lord.
  Psalms 97:1 — new salvation song parallel: God gives a new song after rescue here; Psalm97 calls for a new song because the Lord has done wonders and won salvation. Both connect new praise with saving action.

Psalms 39:5
Blessed is the man whose hope is the Lord’s name and who has not looked toward vanities and false frenzies.
  Jeremiah 17:7 — trust beatitude parallel: Both pronounce blessed the person whose hope or trust is in the Lord. The psalm additionally rejects vanities and false frenzies, while Jeremiah's context contrasts dependence on human power.

Psalms 39:6
Many are the wonders Thou hast done, Lord my God, and in Thy purposes there is no one who can be compared with Thee. I announced and spoke; they multiplied beyond number.
  Job 5:9 — innumerable divine wonders: The speaker cannot number God's many wonders and purposes. Eliphaz likewise describes the Lord's great, extraordinary works as beyond numbering; the connection is to this statement within his speech.

Psalms 39:7
Sacrifice and offering Thou didst not desire, but ears Thou didst prepare for me. Burnt offering and an offering for sin Thou didst not request.
  Hebrews 10:5-6 — quotation with wording difference: Hebrews quotes the rejection of sacrifice, offering, burnt offerings and sin offerings. This Psalms text says ears were prepared, while Hebrews says a body was prepared; both exact wordings are preserved without harmonizing them.

Psalms 39:8
Then I said, “Behold, I have come; in the scroll of the book it is written concerning me.
  Hebrews 10:7 — quotation: Hebrews repeats the declaration of coming and the scroll of the book written concerning the speaker. Those are the distinctive clauses of this psalm unit before the following statement of God's will.

Psalms 39:9
I desired to do Thy will, my God, and Thy law within my belly.”
  Hebrews 10:7-10 — quotation and interpretation: Hebrews quotes coming to do God's will and interprets that will through Jesus Christ's once-for-all bodily offering. The psalm's additional law within my belly wording remains specific to this source.

Psalms 39:10
I proclaimed good news of righteousness in a great assembly; behold, I will certainly not restrain my lips; Lord, Thou knowest.
  Psalms 21:23 — public proclamation parallel: The testimony of proclaiming righteousness in a great assembly corresponds to the pledge to declare God's name among brothers and sing hymns within the assembly. Both locate proclamation in a gathered community, with different past and future wording.

Psalms 39:11
I did not hide Thy righteousness in my heart; I spoke of Thy truth and Thy salvation. I did not hide Thy mercy and Thy truth from a great congregation.
  Acts 20:20-21 — unwithheld public witness: The speaker has not hidden divine righteousness, truth and salvation from the congregation. Paul likewise says he held back nothing beneficial and taught publicly, bearing witness to repentance toward God and faith in Jesus.

Psalms 39:12
But Thou, Lord, do not put Thy compassions far from me; Thy mercy and Thy truth have continually sustained me.
  Exodus 34:6 — divine character correspondence: The plea names God's compassions, mercy and truth as sustaining the speaker. The Lord's self-proclamation to Moses names Him compassionate, merciful, abundant in mercy and true, supplying the same cluster of attributes.

Psalms 39:13
For evils without number surrounded me; my lawless deeds overtook me, and I could not see. They multiplied beyond the hairs of my head, and my heart abandoned me.
  Psalms 37:5 — overwhelming transgression imagery: Lawless deeds overtake and overwhelm the speaker here. Psalm37 similarly describes them rising above his head and weighing like a heavy load, expressing transgression as a burden exceeding his strength.

Psalms 39:14
Be pleased, Lord, to rescue me; Lord, attend to helping me.
  Psalms 69:2 — literary refrain parallel: The plea for the Lord to rescue and attend to helping the speaker closely corresponds to Psalm69's request for the Lord to save and for God to attend to his help.

Psalms 39:15
May those seeking my soul to remove it be ashamed and abashed together; may those desiring evils for me turn backward and be abashed.
  Psalms 69:3 — literary refrain parallel: Both prayers ask that seekers of the speaker's life be ashamed and that those wishing him evil turn backward in disgrace, retaining the same paired enemy groups and reversals.

Psalms 39:16
Let those saying to me, “Well done! Well done!” receive their shame immediately.
  Psalms 69:4 — literary refrain parallel: The enemies' Well done taunt and requested immediate shame correspond to Psalm69's Well, Well taunt and requested turning back immediately in shame.

Psalms 39:17
May all seeking Thee exult and rejoice in Thee, Lord; let those loving Thy salvation always say, “May the Lord be magnified!”
  Psalms 69:5 — literary refrain parallel: Both verses invite all who seek God to rejoice and all who love His salvation to continually declare that God or the Lord be magnified.

Psalms 39:18
But I am poor and needy; the Lord will care for me. Thou art my helper and defender; my God, do not delay.
  Psalms 69:6 — literary refrain parallel: Both speakers identify themselves as poor and needy, call God helper and rescuer or defender, and end with the request not to delay.

Psalms 40:2
Blessed is the one who understands the poor and needy; in an evil day the Lord will rescue him.
  Proverbs 19:17 — care for the poor and divine repayment: The blessing and promised rescue for someone attentive to the poor correspond to Proverbs' assurance that God repays the person showing mercy to the poor.

Psalms 40:3
May the Lord guard him, keep him alive and bless him in the land; may He not hand him over into his enemy’s hands.
  Psalms 36:33 — protection from enemy hands: The request that the Lord not hand the person into enemy hands corresponds to the assurance that the Lord will not abandon the righteous into the sinner's hands when he faces judgment.

Psalms 40:4
May the Lord help him upon his bed of pain; Thou didst turn all his bed in his weakness.
  James 5:14-15 — divine help in sickness: The request for God's help on a bed of pain corresponds to James' instruction to pray over a sick person and his assurance that the Lord will raise that person up.

Psalms 40:5
I said, “Lord, have mercy on me; heal my soul, for I sinned against Thee.”
  Psalms 102:3 — forgiveness and healing correspondence: The petition joins mercy and healing of the soul with confession of sin. Psalm102 likewise places God's gracious treatment of lawless deeds beside His healing of diseases.

Psalms 40:6
My enemies spoke evils against me: “When will he die and his name perish?”
  Psalms 108:13-15 — imprecatory wording correspondence: The enemies here wish the sufferer's death and the loss of his name. Psalm 108 likewise voices a wish for a person's name and memorial to be erased. The comparison concerns this imprecatory wording without equating the persons or settling the speaking roles of every imprecation.

Psalms 40:7
If someone entered to see me, he spoke vainly; his heart gathered lawlessness for himself; he went outside and spoke.
  Proverbs 26:24-26 — concealed hostility parallel: The visitor speaks vainly while gathering lawlessness in his heart, then goes outside and speaks. Proverbs exposes an enemy's agreeable lips concealing deceitful purposes in his heart and hidden enmity.

Psalms 40:8
Together all my enemies whispered against me; against me they devised evils for me.
  Matthew 26:3-4 — collective hostile planning: The enemies whisper together and devise evils against the speaker. Matthew gives a concrete parallel of leaders gathering together and taking counsel to seize Jesus by deceit and kill Him.

Psalms 40:10
For even the man of my peace, in whom I hoped, who ate my loaves, magnified treachery against me.
  John 13:18 — quotation with wording difference: Jesus quotes Scripture about the person eating His bread lifting a heel against Him. This psalm describes a trusted companion eating the speaker's loaves and magnifying treachery; John applies the meal-and-betrayal pattern to Jesus' impending betrayal.

Psalms 40:11
But Thou, Lord, have mercy on me and raise me up, and I will repay them.
  Psalms 29:3-4 — restoration petition and testimony: The prayer for mercy and being raised up corresponds to Psalm 29's testimony of crying to the Lord, being healed and having the soul brought up from Hades. The shared restoration theme does not identify the two experiences as one event.

Psalms 40:12
By this I knew that Thou didst desire me: my enemy will certainly not rejoice over me.
  Psalms 29:2 — enemy joy prevented: The speaker recognizes divine favor because his enemy will not rejoice over him. Psalm29 likewise praises God for taking the speaker up without gladdening his enemies over him.

Psalms 40:13
But Thou didst sustain me because of innocence and establish me before Thee for ever.
  Psalms 16:15 — righteousness before God: Being sustained in innocence and established before God corresponds to Psalm16's expectation of appearing before God's face in righteousness. Both join right standing with access to the divine presence.

Psalms 40:14
Blessed be the Lord, Israel’s God, from age to age. May it be! May it be!
  Psalms 71:18-19 — liturgical doxology parallel: Both doxologies bless the Lord as Israel's God, extend the blessing across the ages, and conclude with the doubled May it be response.
  1 Chronicles 16:36 — liturgical doxology parallel: Chronicles repeats the blessing of the Lord, Israel's God, from age to age, followed by the people's Amen. It provides a communal worship counterpart to this psalm's closing doxology.

Psalms 41:2
As the deer longs for springs of water, so my soul longs for Thee, God.
  Psalms 62:2 — Thirst and longing for God: The deer longing for water expresses the soul's longing for God. Psalm 62 likewise describes the soul thirsting for God and the flesh longing for Him in a waterless land. Both express desire for God through longing for water, without saying the deer was denied access to the springs.

Psalms 41:3
My soul thirsted for the living God. When will I come and appear before God’s face?
  Psalms 83:3 — Longing to approach the living God: The soul thirsts for the living God and asks when it will appear before His face. Psalm 83 joins longing for the Lord's courts with heart and flesh exulting in the living God, connecting desire for divine presence with that particular designation.

Psalms 41:4
My tears became my bread day and night, while it is said to me every day, “Where is your God?”
  Psalms 79:6 — Tears as food: The speaker's tears became bread by day and night. Psalm 79 asks whether God will feed the people bread of tears and give them tears to drink; both make prolonged grief into the sufferer's food.
  Joel 2:17 — The taunt asking where God is: Daily tears accompany the taunt, 'Where is your God?' Joel's priests plead that God's inheritance not become a reproach so that the nations do not ask, 'Where is their God?' The similar taunt occurs in personal lament and communal intercession respectively.

Psalms 41:5
These things I remembered and poured out my soul within me: for I will pass through the place of a wonderful tent to God’s house, with a voice of exultation and thanksgiving, a celebrating sound.
  Psalms 94:1-2 — Approaching God with exultation and thanksgiving: The speaker remembers and pours out his soul, then says he will pass through the place of a wonderful tent to God's house with exultation and thanksgiving. Psalm 94 invites people to exult and come before God with thanksgiving; the shared approach to God has those two expressions of praise.

Psalms 41:6
Why are you deeply grieved, soul, and why do you disturb me? Hope in God, for I will thank Him: my God is the salvation of my face.
  Psalms 42:5 — A repeated address to the troubled soul: Both verses ask why the soul is deeply grieved and disturbing the speaker, tell it to hope in God, and anticipate thanksgiving to the God who is the salvation of his face. This sustained refrain connects the neighboring laments.

Psalms 41:8
Deep calls upon deep at the sound of Thy waterfalls; all Thy billows and Thy waves passed over me.
  Jonah 2:4 — Divine billows and waves passing over the speaker: The psalm's water imagery culminates in all God's billows and waves passing over the speaker. Jonah uses that same extended statement while praying from the sea creature; the verbal correspondence does not identify the psalm's speaker with Jonah.

Psalms 41:9
By day the Lord will command His mercy, and at night a song is with me, a prayer to the God of my life.
  Psalms 91:2-4 — Mercy and song across day and night: The psalm anticipates the Lord commanding mercy by day and has a song and prayer at night. Psalm 91 commends music to God with declaration of mercy in the morning and truth by night. The daily rhythm joins mercy and worship, with different actions assigned to God and the worshippers.

Psalms 41:10
I will say to God, “Thou art my supporter; why hast Thou forgotten me? Why do I walk downcast while my enemy oppresses me?”
  Psalms 42:2 — Questioning rejection under enemy oppression: Psalm 41 asks God why He has forgotten the speaker and why the speaker goes downcast while an enemy oppresses. Psalm 42 repeats the downcast and enemy question after asking why God has thrust him away; the opening descriptions of God's apparent withdrawal differ.

Psalms 41:11
As they crushed my bones, those afflicting me reproached me, saying to me every day, “Where is your God?”
  Psalms 113:10 — The reproach asking where God is: The enemies reproach the speaker with 'Where is your God?' Psalm 113 asks that the nations not say 'Where is their God?' Both make denial of God's apparent presence a hostile challenge, without identifying the enemies as the same group.

Psalms 41:12
Why are you deeply grieved, soul, and why do you disturb me? Hope in God, for I will thank Him: my God is the salvation of my face.
  Psalms 41:6 — same-chapter refrain of hope: The closing verse repeats the earlier address to the deeply grieved soul, the command to hope in God, and the anticipated thanksgiving. Its recurrence gives the renewed lament the same response of hope that ended the earlier movement.

Psalms 42:1
A psalm of David. Judge me, God, and judge my case against an unholy nation; rescue me from an unjust and deceitful person.
  Psalms 34:1 — Appeal for God to judge a case against wrongdoers: Psalm 42 asks God to judge the speaker's case against an unholy nation and rescue him from an unjust, deceitful person. Psalm 34 opens by asking the Lord to judge those who wrong the speaker; both seek divine adjudication against hostile wrongdoers, with Psalm 34 also using warfare language.

Psalms 42:2
For Thou, God, art my strength; why didst Thou thrust me away? And why do I walk downcast while my enemy oppresses me?
  Psalms 41:10 — The repeated downcast question: Both speakers question God's apparent withdrawal and ask why they go downcast while an enemy oppresses. Psalm 42 says God thrust him away, whereas Psalm 41 asks why God forgot him; the repeated question preserves that difference.

Psalms 42:3
Send out Thy light and Thy truth; they guided me and brought me to Thy holy mountain and to Thy dwellings.
  Psalms 24:4-5 — Divine truth as guidance: Psalm 42 asks God to send His light and truth, then says they guided and brought him to His holy mountain and dwellings. Psalm 24 asks God to guide the speaker into His truth and teach His paths. Both connect truth with divine guidance, without changing Psalm 42's completed guidance into a wholly future action.

Psalms 42:4
Then I will go in to God’s altar, to God who gladdens my youth; I will thank Thee with the lyre, God, my God.
  Psalms 25:6-7 — Altar approach joined to praise: Psalm 42 anticipates going to God's altar and giving thanks with a lyre. Psalm 25 anticipates going around God's altar to hear praise and recount His wonders. Both place praise at an approach to the altar, while describing different movements and sounds.

Psalms 42:5
Why are you deeply grieved, soul, and why do you disturb me? Hope in God, for I will thank Him: my God is the salvation of my face.
  Psalms 41:6 — The refrain shared by adjacent laments: Psalm 42 ends with the same questions to the deeply grieved soul, command to hope in God, and anticipated thanksgiving found in Psalm 41. The repeated wording makes a concrete literary connection between the neighboring poems.

Psalms 43:2
God, with our ears we heard; our fathers announced to us the work Thou didst in their days, in ancient days.
  Psalms 77:3-4 — Fathers transmitting God's ancient works: The community says its fathers announced the work God did in ancient days. Psalm 77 likewise speaks of what the fathers recounted and of announcing the Lord's mighty acts and wonders to another generation; both explicitly transmit divine deeds through generations.

Psalms 43:3
Thy hand destroyed nations, and Thou didst plant our fathers; Thou didst afflict peoples and cast them out.
  Exodus 15:17 — God planting His people: Psalm 43 contrasts God's destruction of nations with His planting of the fathers. The sea song asks God to bring and plant His people in the mountain of His inheritance. The planting image describes divine establishment of the people, expressed as remembered action and petition respectively.

Psalms 43:4
For they did not inherit the land by their sword, and their arm did not save them, but Thy right hand, Thine arm and the light of Thy face, because Thou didst delight in them.
  Joshua 24:12-13 — Possession of the land beyond human weapons: The psalm denies that the fathers inherited the land by their sword or were saved by their arm, crediting God's power and favor. Joshua's retrospect likewise denies conquest by their sword or bow and describes land and cities given without their labor.

Psalms 43:5
Thou Thyself art my King and my God, who commands the salvations of Jacob.
  Psalms 73:12 — God as King who brings salvation: Psalm 43 calls God its King and describes Him as the one who commands Jacob's salvations. Psalm 73 calls God the community's King and says He worked salvation in the earth. Both pair divine kingship with salvation, using a present description and remembered action respectively.

Psalms 43:6
Through Thee we will gore our enemies, and through Thy name we will bring to nothing those rising against us.
  Deuteronomy 33:17 — Goring peoples as an image of victory: The community says it will gore enemies through God. Joseph's blessing depicts horns with which he will gore nations and identifies Ephraim and Manasseh. Both use the animal action for human victory, but the psalm does not name Joseph or those tribes.

Psalms 43:7
For I will not hope in my bow, and my sword will not save me.
  1 Samuel 17:47 — Salvation not secured by weapons: The psalmist refuses to hope in his bow or expect salvation from his sword. David announces that the Lord does not save by sword and spear because the battle belongs to Him. Both explicitly deny that military weapons are the source of salvation.

Psalms 43:10
But now Thou didst thrust us away and put us to shame, and Thou wilt not go out with our forces.
  Psalms 59:12 — Rejection and God's absence from the forces: Both verses say God rejected the people and will not go out with their forces. Psalm 43 adds their shame; Psalm 59 introduces the statement with a question. The extended wording, including the army's lost divine accompaniment, is shared.

Psalms 43:11
Thou didst turn us backward before our enemies, and those who hate us plundered for themselves.
  Leviticus 26:17 — Defeat before enemies under divine action: The psalm attributes the people's retreat before enemies to God. Leviticus threatens falling before enemies and flight when God sets His face against the disobedient. The military reversal is comparable, but Psalm 43:18 denies covenant unfaithfulness, so Leviticus's stated cause is not imposed on the lament.

Psalms 43:12
Thou gavest us like sheep for eating and didst scatter us among the nations.
  Leviticus 26:33 — Scattering among the nations: Psalm 43 says God gave the people like sheep for eating and scattered them among nations. Leviticus threatens scattering among the nations in its covenant sanctions. The scattering language corresponds, while the psalm's assertion of fidelity prevents treating the passages as identical explanations of guilt.

Psalms 43:13
Thou soldest Thy people without a price, and there was no abundance in their exchange.
  Isaiah 52:3 — A people sold without a price: The psalm complains that God sold His people without a price and obtained no abundance in exchange. Isaiah says the people were sold for nothing and will be ransomed without silver. Both use a sale without payment for the people's humiliation; Isaiah also supplies a promise of ransom.

Psalms 43:14
Thou madest us a reproach to our neighbors, scorn and ridicule to those around us.
  Psalms 78:4 — Reproach and ridicule from neighboring peoples: Both verses describe becoming a reproach to neighbors and an object of sneering and ridicule to those around. The matching sequence specifies the communal shame beyond a general reference to enemies.

Psalms 43:15
Thou madest us a parable among the nations, a shaking of the head among the peoples.
  Deuteronomy 28:37 — Becoming a parable among nations: The community says God made it a parable among nations. Deuteronomy's curses predict becoming a riddle, parable and tale among the nations. The particular description of public disgrace corresponds; the curse's disobedience setting is distinct from Psalm 43's profession of covenant fidelity.

Psalms 43:16
All day long my humiliation is before me, and the shame of my face covered me,
  Psalms 68:8 — Shame as a covering: Psalm 43 says all-day humiliation is before the speaker and the shame of his face covered him. Psalm 68 says embarrassment covered his face alongside endured reproach. Both express disgrace as a covering, while the source's shame of the face covering the speaker is not rewritten as the target's covering of the face.

Psalms 43:18
All these things came upon us, and we did not forget Thee or act unjustly in Thy covenant.
  Psalms 118:61 — Affliction without forgetting divine instruction: Despite all that happened, the community says it did not forget God or act unjustly toward His covenant. Psalm 118 says sinners' cords entangled the speaker but he did not forget God's law. Both set remembered fidelity against oppression, rather than assuming suffering proves apostasy.

Psalms 43:20
For Thou didst humble us in a place of affliction, and death’s shadow covered us.
  Psalms 106:10-14 — Humiliation in death's shadow with different stated causes: Psalm 43 says God humbled the people in affliction and death's shadow covered them. Psalm 106 describes people in death's shadow whose hearts were humbled in toil, then rescued. Psalm 106 expressly attributes their plight to rejected divine counsel; Psalm 43's surrounding profession of fidelity is preserved as a difference.

Psalms 43:21
If we forgot our God’s name, and if we spread our hands toward a foreign god,
  Deuteronomy 6:13-14 — Foreign worship as a rejected alternative: The psalm hypothetically considers forgetting God's name and spreading hands to a foreign god before asserting God's knowledge. Deuteronomy commands service of the Lord and forbids following other gods. The comparison concerns exclusive allegiance; the psalm's conditional words are not turned into an admission of idolatry.

Psalms 43:22
will God not search these things out? For He knows the heart’s hidden things.
  Jeremiah 17:9-10 — God searching the hidden heart: The psalm asks whether God will search out betrayal, because He knows the heart's hidden things. Jeremiah calls the heart deep and declares that the Lord examines hearts and tests kidneys. Both assign knowledge of otherwise concealed inward conduct to divine examination.

Psalms 43:23
For Thy sake we are put to death all day long; we were reckoned as sheep for slaughter.
  Romans 8:36 — Quotation of sheep destined for slaughter: Romans explicitly introduces as written the psalm's statement that for God's sake the people are put to death all day and reckoned as sheep for slaughter. Paul uses the quotation amid affliction that cannot separate believers from Christ's love.

Psalms 43:24
Awake! Why dost Thou sleep, Lord? Arise, and do not thrust us away completely.
  Psalms 77:65-66 — God awakening to act against enemies: Psalm 43 asks why God sleeps and calls Him to awake and arise. Psalm 77 narrates the Lord awakening like a sleeper and striking enemies backward. Both use waking for renewed divine action, while the lament's appeal is distinct from the other poem's narrative simile.

Psalms 43:25
Why dost Thou turn Thy face away and forget our poverty and affliction?
  Psalms 12:2 — Hidden face and apparent divine forgetting: Psalm 43 asks why God turns His face away and forgets the people's poverty and affliction. Psalm 12 combines the same two complaints, asking how long God will forget the speaker and turn His face away. The parallel keeps the communal and individual objects distinct.

Psalms 43:26
For our soul was humbled to the dust; our belly clung to the earth.
  Psalms 118:25 — The soul brought down to the ground: The community's soul is humbled to dust and its belly clings to earth. Psalm 118 says the speaker's soul clung to the ground and asks for life. Both portray the soul's distress as prostration against the earth, with different accompanying petitions.

Psalms 43:27
Arise, Lord; help us, and redeem us for the sake of Thy name.
  Psalms 78:9 — Help and rescue for God's name: Psalm 43 asks God to arise, help and redeem for His name's sake. Psalm 78 asks God the Savior to help and rescue for the glory of His name, also seeking propitiation for sins. The linked appeal to aid and the divine name is specific, without importing that confession into Psalm 43.

Psalms 44:1
For the end, concerning those who will be changed. Of the sons of Korah, for understanding. A song concerning the beloved.
  Psalms 68:1 — The heading concerning those who will be changed: Both headings use the designation 'concerning those who will be changed.' Psalm 44 also names the sons of Korah and a song concerning the beloved, whereas Psalm 68 names David. The connection is the exact unusual heading, without assigning it an unexpressed musical meaning or common occasion.

Psalms 44:3
Fair in beauty beyond the sons of men, grace was poured upon your lips; therefore God blessed you for ever.
  Luke 4:22 — Grace associated with lips and spoken words: The king's lips have grace poured upon them in the psalm. Luke describes hearers marveling at words of grace coming from Jesus' mouth. The correspondence concerns gracious speech; Luke does not explicitly quote this verse or identify the psalm's unnamed king in that scene.

Psalms 44:5
and stretch forth, prosper and reign for the sake of truth and meekness and righteousness; your right hand will guide you wondrously.
  Isaiah 11:4-5 — Royal righteousness and truth: The psalm commands its royal addressee to prosper and reign for truth, meekness and righteousness. Isaiah describes Jesse's shoot judging the lowly, girded with righteousness and truth. The shared royal qualities are particular, while Isaiah's treatment of the lowly is not rewritten as an identical phrase about meekness.

Psalms 44:7
Your throne, God, is for age upon age; a rod of uprightness is the rod of your kingdom.
  Hebrews 1:8 — The throne and upright scepter quoted concerning the Son: Hebrews expressly introduces the throne enduring for the age and the scepter of uprightness as words concerning the Son. These correspond to Psalm 44's address to God concerning His enduring throne and upright royal rod.

Psalms 44:8
You loved righteousness and hated lawlessness; therefore God, your God, anointed you with the oil of exultation beyond your companions.
  Hebrews 1:9 — Quotation of anointing beyond companions: Hebrews repeats the love of righteousness, hatred of lawlessness and consequent anointing by God with oil of exultation beyond companions. Its preceding verse identifies this speech as concerning the Son; the extended sequence corresponds directly to the psalm.

Psalms 44:10
Daughters of kings are in your honor; the queen stood at your right in clothing woven with gold, arrayed in varied embroidery.
  1 Kings 2:19 — A woman honored at the king's right: The psalm places the queen at the king's right in golden clothing. Kings describes a throne placed for Solomon's mother Bathsheba, who sits at his right. The court position is comparable, but the psalm's queen is not identified as Bathsheba, and standing is not conflated with sitting.

Psalms 44:11
Listen, daughter, look and incline your ear, and forget your people and your father’s house,
  Genesis 2:24 — Parental ties in royal and marital settings: The royal poem tells the daughter to forget her people and father's house before describing the king's desire. Genesis says a man leaves father and mother and cleaves to his wife. The departure from parental ties is comparable within these relational settings, with the addressed woman and the departing man kept distinct.

Psalms 44:13
And daughters of Tyre will bow down to him with gifts; the rich of the people will entreat your favor.
  Psalms 71:10-11 — Foreign gifts and royal homage: Psalm 44 describes Tyre's daughters bowing to him with gifts and the rich entreating the addressed woman's favor. Psalm 71 describes foreign kings offering gifts and bowing before the king. Both join foreign wealth and royal homage, without merging the different recipients or foreign groups.

Psalms 44:16
They will be brought with gladness and exultation; they will be led into the king’s sanctuary.
  Psalms 99:2 — Joyful entrance into royal or divine presence: The companions are led with gladness and exultation into the king's sanctuary. Psalm 99 commands service to the Lord with gladness and entrance before Him with exultation. The paired expressions and movement into presence correspond, without identifying the psalm's royal procession as the same event.

Psalms 44:18
They will remember your name in every generation; therefore peoples will thank you for ever and for age upon age.
  Psalms 71:17 — An enduring royal name and the response of peoples: Psalm 44 promises remembrance of the addressee's name through generations and the peoples' enduring thanksgiving. Psalm 71 asks that the king's name remain blessed through the ages and describes all nations calling him blessed. Both connect the royal name's endurance with a response extending beyond one people.

Psalms 45:2
Our God is refuge and strength, a helper in the afflictions that have greatly overtaken us.
  Psalms 9:10 — God as refuge and help in distress: Psalm 45 calls God refuge, strength and help in afflictions. Psalm 9 says the Lord became refuge for the needy and helper at fitting times in distress. The paired descriptions of refuge and aid in distress ground both statements of reliance.

Psalms 45:3
Therefore we will not fear when the earth is disturbed and mountains are displaced into the hearts of seas.
  Psalms 22:4 — Fearlessness grounded in God's presence: Psalm 45's confession of God as refuge leads to refusal to fear even amid upheaval of earth and mountains. Psalm 22 refuses fear even in death's shadow because God is with the speaker. The danger differs, but both expressly connect fearlessness in extreme peril with divine protection and presence.

Psalms 45:4
Their waters roared and were disturbed; the mountains were disturbed at his might. Diapsalma.
  Psalms 92:3-4 — Roaring waters and the voices of rivers: Psalm 45 depicts waters roaring and disturbed, with mountains disturbed 'at his might.' Psalm 92 gives rivers raised voices and calls the sea's surges and the Lord on high wondrous. The audible waters are the particular poetic correspondence; the comparison does not independently resolve Psalm 45's pronoun.

Psalms 45:5
The river’s rushing streams gladden God’s city; the Most High sanctified His dwelling.
  Ezekiel 47:1-9 — A river associated with God's holy dwelling: Psalm 45 joins a river's streams gladdening God's city with the Most High's sanctified dwelling. Ezekiel sees water flowing from the house by the altar and becoming a river that brings health and life. The holy dwelling and beneficial waters are comparable; the psalm does not identify its river as Ezekiel's vision.

Psalms 45:6
God is in her midst; she will not be shaken; God will help her toward morning.
  Psalms 124:1-2 — Divine presence and the unshaken city: Psalm 45 says God is in the city's midst, she will not be shaken and He will help her. Psalm 124 says the one dwelling in Jerusalem will never be shaken and describes the Lord surrounding His people. Divine presence and protection explain the stated stability in both, with different spatial images.

Psalms 45:7
Nations were disturbed; kingdoms bent; He gave His voice; the earth was shaken.
  Psalms 28:8 — The divine voice shaking the earth: Psalm 45 describes God's voice and the earth being shaken amid disturbed nations and kingdoms. Psalm 28 specifically attributes the shaking of the wilderness to the Lord's voice. Both assign the disturbance of a terrestrial place to divine speech.

Psalms 45:8
The Lord of powers is with us; Jacob’s God is our supporter. Diapsalma.
  Psalms 19:2 — Jacob's God as protector: The refrain confesses that Jacob's God is the community's supporter. Psalm 19 asks that the name of Jacob's God protect the addressee in affliction. The particular designation and protective role connect assurance and petition.

Psalms 45:9
Come, see the Lord’s works, the wonders He has set upon the earth.
  Psalms 65:5 — Invitation to come and see God's works: Both verses invite the audience to come and see God's works. Psalm 45 points to wonders on earth, while Psalm 65 describes His counsels concerning humanity as fearsome. The repeated invitation frames divine deeds as grounds for recognition and praise.

Psalms 45:10
Bringing wars to an end as far as the earth’s limits, He will shatter a bow and break a weapon apart and burn shields in fire.
  Hosea 2:20 — God ending war by breaking weapons: Psalm 45 describes God ending wars, shattering the bow, breaking a weapon and burning shields. Hosea promises that God will shatter bow, sword and war from the land. Both directly combine divine action against weapons with the cessation of war, without changing shields into chariots.
  Isaiah 2:4 — War ended through the removal of weapons: Psalm 45 describes wars ended and weapons broken or burned. Isaiah describes nations beating swords into plows and spears into sickles and ceasing to wage war. Both connect peace with weapons losing their military use, while destruction and conversion into tools remain different actions.

Psalms 45:11
“Cease your activity and know that I am God; I will be exalted among the nations, exalted in the earth.”
  Exodus 14:13-14 — Ceasing human action before God's power: Psalm 45 commands the audience to cease activity and know God, following the description of His ending of wars. Moses tells the people to stand and behold divine salvation, for the Lord will fight while they keep silence. Both place human cessation alongside recognition of divine action, without equating the two occasions.

Psalms 45:12
The Lord of powers is with us; Jacob’s God is our supporter.
  Psalms 45:8 — same-chapter protective refrain: The closing statement repeats that the Lord of powers is with the people and Jacob's God is their supporter. Its return after the descriptions of God's works and ended warfare reinforces the assurance stated earlier in the poem.

Psalms 46:2
All nations, clap your hands; shout to God with a voice of exultation.
  Psalms 97:8 — Clapping and exultation in praise: Psalm 46 calls all nations to clap their hands and shout to God with exultation. Psalm 97 personifies rivers clapping and mountains exulting before divine judgment. The paired gestures of praise occur with human peoples and natural features as different subjects.

Psalms 46:3
For the Lord Most High is fearsome, a great King over all the earth.
  Malachi 1:14 — God as great King beyond one nation: Psalm 46 calls the Most High a great King over all the earth. Malachi's divine rebuke declares, 'I am a great King,' and says His name is manifest among nations. The shared royal designation has a scope beyond one people, expressed in praise and rebuke respectively.

Psalms 46:4
He subjected peoples to us and nations beneath our feet.
  Psalms 17:48 — Peoples subjected beneath the speaker: Psalm 46 says God subjected peoples under the community and nations under its feet. Psalm 17 describes God subjecting peoples beneath the individual speaker. The action and spatial expression correspond, while the collective and individual recipients differ.

Psalms 46:5
He chose His inheritance for us, the beauty of Jacob, which He loved. Diapsalma.
  Deuteronomy 4:37-38 — Divine choice, love and inheritance: Psalm 46 says God chose His inheritance for the people, 'the beauty of Jacob, which He loved'. Deuteronomy connects God's love of the fathers with choosing their seed and giving land to inherit. Both join choice, love and an inheritance given to the people, without changing 'His inheritance' into their independent choice.

Psalms 46:6
God ascended with a shout of triumph, the Lord with the sound of a trumpet.
  2 Samuel 6:15 — Ascent accompanied by shout and trumpet: The psalm says God ascended with a shout and the Lord with a trumpet's sound. Samuel describes David and Israel bringing up the Lord's ark with shouting and a trumpet's sound. The ceremonial sound and upward movement correspond; this does not prove that the psalm identifies that precise ark procession.

Psalms 46:8
For God is King of all the earth; make music with understanding.
  1 Corinthians 14:15 — Music and understanding: The psalm commands music with understanding because God is King of all the earth. Paul says he will sing with the spirit and also with the mind. Both expressly join song with intellectual participation, while Paul's surrounding argument specifically concerns intelligibility in the Church.

Psalms 46:9
God reigned over the nations; God sits upon His holy throne.
  Psalms 21:29 — Divine kingship over the nations: Psalm 46 says God reigned over nations and sits on His holy throne. Psalm 21 explains that kingship belongs to the Lord and He is master of the nations. Both explicitly connect divine kingship with authority over nations, not merely one earthly realm.

Psalms 47:1
A psalm of song for the sons of Korah, on the second day of the week.
  Psalms 23:1 — Weekday designations in Psalm headings: Psalm 47's heading specifies the second day of the week, while Psalm 23's specifies the first. The exact weekday notices form a concrete heading comparison; neither a shared author nor an unexpressed performance occasion is inferred.

Psalms 47:2
Great is the Lord and greatly to be praised in our God’s city, His holy mountain,
  Psalms 95:4 — The repeated acclamation of the Lord's greatness: Psalm 47 declares the Lord great and greatly to be praised in His city and holy mountain. Psalm 95 uses the same acclamation before calling Him fearsome above all gods. The repeated praise formula occurs with different following descriptions.

Psalms 47:3
taking good root for the exultation of all the earth. Zion’s mountains, the sides of the north, the city of the great King.
  Matthew 5:35 — Jerusalem as the city of the great King: The psalm calls Zion the city of the great King. Jesus forbids swearing by Jerusalem because it is the city of the great King. The distinctive city designation supplies the connection; the Gospel applies it within teaching about oaths.

Psalms 47:4
God is known in her citadels when He supports her.
  Psalms 45:6 — God's presence supporting the city: Psalm 47 says God is known in her citadels when He supports her. Psalm 45 says God is in the city's midst, preventing its shaking and bringing help. Both connect the city's security with divine presence and support, while keeping citadels and the city as distinct expressions.

Psalms 47:5
For behold, the kings gathered; they came together.
  Psalms 2:2-6 — Gathered rulers, trouble and Zion in distinct scenes: Psalm 47's kings gather and come together; the next unit reports that they saw, marveled, and were troubled and shaken, with Zion named in verse 3. Psalm 2 has earth's kings stand ready and rulers gather against the Lord and His anointed; God then troubles them, and Zion is named. This assembled-rulers, trouble and Zion pattern is a literary comparison: Psalm 47 does not state the kings' purpose or what they saw, and the assemblies are not identified.

Psalms 47:7
Trembling seized them; there were pangs like a woman giving birth.
  Jeremiah 6:24 — Terror compared to childbirth: The psalm describes trembling and pains like those of a woman giving birth. Jeremiah's people report paralyzed hands and affliction seizing them like childbirth pains after hearing of an approaching force. The same bodily comparison expresses fear, without equating the unnamed kings with Jeremiah's people.

Psalms 47:8
With a violent wind Thou wilt shatter the ships of Tarshish.
  Ezekiel 27:26 — A destructive wind and ships at sea: Psalm 47 says God will shatter Tarshish ships with a violent wind. Ezekiel's address to the trading city describes the south wind shattering her in the sea after her rowers led her out. Wind-driven maritime destruction is comparable, but the wind, subject and occasion are not harmonized.

Psalms 47:9
Just as we heard, so we saw in the city of the Lord of powers, in our God’s city; God founded her for ever. Diapsalma.
  Psalms 86:5 — God founding Zion: Psalm 47 says God founded His city forever after describing what was heard and seen there. Psalm 86 names Mother Zion and says the Most High Himself founded her. Both expressly attribute the city's foundation to God, while Psalm 86's verse does not repeat the temporal qualification.

Psalms 47:11
According to Thy name, God, so also Thy praise reaches the earth’s limits; Thy right hand is full of righteousness.
