  Psalms 31:11 — The righteous and upright rejoice in the Lord: Both join rejoicing in the Lord with the righteous and those upright in heart. Psalm 63 adds hope and being praised; Psalm 31 commands exultation and boasting.

Psalms 64:2
A hymn befits Thee, God, in Zion, and unto Thee a vow will be paid in Jerusalem.
  Psalms 115:9-10 — Vows repaid at the Lord's house in Jerusalem: Both locate repayment of a vow in Jerusalem and the Lord's worship. Psalm 115 explicitly places the vow before the people in the house's courts, corresponding to Psalm 64's hymn and vow in Zion and Jerusalem.

Psalms 64:3
Hear my prayer; unto Thee all flesh will come.
  Isaiah 66:23 — All flesh coming before God: The psalm joins its prayer with all flesh coming to God. Isaiah likewise speaks of all flesh coming before Him to bow in Jerusalem, with monthly and Sabbath observance specified there.

Psalms 64:4
Words of lawless deeds overpowered us, and Thou wilt make atonement for our impieties.
  Psalms 78:9 — Atonement and propitiation for communal sins: Psalm 64 says words of lawless deeds overpowered the community and that God will make atonement for its impieties. Psalm 78 asks the saving God to be propitious toward the community's sins. The comparison concerns divine action toward confessed communal wrongdoing.

Psalms 64:5
Blessed is the one Thou didst choose and take to Thyself; he will dwell in Thy courts. We will be filled with the good things of Thy house; holy is Thy sanctuary, wondrous in righteousness.
  Psalms 83:5 — Blessedness of dwelling in God's house: Both pronounce blessed the person or people dwelling in God's courts or house. Psalm 64 stresses divine choice and being filled with the house's good things; Psalm 83 describes the inhabitants' continuing praise.

Psalms 64:6
Hear us, God our Savior, the hope of all earth’s ends and of those far away at sea,
  Isaiah 51:5 — Hope in God's salvation reaches distant nations and islands: The psalm calls God the hope of earth's ends and those far away at sea. Isaiah connects outgoing salvation with nations and islands waiting and hoping in God's arm. Both extend hope beyond the immediate land community.

Psalms 64:7
who prepares mountains in His strength, girded with power;
  Psalms 103:8 — Mountains assigned their place by God: Psalm 64 describes God preparing mountains in His strength. Psalm 103 describes mountains rising to the place He founded for them. Both place the mountains' establishment under God's ordering of the earth.

Psalms 64:8
who troubles the sea’s hollow, the sounds of its waves. The nations will be troubled,
  Isaiah 51:15 — God stirs the sea and its sounding waves: The psalm says God troubles the sea's hollow and its waves' sounds. Isaiah identifies Him as the God who stirs the sea and makes its waves resound. Neither text is rewritten as a statement that He calms the water.

Psalms 64:10
Thou didst visit the earth and make it drunk; Thou didst multiply its enrichment. God’s river was filled with water; Thou didst prepare their food, for thus is Thy preparation.
  Psalms 103:13-14 — Watering the earth produces food: God's water enriches the earth and prepares food in Psalm 64. Psalm 103 joins watering the mountains and filling the earth with the production of plants and bread. The connection is the stated water-to-food provision.

Psalms 64:11
Make its furrows drunk; multiply its produce; in its drops it will rejoice as it sprouts.
  Isaiah 55:10 — Heavenly water makes the soil sprout: The psalm asks for drenched furrows and rejoicing growth in drops of water. Isaiah describes rain and snow drenching the earth, making it sprout and yield seed and food. The literal agricultural process supplies Isaiah's following analogy for God's word.

Psalms 64:12
Thou wilt bless the crown of the year of Thy kindness, and Thy plains will be filled with richness.
  Deuteronomy 11:11-12 — Divine care for rain-fed land throughout the year: The psalm says God will bless the year's crown of kindness and that His plains will be rich. Deuteronomy describes hills and plains drinking heaven's rain under God's care from the year's beginning to its end. Both connect annual divine care with the land's fertility.

Psalms 64:13
The beautiful places of the wilderness will grow rich, and the hills will gird themselves with exultation.
  Isaiah 35:1-2 — The wilderness becomes fruitful and rejoices: Both picture wilderness places transformed into fruitful abundance and exultation. The psalm gives rich wilderness places and rejoicing hills; Isaiah commands the thirsty wilderness to rejoice, exult and flower, then predicts that the Jordan's desert places will flower and exult.

Psalms 65:1
For the end. A song of a psalm. Of resurrection. Raise a shout to God, all the earth!
  Psalms 99:1 — All the earth summoned to shout to God: The running text after Psalm 65's heading summons all the earth to shout to God, matching Psalm 99's summons to shout to the Lord. The heading Of resurrection is preserved without identifying a particular resurrection event.

Psalms 65:2
Make music, then, to His name; give glory to His praise.
  Psalms 95:7-8 — Glory given in praise of God's name: Psalm 65 commands music to God's name and glory to His praise. Psalm 95 addresses the nations' families and commands glory for the Lord's name. The comparison joins the name and the commanded gift of glory.

Psalms 65:3
Say to God, “How fearsome are Thy works! Through the abundance of Thy power, Thine enemies will lie unto Thee.
  Psalms 17:44-45 — Power is accompanied by the adversaries' lying: Psalm 65 says enemies will lie to God through His abundant power. Psalm 17 places foreigners' lying alongside the speaker's appointment over nations and their obedience. The actual language of lying is retained rather than converted into sincere submission.

Psalms 65:4
Let all the earth bow unto Thee and make music unto Thee; let them make music to Thy name.” Diapsalma.
  Psalms 85:9 — Worldwide worship and glorification of God's name: Psalm 65 calls all the earth to bow and make music to God's name. Psalm 85 expects all the nations He made to come, bow before Him and glorify His name. The paired bowing and honoring the name form the correspondence.

Psalms 65:5
Come and see God’s works, fearsome in His counsels concerning the sons of men,
  Psalms 45:9 — The invitation to come and see God's works: Both explicitly invite listeners to come and see the Lord's works. Psalm 45 calls them wonders upon the earth, while Psalm 65 calls His counsels concerning humanity fearsome.

Psalms 65:6
He who turns the sea into dry land; through a river they will pass on foot. There we will rejoice in Him,
  Exodus 14:21-22 — The sea made dry for Israel's passage: The psalm's sea turned into dry land corresponds to Exodus's account of the Lord making the sea dry and Israel entering on dry ground. This connection concerns the sea clause; the psalm's river clause is separately compared with Joshua.
  Joshua 3:17 — Passage on foot through a river: The psalm says they will pass through a river on foot. Joshua describes the priests standing on dry ground in the middle of the Jordan while all Israel crosses. The river-crossing correspondence preserves the psalm's future formulation and is kept distinct from its preceding sea image.

Psalms 65:7
who rules the age by His power; His eyes look upon the nations. Let those provoking Him not exalt themselves within themselves. Diapsalma.
  Psalms 32:13-15 — God observes peoples from His sovereign position: Psalm 65 joins God's rule with His eyes looking upon nations. Psalm 32 describes His heavenly observation of all humanity and understanding of their deeds. Both connect universal divine oversight with human conduct.

Psalms 65:8
Bless our God, nations, and cause the sound of His praise to be heard,
  Psalms 116:1 — Nations and peoples called to praise: Psalm 65 addresses the nations and commands audible blessing of God. Psalm 116 likewise addresses all nations and peoples with a command to praise the Lord. Both broaden the worshipping audience beyond one people.

Psalms 65:9
He who set my soul in life and did not give my feet over to shaking.
  Psalms 55:14 — Life preserved and feet kept from slipping: Both join the preservation of the speaker's life with protection of the feet from slipping or shaking. Psalm 55 explicitly says rescue from death; Psalm 65 says God set the soul in life.

Psalms 65:10
For Thou didst test us, God; Thou didst try us by fire as silver is tried by fire.
  Zechariah 13:9 — God tests His people as silver is refined: Both describe divine testing through fire by comparison with refining silver. Zechariah applies it to a remaining third and adds gold, while the psalm speaks of the praying community without that numerical identification.

Psalms 65:12
Thou madest human beings mount our heads; we passed through fire and water, and Thou broughtest us out to refreshment.
  Isaiah 43:2 — Passing through both water and fire under God's care: The psalm recalls passing through fire and water and emerging into refreshment. Isaiah promises God's presence when crossing water and fire. The two hazards and divine preservation form the parallel without identifying a single historical passage.

Psalms 65:13
I will enter Thy house with whole burnt offerings; I will pay Thee my vows,
  Psalms 115:9-10 — Payment of vows within God's house: The speaker intends to enter God's house and pay vows. Psalm 115 likewise locates intended payment of vows to the Lord in the house's courts before the people. The vow and sanctuary setting are both explicit.

Psalms 65:14
which my lips declared and my mouth spoke in my affliction.
  Jonah 2:3-10 — Distress, prayer and the repayment of vows: Psalm 65 identifies its vows as words spoken in affliction. Jonah's prayer begins with a cry from affliction and concludes with promised repayment of vows. The comparison follows the same distress-to-vow sequence without identifying the two speakers.

Psalms 65:15
I will offer Thee marrow-rich whole burnt offerings, with incense and rams; I will prepare oxen with he-goats for Thee. Diapsalma.
  Leviticus 1:2-3 — Whole burnt offerings and livestock gifts: The psalm names whole burnt offerings alongside rams, oxen and he-goats. Leviticus authorizes gifts from cattle and the flock and specifically describes a cattle whole burnt offering. This supplies a ritual comparison without assigning every animal in the psalm to that offering class or adding its marrow-rich description to the law.

Psalms 65:16
Come, hear, all who fear God, and I will recount all that He did for my soul.
  Psalms 65:5 — Same-chapter movement from seeing divine works to hearing personal testimony: Earlier in this same psalm the audience is invited to come and see God's works. Here those fearing God are invited to come and hear what He did for the speaker's soul. The changed sense and personal focus mark a distinct turn in the invitation.

Psalms 65:18
“If I was regarding injustice in my heart, let the Lord not hear.”
  Isaiah 1:15-17 — Wrongdoing and the refusal of prayer: The psalm asks that the Lord not hear if the speaker was regarding injustice in the heart. Isaiah refuses multiplied supplication from blood-filled hands and commands removal of evil and pursuit of justice. The parallel preserves the psalm's condition rather than declaring it a confession of actual injustice.

Psalms 66:2
May God have compassion on us and bless us; may He make His face shine upon us. Diapsalma.
  Numbers 6:24-26 — Blessing, mercy and the shining divine face: The psalm asks for divine compassion, blessing and a shining face, closely corresponding to the priestly blessing's mercy, blessing and shining face. Numbers also explicitly adds keeping and peace.

Psalms 66:3
That Thy way may be known on earth, Thy salvation among all nations.
  Isaiah 49:6 — Salvation made known beyond Israel to the nations: The psalm seeks knowledge of God's way on earth and salvation among all nations. Isaiah appoints the servant as light for nations to bring salvation to the earth's extremity. The comparison concerns the stated international reach, without naming the psalm's speaker as that servant.

Psalms 66:4
Let peoples give thanks unto Thee, God; let all peoples give thanks unto Thee.
  Psalms 116:1 — All peoples called into thanksgiving: The repeated invitation that all peoples thank God corresponds to Psalm 116's invitation that all peoples and nations praise Him. Both explicitly make every people the addressed worshipping body.

Psalms 66:5
Let nations rejoice and exult, for Thou wilt judge peoples in uprightness and guide nations upon the earth. Diapsalma.
  Psalms 95:10-13 — The nations rejoice before righteous judgment: Psalm 66 joins the nations' gladness with God's upright judgment. Psalm 95 similarly declares judgment of peoples with uprightness and surrounds the coming judgment with creation's rejoicing. Judgment and joy are linked in both passages.

Psalms 66:6
Let peoples give thanks unto Thee, God; let all peoples give thanks unto Thee.
  Psalms 66:4 — Same-chapter refrain around the declaration of just rule: The line repeats verse 4's call for all peoples to thank God, enclosing verse 5's glad nations and upright judgment. This is an explicit structural refrain, not an additional historical event.

Psalms 66:7
The earth gave its fruit; may God, our God, bless us.
  Leviticus 26:3-4 — The land's produce as divine blessing: The psalm couples the earth giving fruit with a request for God's blessing. Leviticus promises rain, the land's produce and trees' fruit within its stated obedience condition. The agricultural blessing is compared without silently adding that condition to the psalm's line.

Psalms 66:8
May God bless us, and let all earth’s ends fear Him.
  Psalms 32:8 — Fear of the Lord extended to the whole earth: Psalm 66 calls for all earth's ends to fear God; Psalm 32 commands all the earth and its inhabitants to fear Him. Both explicitly extend reverence to the entire inhabited world.

Psalms 67:2
Let God arise, and let His enemies be scattered, and let those hating Him flee from His presence.
  Numbers 10:34 — The ark-departure invocation echoed: The psalm's prayer for God to arise, scatter enemies and make His haters flee closely matches Moses' invocation when the ark sets out. The actual supplied Numbers reference is 10:34.

Psalms 67:3
As smoke vanishes, let them vanish; as wax melts before fire, so may sinners perish before God.
  Psalms 36:20 — God's enemies vanish like smoke: Both use vanishing smoke to describe the end of God's enemies. Psalm 67 asks that they vanish; Psalm 36 describes the enemies and sinners vanishing after their exaltation.
  Psalms 96:5 — Melting wax before the divine presence: Psalm 67 compares sinners perishing before God with wax melting before fire. Psalm 96 compares mountains melting before the Lord with wax. The shared melting image is retained while distinguishing the objects that melt.

Psalms 67:4
Let the righteous rejoice and exult before God; let them delight in gladness.
  Psalms 31:11 — Righteous people rejoice in the Lord's presence: Both specifically call the righteous to rejoice and exult in relation to God. Psalm 67 places their gladness before Him; Psalm 31 addresses the righteous and upright in heart.

Psalms 67:5
Sing to God; make music to His name. Make a road for Him who rides upon the west—Lord is His name— and exult before Him. They will be troubled before Him,
  Isaiah 40:3 — Preparing a road for the Lord: Psalm 67 commands a road for the One whose name is Lord. Isaiah commands preparation of the Lord's way and straight paths for God. The road-making correspondence does not replace the psalm's rider upon the west with a different riding image.

Psalms 67:6
the Father of orphans and judge of widows, God in His holy place.
  Deuteronomy 10:18 — God defends orphans and widows: The psalm names God Father of orphans and judge of widows. Deuteronomy explicitly describes His judgment for the orphan and widow. Both assign the protection of these vulnerable groups to God.

Psalms 67:7
God settles those of one disposition in a house, leading out the fettered with courage, likewise the rebellious who dwell in tombs.
  Psalms 145:7 — God releases people in fetters: Psalm 67 describes God leading the fettered out with courage, while Psalm 145 states that the Lord releases those in fetters. Other clauses about a house, one disposition and rebellious tomb-dwellers are not silently reinterpreted through this comparison.

Psalms 67:8
God, when Thou wentest out before Thy people, when Thou didst pass through the wilderness—Diapsalma—
  Exodus 13:20-22 — God precedes His people in the wilderness: The psalm recalls God going before His people through the wilderness. Exodus narrates their wilderness encampment and God's cloud-and-fire guidance before them. The narrative supplies the concrete form of the guidance.

Psalms 67:9
the earth shook, and indeed the heavens dripped before God—this Sinai— before the God of Israel.
  Judges 5:4-5 — Shaking earth, dripping heavens and Sinai: Both poetic recollections join the earth shaking, heavenly water and Sinai before the God of Israel. Judges places the divine departure in Seir and Edom; the psalm preserves its own wilderness sequence.

Psalms 67:10
Thou wilt set apart willing rain, God, for Thine inheritance; it grew weak, but Thou didst restore it.
  Joel 2:23-25 — Rain accompanies restoration after depletion: The psalm joins rain for the weakened inheritance with divine restoration. Joel joins renewed rainfall and full harvests with repayment of years consumed by locusts. The restoration-through-rain pattern is compared without identifying the psalm's weakness as that locust event.

Psalms 67:12
The Lord will give an utterance to those announcing good news with great power,
  Isaiah 52:7 — Divinely given good-news proclamation: Psalm 67 says the Lord will give an utterance to those announcing good news. Isaiah describes the good-news messenger and God's making salvation heard. Both connect good-news proclamation with a divinely supplied utterance or announcement.

Psalms 67:17
Why do you suppose, curdled mountains, that this is the mountain in which God was pleased to dwell? For indeed the Lord will dwell to the end.
  Psalms 131:13-14 — The mountain of God's dwelling and an enduring residence: Psalm 67 questions the curdled mountains about the mountain in which God was pleased to dwell, then affirms that the Lord will dwell to the end. Psalm 131 explicitly says God chose Zion for His enduring dwelling and rest. The comparison preserves the source's question and does not resolve its unnamed mountain's identity.

Psalms 67:18
God’s chariot is ten-thousandfold, thousands of flourishing ones; the Lord is among them, in Sinai, in the holy place.
  Deuteronomy 33:2 — Sinai and the Lord amid a vast company: Psalm 67 joins the Lord at Sinai with a ten-thousandfold chariot and thousands. Deuteronomy likewise places His coming from Sinai with myriads and angels at His right hand. The distinct descriptions of that company are preserved.

Psalms 67:19
Thou didst ascend on high; Thou tookest captivity captive; Thou didst receive gifts among humankind, and indeed the disobedient, to establish a dwelling. Blessed is the Lord God.
  Ephesians 4:8-10 — Ascension and captive-captivity wording with a changed gift action: Ephesians cites ascending on high and taking captivity captive, then interprets the ascent and descent in relation to Christ. The psalm says received gifts among humankind; Ephesians says gave gifts to human beings. That difference is retained rather than harmonized away.

Psalms 67:21
Our God is a God of saving, and to the Lord, the Lord, belong the exits of death.
  Psalms 9:14 — The Lord and escape from death's boundary: Psalm 67 assigns the exits of death to the Lord. Psalm 9 addresses Him as the One raising the speaker from death's gates. The exit and gate images connect divine salvation with departure from death.

Psalms 67:22
Nevertheless God will crush His enemies’ heads, the crown of hair of those walking in their offenses.
  Psalms 109:5-6 — Divine judgment described as crushing heads: Psalm 67 says God will crush His enemies' heads. Psalm 109 describes the Lord crushing kings and heads in judgment among nations. The shared concrete judgment image is retained without identifying their enemies as one historical group.

Psalms 67:24
so that your foot may be dipped in blood, and the tongue of your dogs from enemies, from him.”
  Psalms 57:11 — Blood imagery following the enemies' defeat: Psalm 67 pictures a foot dipped in blood and dogs' tongues in relation to enemies. Psalm 57 pictures a righteous person's hands washed in a sinner's blood after vengeance. The graphic victory images are compared while preserving foot, hand and dog distinctions.

Psalms 67:26
Rulers went first, closely followed by musicians, amid young women beating tambourines.
  Exodus 15:20-21 — Women with tambourines accompany victory praise: The psalm's procession includes young women beating tambourines. Exodus describes Miriam taking a timbrel, the women following with timbrels and dances, and Miriam leading them in song after the sea deliverance. This is a specific musical comparison, not an identification of the psalm's procession with Miriam's occasion.

Psalms 67:27
In assemblies bless God, the Lord, from Israel’s springs.
  Psalms 25:12 — Blessing the Lord in assemblies: Both explicitly locate blessing the Lord in assemblies. Psalm 67 addresses the assembled community from Israel's springs; Psalm 25 states the individual speaker's intention to bless there. No unexpressed interpretation of springs is needed for this link.

Psalms 67:30
From Thy sanctuary over Jerusalem, kings will bring Thee gifts.
  Isaiah 60:3-7 — Foreign royalty and gifts directed toward Jerusalem's worship: The psalm places kings' gifts in relation to God's sanctuary over Jerusalem. Isaiah addresses Jerusalem and joins kings walking by her light with foreign wealth, offerings and the glorified house of prayer. The comparison preserves their distinct descriptions of the visitors.

Psalms 67:31
Rebuke the beasts of the reed, the assembly of bulls among the heifers of peoples, so that those tested by silver may not be shut out; scatter nations that desire wars.
  Psalms 45:10 — God ends the pursuit of war: Psalm 67 asks God to scatter nations that desire wars. Psalm 45 describes Him ending wars to earth's limits and breaking their weapons. The connection rests on the explicit war clause, without identifying the psalm's reed beasts, bulls or silver-tested people.

Psalms 67:32
Envoys will come from Egypt; Ethiopia will hasten its hand toward God.
  Isaiah 45:14-15 — Egypt and Ethiopia in relation to Israel's God: Psalm 67 names Egyptian envoys and Ethiopia hastening its hand toward God. Isaiah names Egypt's toil and the Ethiopians' trade, alongside the Seboin's crossing to an addressed you and acknowledgment that God is in that addressee. The comparison concerns these named nations in relation to Israel's God; the target's addressee and bondage imagery remain distinct from the psalm's Godward gesture and envoys.

Psalms 67:33
Kingdoms of the earth, sing to God; make music to the Lord. Diapsalma.
  Psalms 46:7-9 — Kingdoms sing to the God who rules nations: The psalm addresses earth's kingdoms with commands to sing and make music. Psalm 46 repeats the command to make music and explains that God is King of all earth and reigns over nations. The international kingship supplies the specific scope of the praise.

Psalms 67:34
Make music to God, who rides upon the heaven of heaven toward the east; behold, He will give with His voice a voice of power.
  Psalms 28:3-4 — The divine voice speaks with power: Psalm 67 describes the heavenly rider giving a voice of power. Psalm 28 places the Lord's voice over waters and declares its strength and magnificence. The voice-and-power correspondence does not replace the rider's actual direction toward the east.

Psalms 67:36
Wondrous is God among His holy ones; the God of Israel will give power and strengthening to His people. Blessed is God.
  Psalms 28:11 — God gives strength to His people: Both explicitly promise that God or the Lord gives strength to His people. Psalm 67 adds strengthening and blessing God; Psalm 28 adds His blessing of peace upon the people.

Psalms 68:1
For the end. Concerning those who will be changed. Of David.
  Psalms 44:1 — The distinctive heading concerning those who will be changed: Both headings contain concerning those who will be changed. Psalm 68 names David, while Psalm 44 names the sons of Korah and adds understanding and the beloved. The shared heading is recorded without deciding what change it denotes.

Psalms 68:2
Save me, God, for waters have entered as far as my soul.
  Jonah 2:4-6 — Dangerous waters reach the speaker's life: Psalm 68 cries for rescue because waters reach the soul. Jonah describes sea depths, engulfing waves and water up to his life. The shared drowning lament is compared without locating the psalmist inside Jonah's sea creature.

Psalms 68:3
I was stuck in the mud of the depth, with no firm ground; I entered the sea’s depths, and a tempest drowned me.
  Psalms 39:3 — Mud and the absence or restoration of firm footing: Psalm 68 describes being stuck in deep mud with no firm ground. Psalm 39 recalls rescue from muddy slime followed by feet set upon a rock. The contrasting danger and rescue concern the same mud-and-footing imagery.

Psalms 68:4
I grew weary crying out; my throat became hoarse; my eyes failed from hoping in my God.
  Psalms 118:81-82 — Failing eyes while waiting for divine help: Psalm 68 says the eyes failed from hoping in God. Psalm 118 joins a failing soul and eyes with hope in God's word and a question about comfort. Both portray the bodily strain of prolonged expectation.

Psalms 68:5
More numerous than the hairs on my head became those hating me without cause; my enemies pursuing me unjustly became strong. What I did not plunder, I was then repaying.
  John 15:25 — Without-cause hatred cited in the Gospel: John explicitly cites the scriptural words They hated Me without cause as fulfilled in hostility toward Jesus. Psalm 68 contains that without-cause hatred language; the link does not assert that this wording occurs nowhere else or extend the citation to every statement in the psalm.

Psalms 68:6
God, Thou knewest my folly, and my offenses were not concealed from Thee.
  Psalms 89:8 — Wrongdoing is fully exposed before God: Psalm 68 acknowledges that God knows the speaker's folly and that offenses are not concealed. Psalm 89 places communal lawless deeds before Him. Both confess divine knowledge of wrongdoing, without replacing the latter's actual age wording with secret sins.

Psalms 68:7
May those waiting for Thee not be ashamed because of me, Lord, Lord of powers; may those seeking Thee not be embarrassed because of me, God of Israel,
  Psalms 24:3 — Those waiting for God and the prospect of shame: Psalm 68 asks that those waiting for God not be ashamed because of the speaker. Psalm 24 declares that all who wait for Him will not be ashamed. The community and waiting-not-shame formula correspond, while the source adds concern about the speaker's effect on others.

Psalms 68:8
for on Thine account I endured reproach; embarrassment covered my face.
  Jeremiah 15:15-16 — Reproach endured in relation to God and His words: Psalm 68 says the speaker endured reproach on God's account. Jeremiah likewise asks God to recognize reproach received concerning Him from those rejecting His words. The connection preserves the distinct speakers and their stated relation to God.

Psalms 68:9
I became estranged from my brothers and a foreigner to my mother’s sons,
  Job 19:13-15 — Kinship becomes estrangement: Psalm 68 describes estrangement from brothers and being foreign to the mother's sons. Job describes brothers withdrawing, close people failing to acknowledge him and being a foreigner in the household. Both give concrete family and household alienation.

Psalms 68:10
for zeal for Thy house devoured me, and the reproaches of those reproaching Thee fell upon me.
  John 2:17 — Zeal for God's house remembered concerning Jesus: After Jesus acts in the temple, His disciples remember the written statement about zeal for God's house consuming Him. It corresponds to the psalm's zeal devouring the speaker; the difference between remembered future wording and the psalm's past statement is preserved.
  Romans 15:3 — The reproach clause applied explicitly to Christ: Romans quotes the psalm's reproaches falling upon the speaker and applies it to Christ not pleasing Himself. This is an explicit use of the source's second clause in an argument about bearing others' weaknesses.

Psalms 68:11
I bowed my soul down in fasting, and it became a reproach to me.
  Psalms 34:13 — The soul humbled in fasting amid hostility: Psalm 68 says the speaker bowed the soul down in fasting and received reproach. Psalm 34 describes sackcloth and humbling the soul with fasting when others troubled him. Both connect fasting and the humbled soul with hostile treatment.

Psalms 68:12
I made sackcloth my garment, and became a proverb to them.
  Job 30:9 — The sufferer becomes other people's repeated tale: Psalm 68 says the speaker became a proverb to others. Job describes himself as their lyre and their repeated tale. The comparison concerns a person's suffering becoming material for others' speech and ridicule, not a shared sackcloth detail.

Psalms 68:13
Those sitting in the gate talked against me, and wine-drinkers sang about me.
  Lamentations 3:14 — The sufferer made into a mocking song: Psalm 68 says wine-drinkers sang about the speaker. Lamentations calls its speaker a laughingstock and the people's psalm all day. Both turn personal distress into the community's mocking song, with different groups named.

Psalms 68:14
But I, in my prayer, turn toward Thee, Lord; a time of favor, God, in the abundance of Thy mercy. Hear me in the truth of Thy salvation.
  Isaiah 49:8 — Prayer and hearing at a time of favor: Psalm 68 turns toward God at a time of favor and asks to be heard in salvation. Isaiah speaks of hearing at an acceptable time and help on a day of salvation. The petition and divine assurance share this time-and-salvation formulation.

Psalms 68:15
Save me from clay so that I may not become stuck; may I be rescued from those hating me and from the depth of waters.
  Psalms 68:3 — Same-chapter petition answers the opening mud-and-water lament: The later request to be saved from clay and watery depth answers the earlier description of being stuck in mud without firm ground and entering sea depths. The repeated concrete images connect the lament's description to its explicit petition.

Psalms 68:16
Let no tempest of water drown me, nor the depth swallow me; let no well close its mouth over me.
  Lamentations 3:53-55 — The closing pit and engulfing water threaten life: Psalm 68 asks that water not drown the speaker and a well not close its mouth over him. Lamentations describes life in a pit under a stone, water over the head and a cry from the lowest pit. The confinement and drowning images supply the parallel without equating historical events.

Psalms 68:17
Hear me, Lord, for Thy mercy is kind; according to Thine abundant compassions, look upon me.
  Psalms 50:3 — Appeal to the abundance of divine compassions: Both address God through His mercy and abundance of compassions. Psalm 68 asks for hearing and a favorable look; Psalm 50 asks for the blotting out of wrongdoing. The shared appeal is retained with those different requests.

Psalms 68:18
Do not turn Thy face away from Thy servant; for I am afflicted, hear me quickly.
  Psalms 101:3 — The afflicted ask for an unturned face and a quick hearing: Both ask God not to turn His face away and to hear quickly in affliction. The combination of face, distress and urgent hearing is explicit in both passages.

Psalms 68:21
My soul expected reproach and misery; I waited for someone to grieve with me, but there was none, for comforters, but found none.
  Lamentations 1:2 — No comforter is found in grief: The psalmist looks for someone to grieve and comfort but finds none. Lamentations describes weeping Jerusalem without a comforter among those who love her. The contrast between expected companions and absent consolation is shared, while the latter personifies a city.

Psalms 68:22
They gave gall for my food, and for my thirst made me drink sour wine.
  Matthew 27:34 — Gall offered to the sufferer: The psalm names gall given for food; Matthew describes wine mixed with gall offered to Jesus before crucifixion. The gall correspondence is explicit, while Matthew's drink and Jesus' refusal are not substituted for the source's food clause.
  John 19:28-30 — Thirst and sour wine in the Passion: John links Jesus' stated thirst with Scripture's completion and the sour wine brought to His mouth. This corresponds to the psalm's sour wine given for thirst, without claiming John explicitly names this psalm in the passage.

Psalms 68:23
Let their table before them become a trap, a repayment and a stumbling block.
  Romans 11:9 — The table-as-trap curse quoted as David's words: Romans attributes to David the table becoming a snare, trap, stumbling block and repayment. The psalm supplies the table, trap, repayment and stumbling-block clause; Romans adds snare in its quoted form.

Psalms 68:24
Let their eyes be darkened so they cannot see, and bend their back down continually.
  Romans 11:10 — Darkened eyes and the continually bent back quoted: Romans repeats the request for eyes darkened so as not to see and a continually bent back. This directly corresponds to both bodily images in the source unit.

Psalms 68:25
Pour Thine anger upon them; may the fury of Thine anger seize them.
  Jeremiah 10:25 — Divine fury poured on oppressors: The psalm asks that God's anger be poured upon its adversaries. Jeremiah likewise asks for fury poured upon nations and generations that consumed Jacob. The shared pouring-of-fury request is distinguished from the different descriptions of its recipients.

Psalms 68:26
Let their homestead become deserted; let no one dwell in their dwellings.
  Acts 1:20 — The deserted dwelling clause applied to Judas: Acts quotes a deserted dwelling with no inhabitant in Peter's discussion of Judas. The psalm uses their homestead and dwellings, while Acts uses his dwelling. The singular application is preserved alongside the source's plural wording.

Psalms 68:27
For they persecuted the one Thou didst strike, and added to the pain of Thy wounded.
  Zechariah 1:15 — Adversaries add injury after God has acted: Psalm 68 accuses opponents of persecuting the one God struck and adding pain to His wounded. Zechariah condemns nations that joined in attack to bring evils when God was a little angry. Both distinguish God's preceding action or anger from the adversaries' additional assault.

Psalms 68:29
Let them be erased from the book of the living, and let them not be written with the righteous.
  Exodus 32:32-33 — Erasure from God's book: The psalm asks that adversaries be erased from the book of the living and not recorded with the righteous. Exodus contains Moses' request to be blotted from God's book and God's response about blotting out sinners. The book-and-erasure language is shared without equating Moses with the psalm's adversaries.

Psalms 68:32
and it will please God more than a young calf bringing forth horns and hoofs.
  Psalms 49:13-14 — Praise preferred to animal offerings: The preceding song of praise is said to please God more than a horned, hoofed calf. Psalm 49 questions eating bulls' flesh and drinking goats' blood and commands a sacrifice of praise. Both distinguish praise from the material animals offered.

Psalms 68:33
Let the poor see and rejoice; seek God, and your soul will live,
  Amos 5:4-6 — Seeking God is joined to living: The psalm calls people to seek God so their soul will live. Amos explicitly repeats Seek Me and you will live and Seek the Lord and live. The command and promised life closely correspond.

Psalms 68:34
for the Lord heard the needy and did not despise His bound ones.
  Psalms 101:18-21 — God hears the lowly and those in bonds: Psalm 68 joins hearing the needy with not despising His bound ones. Psalm 101 joins regard for the lowly's prayer with hearing those in bonds. Both connect divine attention to the needy or lowly and to those in bonds.

Psalms 68:35
Let the heavens and earth praise Him, the sea and everything creeping in them.
  Psalms 148:7-10 — Earth, watery depths and creeping creatures summoned to praise: Psalm 68 calls the heavens, earth, sea and everything creeping in them to praise. Psalm 148 calls the earth, deeps and creeping things into its creation-wide praise. The sea/deeps and creature groups form the particular comparison.

Psalms 68:36
For God will save Zion, and Judea’s cities will be built; they will dwell there and inherit it.
  Isaiah 44:26 — Jerusalem and Judea's cities rebuilt and inhabited: Psalm 68 anticipates Zion's salvation, the building of Judea's cities and renewed dwelling. Isaiah likewise promises inhabited Jerusalem and rebuilt cities of Judea. The named city region and restoration of habitation correspond.

Psalms 68:37
His servants’ offspring will possess it, and those loving His name will dwell in it.
  Isaiah 65:9 — God's servants and offspring inherit and dwell: The psalm promises possession to the servants' offspring and dwelling to those loving God's name. Isaiah promises inheritance and dwelling for offspring from Jacob and Judah, the chosen and God's slaves. Both join descent, service, inheritance and residence.

Psalms 69:1
For the end. Of David. For remembrance,
  Psalms 37:1 — The heading for remembrance: Both headings name David and include for remembrance. Psalm 37 additionally specifies the Sabbath; Psalm 69's following source unit continues with saving and help. No particular commemorated event is assumed.

Psalms 69:2
for the Lord to save me. God, attend to my help!
  Psalms 39:14 — The shared opening plea of a repeated closing passage: Psalm 69's plea for God to attend to help corresponds to Psalm 39's request that the Lord attend to helping and rescue. It begins a five-unit sequence in Psalm 69 that closely parallels Psalm 39's closing five units.

Psalms 69:3
May those seeking my soul be ashamed and confounded; may those wishing me evil turn backward and be disgraced.
  Psalms 39:15 — Pursuers shamed and turned backward in corresponding clauses: Both ask that those seeking the speaker's soul be ashamed and that those wishing evil turn backward in disgrace. This is the next corresponding unit in the two passages' parallel sequence.

Psalms 69:4
May those saying to me, “Well! Well!” turn back immediately in shame.
  Psalms 39:16 — The taunting Well response reversed into shame: Both connect an immediate reversal into shame with people uttering a doubled Well or Well done taunt. The precise taunt wording differs slightly and is retained as supplied.

Psalms 69:5
Let all who seek Thee exult and be glad in Thee; let those who love Thy salvation always say, “Let God be magnified!”
  Psalms 39:17 — Seekers rejoice and salvation-lovers magnify God: Both join rejoicing seekers of God with those loving His salvation and their continuing call that God or the Lord be magnified. The sequence and paired groups closely correspond.

Psalms 69:6
But I am poor and needy; God, help me! Thou art my helper and my rescuer; Lord, do not delay!
  Psalms 39:18 — The poor speaker names God helper and asks Him not to delay: Both close with the speaker poor and needy, God as helper, and the request not to delay. Psalm 69 says rescuer and asks for help; Psalm 39 says defender and declares the Lord's care.

Psalms 70:1
Of David; of the sons of Jonadab and of those first taken captive. God, in Thee I have hoped; may I never be disgraced.
  Psalms 30:2 — Hope in God and the plea never to be disgraced: The running prayer in Psalm 70 closely matches Psalm 30's opening hope in the Lord and request never to be put to shame. This link does not resolve Psalm 70's additional Jonadab-and-captivity heading.

Psalms 70:2
In Thy righteousness rescue me and bring me out; bend Thine ear toward me and save me.
  Psalms 30:2-3 — Righteous rescue and the inclining ear: Both couple rescue in God's righteousness with His inclining an ear to the speaker. Psalm 70 gathers in one unit wording spread over the two Psalm 30 units.

Psalms 70:3
Become for me a defending God and a fortified place to save me, for Thou art my firm support and my refuge.
  Psalms 30:3-4 — God as a defending place of refuge: Both ask for or declare God as a protecting refuge that saves the speaker. Psalm 30 joins protecting God, house of refuge and stronghold; Psalm 70 joins defending God, fortified place and firm support.

Psalms 70:4
My God, rescue me from a sinner’s hand, from the hand of one acting lawlessly and unjustly.
  Jeremiah 15:21 — Rescue from the hand of wrongdoers: Psalm 70 asks rescue from the hand of a sinner and one acting lawlessly. Jeremiah records God's promise to rescue from the hand of evil people and ransom from pestilent people. The repeated hand-of-wrongdoers formulation connects petition and assurance.

Psalms 70:6
Upon Thee I was supported from the womb; from my mother’s belly Thou art the One who shelters me. My hymn is continually in Thee.
  Psalms 21:10-11 — Divine support beginning in the mother's womb: Both describe dependence upon God from the womb and mother's belly. Psalm 70 says supported and sheltered; Psalm 21 says drawn out and cast upon God. The birth-stage dependence is explicit without identifying the speakers.

Psalms 70:9
Do not cast me away at the time of old age; when my strength fails, do not forsake me.
  Isaiah 46:3-4 — God's care sought into old age: The psalm asks not to be cast away or forsaken when age weakens the speaker. Isaiah addresses those carried from the belly and promises divine bearing, rescue and salvation into advanced age. The whole-life care provides a particular answer-pattern to the petition.

Psalms 70:10
For my enemies spoke to me, and those watching my soul plotted together,
  Psalms 30:14 — Enemies gather and plan against the speaker's life: Psalm 70 describes enemies watching the soul and plotting together. Psalm 30 describes people gathering against the speaker and planning to take his soul. The shared collective conspiracy is explicit.

Psalms 70:11
saying, “God has forsaken him! Pursue him and overtake him, for there is no rescuer!”
  Psalms 3:3 — The enemy taunt denies rescue from God: Psalm 70's enemies declare God has forsaken the speaker and that there is no rescuer. Psalm 3 reports the claim that there is no salvation for him in his God. Neither hostile assertion is treated as the narrator's judgment.

Psalms 70:12
God, do not go far from me; my God, attend to my help!
  Psalms 21:20 — Distance and the plea to attend to help: Psalm 70 asks God not to go far from the speaker and to attend to his help. Psalm 21 asks the Lord not to put the speaker's help far away and to attend to his support. The paired distance-and-attending clauses supply the correspondence while preserving their different objects.

Psalms 70:13
Let those slandering my soul be ashamed and fail; let those seeking evil for me wrap themselves in shame and confusion.
  Psalms 108:29 — Slanderers clothed in shame: Psalm 70 asks for shame for slanderers and for evil-seekers to wrap themselves in shame and confusion. Psalm 108 asks that slanderers wear humiliation and wrap shame like a double cloak. Both picture hostile people covered by shame, while the source's two clauses remain distinct.

Psalms 70:15
My mouth will proclaim Thy righteousness, Thy salvation all day, for I did not know literary learning.
  Acts 4:12-13 — Proclaiming salvation without literary learning: The psalmist proclaims divine salvation while saying he did not know literary learning. Acts places the proclamation of salvation alongside recognition that Peter and John were unlettered ordinary men. The comparison concerns proclamation and learning, without identifying the psalmist as either apostle.

Psalms 70:18
And until old age and advanced years, God, do not forsake me, until I announce Thine arm to the whole coming generation, Thy power and Thy righteousness—
  Psalms 77:4-7 — Divine mighty acts announced to the coming generation: Psalm 70 asks not to be forsaken until God's arm and power are announced to the coming generation. Psalm 77 describes transmission of His mighty acts to children yet to be born so they will hope in Him. The intergenerational purpose is explicit in both; the source's righteousness clause continues into the following unit.

Psalms 70:19
God, up to the highest—what great things Thou hast done! God, who is like Thee?
  Exodus 15:11 — Who is like God who does great wonders: The psalm's question Who is like Thee accompanies God's great acts. Exodus asks the same incomparable-God question and describes Him working wonders. The question and divine acts occur together in both.

Psalms 70:20
How many troubles, many and grievous, Thou didst show me! And turning back, Thou madest me live, and from the depths of the earth Thou broughtest me up again.
  1 Samuel 2:6 — God makes alive and brings up from below: The psalm recalls renewed life and being brought up from earth's depths after grievous troubles. Hannah's song declares that the Lord gives life and brings up from Hades. The rescue images correspond without turning the psalm's earth-depths language into an unqualified account of bodily resurrection.

Psalms 70:21
Thou didst increase Thy greatness, and turning back, didst comfort me, and from the depths of the earth Thou broughtest me up again.
  Psalms 85:13 — Deliverance from the lowest depth: Psalm 70 repeats being brought up from earth's depths, now alongside comfort. Psalm 85 names deliverance of the soul from lowest Hades. The upward rescue corresponds while earth's depths and Hades remain the distinct supplied expressions.

Psalms 70:22
For I too will acknowledge unto Thee Thy truth with a musical instrument, God; I will sing unto Thee with a lyre, Holy One of Israel.
  Psalms 32:2-4 — Instrumental praise of the faithful God: Psalm 70 joins acknowledging God's truth with instruments and a lyre. Psalm 32 calls for lyre and psaltery praise and grounds it in the upright word and faithful works of the Lord. Both connect instrumental praise with divine truth or faithfulness.

Psalms 70:23
My lips will exult when I sing unto Thee, and my soul, which Thou didst redeem.
  Isaiah 35:9-10 — The redeemed rejoice in praise: The psalm's redeemed soul and singing lips exult. Isaiah places the redeemed on the holy way and describes returning people with praise, exultation and gladness. Individual and communal portrayals share redemption expressed in joyful praise.

Psalms 70:24
And my tongue too will meditate on Thy righteousness all day, when those seeking evil for me become ashamed and confounded.
  Psalms 34:26-28 — The tongue praises righteousness after the adversaries' shame: Psalm 70 joins opponents' shame with the tongue's all-day meditation on God's righteousness. Psalm 34 moves from enemies' shame and humiliation to the same tongue, righteousness and all-day praise wording.

Psalms 71:1
For Solomon. God, give Thy judgment to the king, and Thy righteousness to the king’s son,
  1 Kings 3:6-12 — Solomon asks for the ability to judge God's people: The psalm's title names Solomon and asks God to give judgment and righteousness to the king and king's son. First Kings recounts David's son Solomon requesting a hearing heart to judge the people in righteousness and God's answer. The link does not settle every later royal image in the psalm.

Psalms 71:2
to judge Thy people in righteousness, and Thy poor in justice.
  Isaiah 11:4-5 — Royal judgment attends to the lowly and righteousness: Psalm 71 asks for righteous judgment of God's people and justice for the poor. Isaiah's shoot from Jesse judges the cause of the lowly and wears righteousness. The comparison concerns the stated character of rule, without identifying the two portraits solely from this unit.

Psalms 71:4
He will judge the poor of the people, and save the children of the needy, and humble the false accuser,
  Job 29:16-17 — The vulnerable defended against unjust aggressors: Psalm 71 describes saving the needy's children and humbling the false accuser. Job recalls fatherly care for the powerless, investigating cases and taking plunder from unjust people's teeth. This is a concrete claimed practice of protective justice, not a claim that Job is the king in the psalm.

Psalms 71:5
and he will endure alongside the sun, and before the moon, through generations of generations.
  Psalms 88:36-38 — Royal endurance measured by sun and moon: Psalm 71 describes the king enduring alongside sun and moon through generations. Psalm 88's covenant speech about David describes enduring seed and a throne like sun and moon. The astronomical comparison of royal duration is shared.

Psalms 71:6
And he will descend like rain upon a fleece, and like drops dripping upon the earth.
  Judges 6:37-40 — Moisture on a fleece and the ground: The psalm compares the king's descent to rain upon a fleece and drops upon the earth. Gideon's signs distinguish dew on the fleece with dry ground, then a dry fleece with dew on the ground. The particular fleece-moisture-ground imagery corresponds, while rain and dew, a royal comparison and requested signs remain distinct. A historical allusion or identical occasion is not established.

Psalms 71:7
Righteousness will arise in his days, and abundant peace until the moon is taken away.
  Isaiah 9:5-6 — Righteous rule with enduring peace: Psalm 71 joins the rise of righteousness with abundant peace in the king's days. Isaiah joins the child's rule and limitless peace on David's throne with righteousness and judgment. The supplied Isaiah numbering is 9:5-6.

Psalms 71:8
And he will rule from sea to sea, and from the river to the ends of the inhabited world.
  Zechariah 9:9-10 — The coming king's rule expressed through seas and rivers: Psalm 71 extends royal rule from sea to sea and river to the inhabited world's ends. Zechariah describes the coming king ruling waters as far as the sea and earth's river outlets. The maritime reach corresponds, but Zechariah is not falsely quoted as containing the psalm's identical sea-to-sea formula.

Psalms 71:9
Ethiopians will fall before him, and his enemies will lick dust.
  Isaiah 49:23 — Subjection pictured as bowing and licking dust: Psalm 71 describes Ethiopians falling before the king and enemies licking dust. Isaiah pictures kings and ruling women bowing to Zion and licking the dust of her feet. The bodily image corresponds while the groups and recipient of homage remain distinct.

Psalms 71:10
kings of Tarshish and the islands will offer gifts; kings of the Arabians and Saba will bring gifts.
  2 Chronicles 9:1-9 — Royal gifts from Saba to Solomon: The psalm anticipates gifts from kings including Saba, under a title naming Solomon. Chronicles recounts Saba's queen bringing and giving gold, spices and stones to Solomon. This illustrates royal gifts from the named region without claiming the single embassy exhausts the psalm's broader language.

Psalms 71:11
And all kings will bow before him; all nations will serve him.
  Daniel 7:13-14 — Worldwide homage accompanies enduring royal authority: Psalm 71 describes all kings bowing and all nations serving the ruler. Daniel's vision gives authority to the one like a son of man and describes all earth's nations worshipping him. The universal homage corresponds without importing a different version of Daniel's vision.

Psalms 71:12
For he rescued a poor person from a powerful man’s hand, and a needy person who had no helper.
  Job 29:12 — Rescue of the poor from a powerful person's hand: Both use the specific rescue of a poor person from a powerful man's hand and help for one lacking a helper. The psalm names the needy; Job names an orphan in its second clause.

Psalms 71:14
From interest-taking and injustice he will redeem their souls, and their name is honored before him.
  Nehemiah 5:7-11 — Redemption of vulnerable people amid financial exaction: The psalm names redemption from interest-taking and injustice. Nehemiah combines purchase of Jewish brothers sold to nations with rebuke of demands against brothers and relinquishment of exaction. This compares economic rescue without asserting that Nehemiah explicitly uses the psalm's interest-taking term.

Psalms 71:15
And he will live, and some of Arabia’s gold will be given to him; and they will pray concerning him continually, and bless him all day.
  2 Chronicles 9:13-14 — Arabia's gold brought to Solomon: Psalm 71 anticipates Arabia's gold being given to the ruler. Chronicles explicitly records Arabia's kings bringing gold and silver to Solomon. The named region, gift metal and royal recipient supply the connection; the psalm's prayers are not attributed to that narrative.

Psalms 71:16
There will be a firm support in the land upon the mountain summits; his fruit will be raised far above Lebanon, and they will blossom from the city like the earth’s grass.
  Hosea 14:6-8 — Communal flourishing pictured through Lebanon and fruitful growth: Psalm 71 describes raised fruit beyond Lebanon and people blossoming like grass. Hosea describes Israel blossoming, rooted and fragrant like Lebanon, with spreading branches and fruit. The related human-growth imagery is retained without rewriting Psalm 71's firm support as an absent grain clause.

Psalms 71:17
Let his name be blessed through the ages; before the sun his name will endure. And all the tribes of the earth will be blessed in him; all nations will call him blessed.
  Genesis 22:18 — Blessing extending to all earth's nations: The psalm describes all tribes blessed in the ruler and all nations calling him blessed. Genesis promises all earth's nations blessed in Abraham's seed. The shared worldwide blessing formulation is compared without reducing that seed to a single identification from this verse alone.

Psalms 71:18
Blessed be the Lord God, the God of Israel, who alone does wonders!
  Psalms 85:10 — God alone works wonders: Both combine God's uniqueness with His doing wonders. Psalm 71 blesses the God of Israel who alone does wonders; Psalm 85 says He does wondrous things and alone is the great God.

Psalms 71:19
And blessed be His glorious name for the age and for the age of the age; and the whole earth will be filled with His glory. May it be! May it be!
  Numbers 14:21 — The whole earth filled with the Lord's glory: Psalm 71 declares that the whole earth will be filled with God's glory and closes with May it be. Numbers states in the Lord's oath that His glory shall fill all the earth. The same worldwide filling by glory occurs in a doxology and in divine speech.

Psalms 71:20
The hymns of David son of Jesse have ended.
  Psalms 85:1 — A hymn-collection ending alongside a later prayer for David: The notice says the hymns of David son of Jesse have ended here. A later psalm is nevertheless headed A prayer for David. This compares the collection notice with the later heading without claiming that for David asserts Davidic authorship or that every David-related text ends at Psalm 71.

Psalms 72:1
A psalm of Asaph. How good God is to Israel, to those upright in heart!
  Psalms 124:4 — Shared divine-goodness formula: The declaration that God is good to those upright in heart corresponds to the prayer that the Lord do good to the good and upright in heart; one affirms what the other requests.

Psalms 72:2
But my feet were almost shaken; my steps were nearly poured out.
  Psalms 93:18 — Unsteady-foot imagery: The speaker's almost-shaken feet correspond to the confession that a foot has been shaken and the Lord's mercy helps. Both place endangered footing within personal dependence on God.

Psalms 72:3
For I envied the lawless, beholding sinners’ peace,
  Psalms 36:1 — Confession and corrective instruction: The speaker admits envying lawless people when seeing their peace; the other psalm expressly forbids envy of evildoers and workers of lawlessness.

Psalms 72:5
They are not in human toils, and they will not be scourged with humans.
  Job 21:9 — Prosperity of the ungodly: The claim that sinners escape human scourging corresponds to Job's complaint that the ungodly have flourishing houses without the Lord's scourge. This compares the complaints without treating prosperity as moral approval.

Psalms 72:6
Therefore arrogance seized them; they wrapped themselves in their injustice and ungodliness.
  Psalms 108:18 — Clothing imagery for injustice and curse: Arrogant people wrap themselves in injustice and ungodliness here; Psalm108 pictures a curse worn like a garment. Both make harmful moral conditions into clothing, while naming different conditions.

Psalms 72:11
And they said, “How did God know? And is there knowledge in the Most High?”
  Psalms 93:7-10 — Denial of divine knowledge: The sinners ask whether the Most High knows; Psalm93 reports the claim that the Lord neither sees nor understands and answers it through God's making human faculties of perception.

Psalms 72:12
Behold, these are sinners, and they prosper; for the age they took possession of wealth.
  Job 21:7 — Prosperous sinners: Both complaints explicitly place ungodly people alongside continued life or prosperity and accumulated wealth, challenging an uncomplicated equation between present riches and righteousness.

Psalms 72:13
And I said, “Then in vain I made my heart righteous and washed my hands among the innocent;
  Psalms 25:6 — Shared innocence and washing language: Washing hands among the innocent repeats the other psalm's wording. Here the speaker questions its value; there washing accompanies approaching the Lord's altar.
  Malachi 3:14-15 — Complaint that fidelity is futile: The speaker calls his righteous conduct vain while observing prosperous sinners. Malachi reports service to God called futile, outsiders called blessed, and lawbreakers being built up. These distinct clauses support the comparison without merging their subjects.

Psalms 72:16
And I undertook to understand this; it is toil before me,
  Ecclesiastes 8:16-17 — Toil of understanding providence: Trying to understand the apparent prosperity of sinners becomes toil for the psalmist. Ecclesiastes likewise joins the effort to understand God's works with toil and the limits of human discovery.

Psalms 72:18
Nevertheless, because of their deceits Thou didst set things for them; Thou didst cast them down when they were lifted up.
  Luke 1:51-52 — Reversal of the elevated: The psalm describes those lifted up being cast down; Mary's song describes proud people scattered and rulers brought down from thrones. The comparison preserves the different groups and settings.

Psalms 72:19
How they became desolate suddenly! They failed; they perished through their lawlessness.
  Job 20:7 — Sudden loss of apparent security: The sinners' sudden desolation corresponds to Zophar's description of the ungodly perishing when they think themselves established. This compares the stated reversal without endorsing Zophar's application to Job.

Psalms 72:20
Like the dream of someone awakening, Lord, in Thy city Thou wilt count their image as nothing.
  Job 20:8 — Vanishing-dream image: The sinners' image becomes worthless like a dream on awakening; Zophar likewise pictures the ungodly disappearing like a dream or nighttime apparition. The shared image does not establish his accusation against Job.
  Isaiah 29:7-8 — Dream and awakening at Zion: Both passages compare threatening wealth or appearances with dreams emptied of substance on waking. Isaiah explicitly describes nations attacking Jerusalem, while the psalm places the divine dismissal in God's city.

Psalms 72:22
and I was counted as nothing and did not understand; I became beastlike beside Thee.
  Psalms 48:21 — Human incomprehension likened to beasts: The speaker admits not understanding and becoming beastlike beside God; Psalm48 compares an honored but uncomprehending person to senseless beasts. The self-confession and general warning retain their different scopes.

Psalms 72:23
And I am continually with Thee; Thou didst grasp my right hand.
  Isaiah 41:13 — God grasping the right hand: The testimony that God grasped the speaker's right hand corresponds to God's promise to hold Israel's right hand and tell it not to fear.

Psalms 72:24
In Thy counsel Thou didst guide me, and with glory Thou didst receive me.
  Psalms 48:16 — Reception by God amid the wealth question: The speaker says God received him with glory; Psalm48 anticipates God's receiving and redeeming the soul from Hades. Both occur in reflections on riches and mortality, with different temporal wording.

Psalms 72:26
My heart and my flesh failed; the God of my heart and my portion is God for ever.
  Psalms 15:5 — God as one's portion: The failing speaker identifies God as his enduring portion; Psalm15 explicitly calls the Lord the portion of the speaker's inheritance and cup. The shared possession language concerns God Himself.

Psalms 72:27
For behold, those distancing themselves from Thee will perish; Thou didst destroy everyone who goes whoring away from Thee.
  Hosea 1:2 — Unfaithfulness as departing prostitution: Going whoring away from God corresponds to Hosea's description of the land prostituting itself away from following the Lord. Both use marital infidelity to describe religious departure.

Psalms 72:28
But for me, clinging to God is good, to set my hope in the Lord, to proclaim all Thy praises in the gates of the daughter of Zion.
  Psalms 9:15 — Shared proclamation at Zion's gates: The resolution to proclaim all God's praises in the gates of daughter Zion repeats the same specific proclamation and location in Psalm9.

Psalms 73:1
Of understanding. Of Asaph. Why, God, didst Thou reject to the end? Why was Thy wrath angry against the sheep of Thy pasture?
  Psalms 99:3 — Israel as God's pasture flock: The lament addresses divine anger against the sheep of God's pasture; Psalm99 identifies the community as His people and the sheep of His pasture, supplying the same communal flock description.

Psalms 73:2
Remember Thy congregation, which Thou didst acquire from the beginning; Thou didst redeem the rod of Thine inheritance, this Mount Zion, where Thou didst encamp.
  Exodus 15:16-17 — Acquired people and sanctuary mountain: The prayer recalls God's acquired and redeemed congregation and His dwelling at Zion. Exodus15 combines the purchased people, the mountain of inheritance and God's prepared sanctuary.

Psalms 73:3
Raise Thy hands against their arrogance to the end, for all the evil the enemy did in Thy holy places.
  Lamentations 2:7 — Enemy action in the sanctuary: The plea focuses on evil done by an enemy in God's holy places; Lamentations describes the sanctuary's rejection and an enemy's shout within the Lord's house. The parallel does not settle the psalm's date.

Psalms 73:4
And those hating Thee boasted within Thy feast; they set their signs as signs, and did not understand.
  Lamentations 2:7 — Enemy noise in a sacred feast setting: Enemies boast inside God's feast here; Lamentations depicts a shout in the Lord's house as on a festival day. The correspondence concerns hostile occupation of a sacred celebration setting.

Psalms 73:6
as in a forest of trees, with axes they cut out her doors; together with axe and stone-cutting tool they tore her down.
  1 Maccabees 4:38 — Damaged sanctuary entrances: The psalm describes doors cut down with tools; Maccabees describes burned gates and demolished sanctuary chambers. These are concrete sanctuary-ruin parallels, with different destructive methods and no asserted single occasion.

Psalms 73:7
They burned Thy sanctuary with fire; they profaned the tent of Thy name to the ground.
  2 Kings 25:8-10 — Burning of the sanctuary: The complaint that enemies burned God's sanctuary corresponds to the narrative of the Babylonian chief cook setting the Lord's house and Jerusalem's houses on fire. This is a historical counterpart, not a dating claim.

Psalms 73:8
Their kindred said in their hearts together, “Come, let us burn all God’s feasts from the earth!”
  Lamentations 2:6 — Destruction of sacred festivals: The enemy wishes to burn God's feasts from the earth; Lamentations laments the destruction of festival and Sabbath in Zion. The agents and the wish-versus-description forms remain distinct.

Psalms 73:9
We did not see our signs; there is no prophet any longer, and he will know us no longer.
  Lamentations 2:9 — Loss of prophetic guidance: The absence of a prophet or signs corresponds to the lament that Zion's prophets saw no vision from the Lord. The texts describe deprivation of prophetic guidance without supplying an identical historical date.

Psalms 73:10
How long, God, will the enemy reproach? Will the adversary provoke Thy name to the end?
  2 Kings 19:22-23 — Hostile reproach directed at God: The psalm asks how long the adversary will reproach God's name; the oracle against the Assyrian king identifies his reproach and blasphemy as directed against Israel's Holy One.

Psalms 73:11
Why dost Thou turn away Thy hand and Thy right hand from within Thy bosom to the end?
  Lamentations 2:3 — Divine right hand turned away: The psalm asks why God turns away His hand and right hand; Lamentations says He turned back His right hand before the enemy. Both use a turned-hand image in sanctuary-devastation laments, without normalizing the psalm's bosom clause.

Psalms 73:12
But God is our King before the age; He worked salvation in the midst of the earth.
  Exodus 15:13-18 — Ancient saving kingship: The confession of God's ancient kingship and saving work corresponds to the sea song's account of redeemed people led to the sanctuary and its affirmation that the Lord reigns for ever.

Psalms 73:13
Thou didst strengthen the sea by Thy power; Thou didst crush the dragons’ heads upon the water.
  Isaiah 27:1 — Divine defeat of dragons: The psalm depicts God crushing dragons' heads on water; Isaiah depicts God killing a dragon with His powerful sword. The comparison concerns divine victory over dragon imagery, without identifying each creature.

Psalms 73:14
Thou didst crush the dragon’s heads; Thou gavest him as food to the Ethiopian peoples.
  Ezekiel 29:3-5 — Defeated dragon given as food: Both passages picture God's overthrow of a dragon followed by its being given as food. Ezekiel names Pharaoh and animal or bird consumers; the psalm names Ethiopian peoples as recipients. The dragon identities are not equated.

Psalms 73:15
Thou didst break open springs and torrents; Thou didst dry up rivers of Etham.
  Psalms 113:3-8 — Miraculous control of rivers and springs: Both poems combine retreating or dried waters with waters opened as springs. Psalm113 names the Jordan and a rock; Psalm73 names rivers of Etham. Those names and images are preserved, not equated.

Psalms 73:16
Thine is the day, and Thine is the night; Thou didst fashion light and the sun.
  Genesis 1:14-18 — Creation of day and night lights: The declaration that day and night belong to God and that He fashioned light and sun corresponds to God's making and appointing the heavenly lights to govern day and night.

Psalms 73:17
Thou madest all the earth’s boundaries; summer and spring—Thou didst shape them.
  Genesis 8:22 — Ordered seasons: Summer and spring shaped by God correspond to the post-flood assurance that summer and spring will not cease. Both retain this edition's season names rather than substituting another translation's wording.

Psalms 73:18
Remember this: an enemy reproached the Lord, and a foolish people provoked Thy name.
  Psalms 73:10 — Same-chapter repeated reproach motif: The request to remember the enemy's reproach and provocation of God's name returns to verse10's question about the adversary reproaching that name, renewing the appeal after the creation imagery.

Psalms 73:19
Do not hand over to wild beasts a soul acknowledging Thee; do not forget Thy needy ones’ souls to the end.
  Psalms 9:19 — The needy not forgotten: The plea not to forget needy souls corresponds to the assurance that the poor will not be utterly forgotten and the needy person's endurance will not perish for ever.

Psalms 73:20
Look upon Thy covenant, for the darkened ones of the earth were filled with houses of lawlessness.
  Leviticus 26:42-45 — Appeal to remembered covenant: The plea that God look upon His covenant during violence corresponds to the covenant assurances that He will remember the ancestral covenant and not dissolve it even while His people are in their enemies' land.

Psalms 73:21
Let a humbled person not turn back disgraced; the poor and needy will praise Thy name.
  Psalms 68:31-34 — Praise joined to the poor's relief: The humbled and needy are expected to praise God's name here. Psalm68 joins praise of that name with the poor's rejoicing and the assurance that the Lord hears the needy.

Psalms 73:22
Arise, God, judge Thy case; remember the reproaches against Thee by a fool all day.
  Micah 6:1-2 — God's own judicial case: The appeal that God arise and judge His case corresponds to Micah's announcement that the Lord has a case to argue. Micah names Israel as the opposing party; the psalm's hostile reproachers are not thereby equated with Israel.

Psalms 73:23
Do not forget Thy suppliants’ voice; the arrogance of those hating Thee ascended continually toward Thee.
  2 Kings 19:28 — Arrogance rising to God: The enemies' arrogance ascends continually toward God; the Assyrian oracle likewise says the king's arrogance rose to God's ears. The shared upward image specifies divine awareness of hostile arrogance.

Psalms 74:1
For the end. Do not destroy. A psalm of Asaph, of song.
  Psalms 56:1 — Specific heading correspondence: Both headings contain the instruction Do not destroy. They name Asaph and David and give different additional information, so this does not identify a tune, establish authorship or equate their occasions.

Psalms 74:2
We will acknowledge Thee, God; we will acknowledge and call upon Thy name.
  Psalms 104:1 — Thanksgiving and invoking the name: Acknowledging God and calling upon His name corresponds to the command to give thanks to the Lord and call upon His name. Both join thanksgiving to this act of invocation.

Psalms 74:3
I will recount all Thy wonders when I take an appointed time; I will judge uprightness.
  Acts 17:31 — Appointed time and righteous judgment: The psalm associates an appointed time with judging uprightness; Paul describes a fixed day for judging the world in righteousness. This formal correspondence does not identify the psalm's first-person speaker with Paul's appointed Man.

Psalms 74:4
The earth and all who inhabit it melted; I established its pillars. Diapsalma.
  Job 9:6 — Earth's pillars and stability: The psalm contrasts the melting earth with established pillars; Job describes God shaking the earth so that its pillars sway. Both use earth-pillars imagery, with opposite stability conditions.

Psalms 74:6
do not lift your horn on high; do not speak injustice against God.”
  1 Samuel 2:3 — Proud speech opposed: The command not to lift the horn high or speak injustice against God corresponds to the prohibition of lofty and grandiose speech in Hannah's prayer. The horn image remains the psalm's own wording.

Psalms 74:8
for God is judge: He humbles this one and raises that one.
  1 Samuel 2:7 — God humbling and raising: God's humbling one and raising another closely corresponds to Hannah's declaration that the Lord humbles and raises high, explicitly placing reversals of standing in His hands.

Psalms 74:9
For in the Lord’s hand is a cup of unmixed wine, full of mixture; and He tilted from this into that, but its dregs were not emptied out; all the earth’s sinners will drink.
  Jeremiah 32:15-17 — The Lord's cup of punitive wine: Both passages place a cup of unmixed wine in the Lord's hand and make nations or sinners drink it. The psalm's mixture and dregs details are retained rather than harmonized with Jeremiah.
  Isaiah 51:17-23 — Cup of anger passed to oppressors: The Lord's cup drunk by sinners corresponds to Isaiah's cup of anger taken from Jerusalem and given to her oppressors. Isaiah supplies a transfer between groups, rather than the psalm's precise dregs description.

Psalms 74:10
But I will exult for ever; I will sing to the God of Jacob.
  Psalms 80:2 — Shared praise to Jacob's God: The promise to exult and sing to the God of Jacob corresponds to the invitation to exult and shout to the God of Jacob. The first is a personal pledge; the second calls a community to praise.

Psalms 74:11
And I will break together all sinners’ horns; and the righteous person’s horns will be raised.
  1 Samuel 2:10 — Reversal expressed through horns: The righteous person's raised horn contrasts with broken sinners' horns; Hannah's prayer similarly combines opponents' weakening with the raising of the horn of God's anointed. The righteous figure and anointed are not assumed identical.

Psalms 75:2
God is known in Judea; His name is great in Israel.
  Psalms 147:8-9 — God's special disclosure to Israel: God's being known in Judea and His great name in Israel corresponds to the declaration of His word and judgments to Israel in a way not given to other nations. The latter specifies the disclosure's content.

Psalms 75:3
His place came to be in peace, and His dwelling in Zion.
  Psalms 131:13-14 — God's dwelling in Zion: The placement of God's dwelling in Zion corresponds to the explicit declaration that He chose Zion as His dwelling and enduring resting place.

Psalms 75:4
There He crushed the bows’ powers, weapon and sword and war. Diapsalma.
  Psalms 45:10 — Divine destruction of weapons: Both passages describe the breaking of bows and weapons by God in the ending or defeat of war. Psalm75 locates the action in His dwelling's setting; Psalm45 gives it an earth-wide horizon.

Psalms 75:5
Thou givest light wondrously from everlasting mountains.
  Habakkuk 3:3-4 — Divine light and mountain manifestation: The light from everlasting mountains corresponds to the Holy One's coming from a shaded mountain with brightness like light. The descriptions retain their different mountain qualities and temporal wording.

Psalms 75:7
At Thy rebuke, God of Jacob, those mounted on horses grew drowsy.
  Exodus 15:1 — God overcoming mounted warriors: The drowsiness of mounted warriors at God's rebuke corresponds to the sea song's overthrow of horse and rider. This compares poetic depictions of mounted military power disabled by God, not identical methods or events.

Psalms 75:8
Thou art fearsome, and who will withstand Thee? From that time is Thy wrath.
  Nahum 1:6 — Who can withstand divine wrath: Both passages ask who can stand before God's anger, making the inability to resist His wrath the explicit rhetorical question.

Psalms 75:10
when God arose for judgment, to save all the earth’s meek. Diapsalma.
  Isaiah 11:4 — Judgment concerning the earth's lowly: God arises to save the earth's meek; Isaiah's Jesse-root figure judges the cause of the lowly of the earth. The shared judicial concern for the lowly does not collapse the two acting figures.

Psalms 75:12
Make vows and repay the Lord your God; all around Him will bring gifts
  Deuteronomy 23:22 — Payment of vows: The call to make and repay vows to the Lord corresponds to the law requiring a vow to the Lord to be paid without delay. The law also specifies liability for failure.

Psalms 75:13
to the Fearsome One, who takes away rulers’ spirits, who is fearsome among the earth’s kings.
  Psalms 103:29 — Divine withdrawal of spirit or breath: God takes away rulers' spirits here; Psalm103 describes Him taking creatures' breath so that they fail and return to dust. The correspondence preserves the rulers-versus-creatures scope and different English nouns.

Psalms 76:1
For the end, concerning Jeduthun. Of Asaph. A psalm.
  Psalms 61:1 — Shared heading concerning Jeduthun: Both headings say For the end and concerning Jeduthun, while naming Asaph and David respectively. No authorship determination or identification with differently spelled Chronicles names is required.

Psalms 76:2
With my voice I cried to the Lord, with my voice to God, and He attended to me.
  Psalms 3:5 — Repeated vocal-prayer formula: Both lines say that the speaker cried to the Lord with his voice and received divine attention or hearing. Psalm3 adds the holy mountain as the place of response.

Psalms 76:3
In the day of my affliction I sought God, with my hands at night before Him, and I was not deceived; my soul refused to be comforted.
  Genesis 37:35 — Refusal of consolation: The soul's refusal to be comforted corresponds to Jacob's refusal of his family's comfort while mourning Joseph. The comparison concerns the refusal itself, not an identification of the psalmist's loss.

Psalms 76:4
I remembered God and was glad; I spoke at length, and my spirit lost heart. Diapsalma.
  Psalms 142:4-5 — Despondency alongside remembrance: The movement between remembering God and a fainting spirit corresponds to Psalm142's despondent spirit followed by remembering ancient days and God's works. The psalms order these responses differently.

Psalms 76:5
My eyes anticipated the watches; I was troubled and did not speak.
  Psalms 118:148 — Wakeful eyes: Eyes anticipating the watches correspond to eyes awake before dawn for meditation on God's sayings. The troubled silence here remains distinct from the target's expressly stated meditation purpose.

Psalms 76:6
I considered ancient days, and remembered everlasting years and meditated.
  Deuteronomy 32:7 — Remembering ancient days and years: The speaker considers ancient days and everlasting years; Moses commands remembering old days and considering successive generations' years, directing remembrance toward inherited testimony.

Psalms 76:7
At night I spoke at length with my heart, and my spirit kept searching.
  Psalms 4:5 — Speaking inwardly to the heart: Speaking at night with one's heart corresponds to the instruction to speak in one's heart, followed by remorse upon one's bed. The searching reflection and bedside remorse remain distinct responses.

Psalms 76:8
“Will the Lord reject for the ages, and not again take pleasure?
  Lamentations 3:31-32 — Question and assurance about rejection: The question whether the Lord rejects for the ages corresponds to the assurance that He will not reject for ever and will have compassion after humbling.

Psalms 76:9
Or will He cut off His mercy to the end, from generation to generation?
  Isaiah 54:7-10 — Duration of abandonment and mercy: The question whether mercy will be cut off for ever corresponds to the promise contrasting brief abandonment and anger with everlasting mercy and an enduring covenant of peace.

Psalms 76:10
Or will God forget to show pity, or hold His compassions shut in His wrath?” Diapsalma.
  Isaiah 49:14-15 — Whether God forgets compassion: The question whether God forgets to show pity corresponds to Zion's complaint of being forgotten and God's answer that even if a mother forgets, He will not forget Zion.
