  Habakkuk 3:2 — Mercy remembered within wrath: The question whether wrath shuts divine compassion corresponds to Habakkuk's statement that in wrath God will remember mercy. The different interrogative and affirming forms remain explicit.

Psalms 76:12
I remembered the Lord’s works, for I will remember Thy wonders from the beginning,
  Psalms 104:5 — Remembering God's wonders: The resolve to remember the Lord's works and original wonders corresponds to the command to remember His wonders, portents and judgments.

Psalms 76:13
and meditate on all Thy works, and speak at length about Thy practices.
  Psalms 142:5 — Meditation on divine works: Meditating on all God's works closely repeats the combination of remembering, meditating on all His works and pondering the work of His hands in Psalm142.

Psalms 76:14
God, Thy way is in the Holy One; what god is great like our God?
  Exodus 15:11 — God's incomparable holiness: The question what god is great like Israel's God corresponds to the sea song's question who is like the Lord among the gods, glorious among the holy ones and working wonders.

Psalms 76:15
Thou art the God who does wonders; Thou madest Thy power known among the peoples.
  Exodus 7:3-5 — Wonders revealing divine power to peoples: The declaration that God makes His power known among peoples through wonders corresponds to the promised signs in Egypt through which the Egyptians will know the Lord.

Psalms 76:16
With Thine arm Thou didst redeem Thy people, the sons of Jacob and Joseph. Diapsalma.
  Exodus 6:6 — Redemption by God's arm: Redeeming the people with God's arm corresponds to the promise to ransom Israel from Egyptian slavery with an uplifted arm and great judgment.

Psalms 76:17
The waters saw Thee, God; the waters saw Thee and feared, and the depths were troubled—a multitude of sounding waters.
  Psalms 113:3-7 — Waters reacting to God's presence: The waters see God and fear here; Psalm113 personifies the sea as seeing and fleeing, then places the earth's trembling before the God of Jacob.

Psalms 76:18
The clouds gave voice, for Thine arrows too pass through.
  Habakkuk 3:11 — Divine projectiles in manifestation imagery: God's arrows passing through the sounding clouds correspond to Habakkuk's divine missiles and flashing weapons moving into light. The comparison concerns the theophany's weapon imagery, not identical weather details.

Psalms 76:19
Thy thunder’s voice was in the wheel; Thy flashes of lightning shone upon the inhabited world; the earth shook and became trembling.
  Psalms 17:14-16 — Thunder, lightning and shaken creation: The thunderous divine voice, lightning and trembling earth correspond to Psalm17's heavenly thunder, multiplied lightning and exposed foundations at God's rebuke. The wheel remains this psalm's distinct wording.

Psalms 76:20
Thy way is in the sea, and Thy paths in many waters, and Thy tracks will not be known.
  Exodus 14:21-22 — A way through the sea: The divine way and paths through waters correspond to the sea being divided and Israel walking through its midst on dry ground. Exodus describes the passage, without stating the psalm's hidden-tracks image.

Psalms 76:21
Thou didst guide Thy people like sheep by the hand of Moses and Aaron.
  Micah 6:4 — Moses and Aaron in the Exodus leadership: The people guided by the hands of Moses and Aaron correspond to God's statement that He brought Israel from Egypt and sent Moses, Aaron and Miriam before them. Micah names an additional leader.

Psalms 77:1
Of understanding. Of Asaph. Attend, my people, to my law; bend your ear to the words of my mouth.
  Deuteronomy 5:1 — Call to hear covenant instruction: The psalm's speaker addresses the people with my law and the words of my mouth; Moses likewise calls Israel to hear the ordinances and judgments he speaks in their ears. This compares teaching addresses without assigning the psalm's first-person voice directly to God.

Psalms 77:2
I will open my mouth in parables; I will utter riddles from the beginning,
  Matthew 13:34-35 — Explicit scriptural citation: Matthew introduces as prophetic Scripture the words about opening the mouth in parables and uttering things hidden from the foundation. These correspond to the psalm's parables and original enigmas, with its wording preserved.

Psalms 77:3
whatever we heard and knew, and our fathers recounted to us,
  Deuteronomy 32:7 — Fathers conveying ancient testimony: What the community heard and learned from its fathers corresponds to Moses' instruction to ask fathers and elders about ancient days so that they will tell the next generation.

Psalms 77:4
it was not hidden from their children for another generation, as we announce the Lord’s praises, His mighty acts and the wonders He did.
  Deuteronomy 4:9 — Transmission of divine works to children: The declaration that the wonders were not hidden from the children and the present announcement of God's mighty acts correspond to the command not to forget what Israel saw and to teach sons and sons' sons.

Psalms 77:5
And He raised a testimony in Jacob and set a law in Israel, whatever He commanded our fathers to make known to their sons,
  Deuteronomy 6:6-7 — Commanded intergenerational teaching: God's established testimony and law are given for fathers to teach their sons; Deuteronomy expressly commands teaching these words to one's sons and speaking them during daily activities.

Psalms 77:6
so that another generation might know, sons who will be born; and they will rise and announce it to their sons,
  Deuteronomy 11:19-21 — Teaching succeeding generations: The psalm's children rising to announce the testimony to their own children corresponds to instruction addressed to parents to teach their children, followed by a promise concerning the days of both parents and children.

Psalms 77:7
so that they may set their hope upon God and not forget God’s works, and seek out His commandments,
  Deuteronomy 4:9 — Remembered works and guarded obedience: The purpose is to avoid forgetting God's works and to seek His commandments; Deuteronomy likewise warns against forgetting the witnessed works or letting them depart from the heart.

Psalms 77:8
so that they may not become like their fathers, a crooked and embittering generation, a generation that did not direct its heart, whose spirit was not faithful with God.
  Deuteronomy 32:5-6 — Faithless generation: The crooked and provoking generation whose spirit is not faithful corresponds to Moses' address to a crooked generation that fails to respond rightly to its Father and maker.

Psalms 77:10
They did not keep God’s covenant and were unwilling to walk in His law,
  Hosea 8:1 — Covenant and law transgressed: The psalm joins failure to keep God's covenant with refusal of His law; Hosea joins transgressing His covenant with acting impiously against His law.

Psalms 77:11
and forgot His benefits and the wonders He showed them,
  Psalms 105:13 — Forgotten works after deliverance: The people forget God's benefits and displayed wonders here; Psalm105 says they quickly forgot His works instead of awaiting His counsel.

Psalms 77:12
the wonders He did before their fathers, in Egypt’s land, in the plain of Tanis.
  Exodus 7:3-5 — Wonders in Egypt: The wonders before Israel's fathers in Egypt correspond to God's promised multiplication of signs and portents in Egypt. Exodus does not name the psalm's specific plain of Tanis.

Psalms 77:13
He tore the sea apart and led them through; He stood the waters like a skin-bag,
  Exodus 14:21-22 — Divided sea and crossing: The psalm recounts God splitting the sea and taking the people through; Exodus narrates the divided waters and Israel's passage on dry ground. Skin-bag and wall descriptions remain distinct images.

Psalms 77:14
and guided them with a cloud by day, and all night with the light of fire.
  Exodus 13:21-22 — Cloud and fire guidance: Daytime guidance by cloud and nighttime guidance by fiery light correspond directly to the pillar of cloud by day and pillar of fire by night leading Israel.

Psalms 77:15
He split a rock in the wilderness and gave them drink as in a great deep,
  Exodus 17:6 — Water from rock in the wilderness: The psalm's split rock supplying drink in the wilderness corresponds to the command to strike the rock at Horeb so water will come out for the people to drink.
  1 Corinthians 10:4 — Later interpretation of wilderness rock: Paul speaks of the ancestors drinking from the accompanying spiritual rock and identifies that rock as Christ. This is an explicit later interpretation alongside the psalm's water-giving rock, not an explicit Christ-name in the psalm.

Psalms 77:16
and brought water out of a rock and made waters descend like rivers.
  Numbers 20:11 — Abundant water from rock: The picture of water brought from rock in torrents corresponds to Moses striking the rock and abundant water flowing for the congregation and its animals. This compares the water provision without merging the two wilderness rock narratives.

Psalms 77:17
And they continued still to sin against Him; they embittered the Most High in a waterless place,
  Numbers 20:2-5 — Wilderness complaint despite provision: The continued sin and provocation in a waterless place correspond to the congregation's bitter dispute with Moses and Aaron over the wilderness lacking water and food-producing land.

Psalms 77:18
and put God to the test in their hearts, asking food for their souls,
  Numbers 11:4-6 — Demand for desired food: Testing God by asking food for their souls corresponds to the mixed crowd and Israel craving food and weeping for meat rather than being satisfied with manna. Desire becomes explicit later in the psalm at verses29-30; it is not substituted for souls here.
  Psalms 105:14 — Craving joined to testing: Psalm105 combines desiring a desire in the wilderness with testing God. Psalm77 combines testing in the heart with asking food for their souls, with desire named later at verses29-30. The related food-testing episode does not erase those differences.

Psalms 77:19
and spoke against God and said, “Surely God cannot prepare a table in a wilderness?
  Numbers 11:21-23 — Questioning adequate wilderness food: The people's question whether God can prepare a wilderness table corresponds to Moses asking how the immense people can be supplied with meat. The speakers differ, and God's reply challenges the supposed limit on His hand.

Psalms 77:20
Since He struck a rock and waters flowed, and torrents were flooded, surely He cannot also give bread or prepare a table for His people?”
  Exodus 16:4 — Bread supplied from heaven: The question whether God can give bread after providing rock-water is answered by comparison with His explicit promise to rain bread from heaven for the people. Exodus is a narrative counterpart, not a quoted answer to this line.

Psalms 77:21
Therefore the Lord heard and delayed; and fire was kindled in Jacob, and wrath rose against Israel,
  Numbers 11:1 — Complaint heard and answered with fire: Numbers recounts the Lord hearing a complaint, becoming angry and kindling fire among the people. The psalm likewise connects His hearing with fire and wrath, while retaining its intervening statement that He delayed.

Psalms 77:22
because they did not believe in God nor hope in His salvation.
  Deuteronomy 9:23 — Israel's refusal to believe: The psalm explains the judgment through unbelief in God; Moses likewise accuses Israel of disobeying the command to enter the land and not believing Him or listening to His voice.

Psalms 77:23
And He commanded the clouds above and opened heaven’s doors,
  2 Kings 7:1-2 — Heavenly openings and food provision: Opening heaven's doors for the ensuing manna contrasts with the officer's mention of heavenly floodgates and his doubt about promised food abundance. His wording is not replaced by an even-if clause, and the wilderness and besieged-city events remain distinct.

Psalms 77:24
and rained manna for them to eat, and gave them heaven’s bread.
  Exodus 16:4 — Heavenly bread rained down: The raining of manna as heaven's bread corresponds to God's promise to rain bread from heaven, retaining the same provision and directional imagery.
  John 6:31-33 — Citation and interpretation of heavenly bread: John's speakers cite the giving of bread from heaven while discussing their fathers' manna; Jesus then distinguishes the Father-given true bread. This provides an explicit later citation and interpretation of the shared bread language.

Psalms 77:25
A man ate angels’ bread; He sent them food supplies to fullness.
  Wisdom of Solomon 16:20-21 — Angelic food supplied from heaven: The angels' bread eaten by people corresponds to Wisdom's account of God's feeding His people with angels' food and providing bread from heaven, elaborating the gift's sweetness and adaptability.

Psalms 77:26
He moved the south wind from heaven, and brought the southwest wind by His power,
  Numbers 11:31 — Divinely directed wind for quail: Numbers describes a wind from the Lord bringing quails from the sea. It supports the food-bearing divine wind context, while not supplying the psalm's south and southwest compass directions.

Psalms 77:27
and rained flesh upon them like dust, and winged birds like the sands of seas,
  Numbers 11:31-32 — Abundant birds supplied as food: The raining of flesh and winged birds in immense numbers corresponds to the quails spread in abundance around the camp and gathered by the people.

Psalms 77:28
and they fell within their camp, around their tents,
  Numbers 11:31-32 — Birds around the encampment: The birds fall amid the camp and around its tents; Numbers likewise places the quails all around the camp and describes the people's gathering and cooling them there.

Psalms 77:29
and they ate and were filled exceedingly, and He brought them their desire.
  Numbers 11:18-20 — Desired meat granted: The people's eating and receiving their desire corresponds to God's announced granting of the meat they demanded. Numbers also makes the gift a judgment, so satisfaction is not treated as divine approval of the craving.

Psalms 77:30
They were not deprived of their desire. While their food was still in their mouth,
  Numbers 11:33 — Food still in the mouth: The food is still in the people's mouth before the psalm's next judgment; Numbers places the onset of divine anger while the meat is still between their teeth.

Psalms 77:31
then God’s wrath rose against them, and He killed among their fat ones, and entangled Israel’s chosen men.
  Numbers 11:33-34 — Wrath and death after craving: The deadly anger during the food episode corresponds to the great plague and burial of the people who craved. The psalm's particular description of the victims remains its own wording.

Psalms 77:32
In all these things they sinned still and did not believe in His wonders,
  Numbers 14:11 — Unbelief despite signs: Continued unbelief after the wonders corresponds to God's question how long the people will refuse to believe Him despite all the signs He performed among them.

Psalms 77:33
and their days failed in futility, and their years with haste.
  Numbers 14:29-35 — Years consumed in wilderness judgment: The wasting of their days and years corresponds to the judgment that the rebellious adults' bodies will fall in the wilderness and their children will bear the prolonged wandering.

Psalms 77:34
Whenever He killed them, they sought Him; and they returned and rose early toward God,
  Numbers 21:6-7 — Death followed by turning to God: The people seek God when He kills among them; Numbers narrates deaths from the serpents followed by confession of sin and a request that Moses pray to the Lord.

Psalms 77:35
and remembered that God is their helper, and God Most High is their redeemer.
  Exodus 6:6 — God remembered as redeemer: Remembering the Most High as their redeemer corresponds to His promise to free and ransom the sons of Israel from Egyptian bondage.

Psalms 77:36
And they deceived Him with their mouth, and lied to Him with their tongue,
  Isaiah 29:13 — Lip honor without faithful heart: The people deceive God with their mouth and lie with their tongue here; Isaiah exposes lip honor while the heart remains far away. The psalm's next line similarly diagnoses the heart.

Psalms 77:37
but their heart was not upright with Him, nor were they faithful in His covenant.
  Deuteronomy 32:20 — Children lacking faithfulness: The unstraight heart and unfaithfulness to covenant correspond to Moses' description of a perverse generation and sons without faithfulness.

Psalms 77:38
But He is compassionate; He will make atonement for their sins and not destroy, and will turn back His wrath again and again, and will not kindle all His anger.
  Psalms 102:8-10 — Compassion restraining judgment: Mercy, expiation and repeated restraint of anger correspond to the confession that the Lord is compassionate, slow to anger and does not deal with people according to their sins.

Psalms 77:39
And He remembered that they are flesh, a spirit going away and not returning.
  Psalms 102:14-16 — Mercy mindful of mortal frailty: God remembers that the people are flesh and a departing spirit. Psalm102 states His knowledge of human formation, calls for remembering human dust, and compares mortal flourishing with grass ended by a passing wind. The imperative and the different nouns are preserved.

Psalms 77:40
How often they embittered Him in the wilderness and angered Him in a waterless land!
  Deuteronomy 9:22-24 — Repeated wilderness provocation: The repeated provocation in the wilderness corresponds to Moses' list of places where Israel provoked the Lord and his conclusion that they remained disobedient.

Psalms 77:41
And they turned again and tested God, and provoked the Holy One of Israel.
  Numbers 14:22 — Repeated testing after signs: The repeated testing of God corresponds to His statement that those who saw His glory and signs in Egypt and the wilderness tested Him ten times and did not listen.

Psalms 77:42
They did not remember His hand, the day He redeemed them from an oppressor’s hand,
  Exodus 6:5-6 — Forgotten redemption from oppression: The people fail to remember the hand that ransomed them from affliction; Exodus places God's covenant remembrance alongside His promise to ransom them from Egyptian slavery. The remembering parties differ.

Psalms 77:43
how He set His signs in Egypt and His portents in the plain of Tanis,
  Exodus 7:3-5 — Egyptian signs as divine display: God's setting signs and portents in Egypt corresponds to His announced multiplication of signs and portents there. The plain of Tanis remains a location named only in the psalm's description.

Psalms 77:44
and turned their rivers into blood, and their rain-waters, so that they might not drink.
  Exodus 7:20-21 — Water turned to undrinkable blood: The turning of water into blood so people cannot drink corresponds to the Nile becoming blood, fish dying and Egyptians being unable to drink its water.

Psalms 77:45
He sent a dog-fly among them, and it devoured them, and a frog, and it destroyed them.
  Exodus 8:20 — Dogflies in Egypt: The dog-fly that devours the people corresponds to the multitude of dog-flies entering Pharaoh's and his attendants' houses and ruining the land.
  Exodus 8:2 — Frogs in Egypt: The frog that ruins the Egyptians corresponds to Aaron extending his hand over the waters and frogs coming up to cover Egypt's land. This is the verse's other separately named plague.

Psalms 77:46
And He gave their fruit to mildew, and their labors to the locust.
  Exodus 10:14-15 — Produce consumed by locusts: The giving of their labors to the locust corresponds to locusts consuming the land's vegetation and remaining tree fruit. The psalm separately assigns fruit to mildew, which is not relabeled as locusts.

Psalms 77:47
He killed their vine with hail, and their sycamores with frost.
  Exodus 9:25 — Hail damage to vegetation: The striking of vines with hail corresponds to the hail's destruction of field vegetation and trees. The psalm's additional frost upon sycamores is retained without claiming Exodus supplies that detail.

Psalms 77:48
And He handed their cattle over to hail, and their possessions to fire.
  Exodus 9:23-25 — Hail and fire against livestock and property: The cattle delivered to hail and possessions to fire correspond to the hail with flashing fire striking people, livestock and plants in the field.

Psalms 77:49
He sent upon them the anger of His wrath, wrath, anger and affliction, a mission through evil angels.
  Exodus 12:23 — Destructive agency in Egyptian judgment: Within the Egyptian plague account, the psalm describes a mission through evil angels; Exodus mentions the destroyer barred from marked Israelite houses. Both describe destructive agency in that judgment, without equating the plural angels with the singular destroyer.

Psalms 77:50
He made a road for His wrath; He did not spare their souls from death, and confined their cattle to death,
  Exodus 9:3-6 — Death among Egyptian livestock: The beasts shut up in death correspond to the announced deadly plague upon Egyptian livestock and its execution. This retains the psalm's animal clause rather than substituting an account solely of human deaths.

Psalms 77:51
and struck every firstborn in Egypt, the firstfruits of their labors in Ham’s tents.
  Exodus 12:29 — Egyptian firstborn struck: The smiting of every firstborn in Egypt corresponds directly to the midnight striking of Egyptian firstborn from Pharaoh's house to the captive, together with livestock firstborn.

Psalms 77:52
And He led His people away like sheep, and brought them up like a flock in the wilderness,
  Psalms 76:21 — Exodus guidance as flock-leading: Both psalms describe God guiding His people like sheep. Psalm76 names Moses and Aaron as the means; Psalm77 places this flock-leading in the wilderness after Egypt's judgment.

Psalms 77:53
and guided them in hope, and they did not grow cowardly; and the sea covered their enemies.
  Exodus 14:28-31 — Safe passage and enemies overwhelmed: The people's secure leading and their enemies' covering by the sea correspond to Israel crossing safely while the waters cover Pharaoh's forces, followed by Israel's trust in the Lord.

Psalms 77:54
And He brought them into the boundary of His sanctuary, this mountain which His right hand acquired,
  Exodus 15:17 — Inheritance mountain and divine sanctuary: Bringing the people to the holy mountain acquired by God's right hand corresponds to the sea song's anticipation of bringing and planting them on God's inheritance mountain and prepared sanctuary.

Psalms 77:55
and expelled nations from before them, and assigned them by a cord of inheritance, and settled Israel’s tribes in their tents.
  Joshua 11:23 — Tribal land inheritance after displacement: The dispossession of nations and allocation of land to Israel's tribes correspond to Joshua's taking the land and giving it as an inheritance divided among their tribes.

Psalms 77:56
And they tested and embittered God Most High, and did not keep His testimonies,
  Judges 2:11-14 — Rebellion after settlement: The refusal to keep God's testimonies after land settlement corresponds to Judges' account of Israel doing evil, abandoning the Lord and turning to the surrounding peoples' gods.

Psalms 77:57
and turned back and broke faith just like their fathers, and changed into a crooked bow,
  Hosea 7:16 — Infidelity represented by a bow: The people's turning aside like a crooked bow corresponds to Hosea's turning-away charge and bow comparison. This edition calls Hosea's bow drawn, not crooked; the image is related without claiming identical wording.

Psalms 77:58
and angered Him with their hills, and provoked Him to jealousy with their carvings.
  Deuteronomy 32:16-19 — Idols provoking jealousy: Anger through the people's hills and jealousy through their carvings correspond to Moses' description of Israel provoking God with foreign gods and abominations, followed by His jealousy. The psalm's hills and carvings remain its own wording.

Psalms 77:59
God heard and disregarded, and counted Israel as utterly nothing,
  Jeremiah 7:13-15 — Rejection after disobedience: God hears and rejects Israel here; Jeremiah describes ignored divine speech followed by rejection from His presence, explicitly recalling Ephraim's rejection.

Psalms 77:60
and rejected Shiloh’s tent, His dwelling, where He encamped among humans.
  Jeremiah 7:12 — Abandonment of Shiloh: The rejection of the tent at Shiloh corresponds to God's instruction to consider what He did to Shiloh, where His name formerly dwelt, because of Israel's evil.

Psalms 77:61
And He handed their strength over to captivity, and their beauty into an enemy’s hands,
  1 Samuel 4:17-22 — Captured glory in the Shiloh narrative: The people's strength and beauty are handed to captivity and an enemy in the psalm's Shiloh context; Samuel narrates the ark's capture and Israel's glory carried away. This is a contextual literary correspondence, not a replacement of the psalm's nouns with ark.

Psalms 77:62
and confined His people to the sword, and disregarded His inheritance.
  1 Samuel 4:10 — Israel given to the sword: The handing of God's people to the sword corresponds to Israel's defeat and the great slaughter in the battle that precedes the ark's capture.

Psalms 77:64
Their priests fell by the sword, and their widows will not be wept for.
  1 Samuel 4:11 — Deaths of the priests in the ark battle: The priests falling by the sword correspond to the deaths of Hophni and Phinehas when the ark was taken; their priestly office is supplied in 1Samuel1:3. Samuel does not expressly state that the widows will not be wept for, as the psalm does.

Psalms 77:65
And the Lord awoke like one sleeping, like a powerful man heavy with wine,
  Psalms 43:24 — Awakening imagery addressed to the Lord: The Lord's rising as from sleep corresponds to the plea that He awaken and the question why He sleeps. Both use sleep imagery for divine apparent inactivity; the heavy-with-wine simile belongs only to Psalm77.

Psalms 77:67
And He rejected Joseph’s dwelling, and did not choose Ephraim’s tribe.
  Jeremiah 7:15 — Ephraim rejected: The refusal to choose Ephraim's tribe corresponds to God's explicit statement that He threw away all Ephraim's offspring. The psalm additionally names Joseph's dwelling.

Psalms 77:68
And He chose Judah’s tribe, Mount Zion, which He loved,
  1 Chronicles 28:4 — Judah chosen: God's choice of Judah corresponds to David's explanation that the Lord chose Judah for royal rule. Chronicles narrows the purpose to kingship, while the psalm continues into Zion's choice.
  Psalms 131:13 — Zion chosen as dwelling: The choice and love of Mount Zion correspond to the declaration that the Lord chose Zion and selected her as His dwelling.

Psalms 77:69
and built His sanctuary like that of one-horned beasts; in the earth He founded it for ever.
  1 Kings 9:3 — Enduring sanctuary establishment: The sanctuary built with enduring establishment corresponds to God's consecration of Solomon's house with His name there for ever. The one-horned-beasts comparison is preserved without being reinterpreted as heights or architectural features.

Psalms 77:70
And He chose David His servant, and took him up from the flocks of sheep;
  1 Samuel 16:11-13 — David selected from shepherding: God chooses David and takes him from the flocks of sheep here; Samuel narrates the youngest son being fetched from tending the flock and anointed in the midst of his brothers.

Psalms 77:71
from behind the ewes giving birth He took him, to shepherd Jacob His people and Israel His inheritance.
  2 Samuel 7:8 — From flock to rule: David is brought from behind the ewes giving birth to shepherd God's people; God's oracle says He took David from the sheepfold to become ruler over His people Israel. Following the flock is not inserted as an additional phrase in the target.
  1 Chronicles 11:2 — David shepherding Israel: The task of shepherding Jacob and Israel corresponds to the Lord's reported command that David shepherd His people Israel and become their ruler.

Psalms 77:72
And he shepherded them in his heart’s innocence, and guided them with his hands’ understanding.
  1 Kings 9:4 — David's upright-hearted rule: David shepherds with an innocent heart and skill here; God's instruction to Solomon cites David's conduct with holiness of heart and uprightness. The practical skill clause remains a distinct psalm detail.

Psalms 78:1
A psalm for Asaph. God, nations entered Thine inheritance; they defiled Thy holy temple; they made Jerusalem into a fruit-watchman’s hut.
  1 Maccabees 4:38 — Desecrated sanctuary and devastated precinct: The lament over the holy temple's defilement corresponds to the inspection of a desolate sanctuary, profaned altar and burned gates. The correspondence does not establish that both texts describe the same invasion.
  Isaiah 1:8 — Zion reduced to a watcher's shelter: Jerusalem's becoming a fruit-watchman's hut corresponds to daughter Zion left as a fruit-watcher's shelter and a besieged city. Both use an exposed agricultural shelter for the city's diminished condition.

Psalms 78:2
They made the dead bodies of Thy servants food for the birds of heaven, the flesh of Thy holy ones for the beasts of the earth.
  Jeremiah 7:33 — Corpses left to birds and beasts: The servants' bodies and holy ones' flesh given to birds and beasts correspond to Jeremiah's warning that the people's corpses will become food for heaven's birds and earth's beasts.
  1 Maccabees 7:16-17 — Later scriptural quotation: Maccabees explicitly introduces the flesh of Thy holy ones as part of a written quotation, joining this verse's flesh wording to the blood around Jerusalem and absent burial of the next verse. Its pouring-out wording is preserved.

Psalms 78:3
They poured out their blood like water around Jerusalem, and there was no one to bury them.
  1 Maccabees 7:16-17 — Later scriptural quotation: Maccabees quotes the pouring of blood around Jerusalem with no one to bury, applying the psalm's distinctive language to the killing narrated there.

Psalms 78:4
We became a reproach to our neighbors, sneering and ridicule to those around us.
  Psalms 43:14 — Repeated reproach formula: Both verses describe becoming a reproach to neighbors and a mockery and derision to those around, repeating the same communal humiliation formula.

Psalms 78:5
How long, Lord? Wilt Thou be angry to the end? Will Thy jealousy blaze like fire?
  Psalms 88:47 — How-long question and fiery wrath: Both laments ask How long of the Lord, speak of persistence to the end and compare divine anger with blazing fire. Jealousy here and wrath in Psalm88 retain their distinct wording.

Psalms 78:6
Pour out Thy wrath upon nations that do not know Thee, and upon kingdoms that did not call on Thy name,
  Jeremiah 10:25 — Close verbal parallel in the wrath petition: Both ask God to pour wrath on peoples that do not know Him and communities that do not call on His name. The psalm's kingdoms and Jeremiah's generations remain distinct labels.

Psalms 78:7
for they devoured Jacob and made his place desolate.
  Jeremiah 10:25 — Close verbal parallel about Jacob: Both explain the petition by saying the enemies consumed Jacob and desolated his place, linking the plea for judgment to the same named victim and devastation.

Psalms 78:8
Do not remember our ancient acts of lawlessness; let Thy compassions swiftly reach us first, for we became very poor.
  Psalms 24:6-7 — Selective remembrance of mercy and sins: The plea not to remember ancient lawlessness and to bring compassion corresponds to the request to remember ancient mercies rather than the speaker's youthful sins. The communal and personal confessions remain distinct.

Psalms 78:9
Help us, God our Savior. For the glory of Thy name, Lord, rescue us, and be propitious toward our sins for Thy name’s sake,
  Numbers 14:15-19 — Forgiveness and God's reputation among nations: The appeal for rescue and expiation for God's name corresponds to Moses' appeal concerning what foreign nations will say, followed by his request to forgive the people's sin according to great mercy.

Psalms 78:10
lest the nations say, “Where is their God?” And let vengeance for the poured-out blood of Thy servants be known among the nations before our eyes.
  Psalms 113:9-11 — Nations asking where God is: The question why nations should ask where Israel's God is repeats the challenge in Psalm113, whose preceding appeal likewise concerns God's name, mercy and truth.

Psalms 78:11
Let the groaning of those in bonds enter before Thee; according to the greatness of Thine arm, preserve the sons of those put to death.
  Psalms 101:21 — Prisoners and sons of those put to death: Both passages combine the groan of bound people with concern for the sons of those put to death. One petitions for their preservation; the other describes God's hearing and release.

Psalms 78:12
Repay our neighbors sevenfold into their bosom their insult with which they insulted Thee, Lord.
  Leviticus 26:24 — Sevenfold punitive repayment: The requested sevenfold repayment for neighbors' reproach corresponds to the law's sevenfold punishment for Israel's sins. The numerical punishment pattern is shared, while the recipients differ and the law is not presented as an instruction for the psalmist's appeal.

Psalms 78:13
But we, Thy people and the sheep of Thy pasture, will give Thee thanks for ever; from generation to generation we will proclaim Thy praise.
  Psalms 99:3-5 — Pasture people responding with thanksgiving: The community calls itself God's people and pasture sheep and pledges praise; Psalm99 uses that same communal identification and proceeds to thanksgiving and praise of His enduring goodness.

Psalms 79:1
For the end; concerning those who will be changed. A testimony for Asaph; a psalm concerning the Assyrian.
  Psalms 59:1 — Specific heading correspondence: Both headings direct attention to those who will be changed, with yet additionally present in Psalm59. Their other directions and historical notices differ; this correspondence does not identify the Assyrian occasion of Psalm79.

Psalms 79:2
Thou who shepherdest Israel, pay attention, Thou who guidest Joseph like sheep; Thou who sittest upon the cherubim, appear.
  1 Samuel 4:4 — God seated upon the cherubim: The appeal to the One seated upon the cherubim corresponds to the description of the Lord associated with the covenant ark and seated upon the cherubim.

Psalms 79:3
Before Ephraim and Benjamin and Manasseh, rouse Thy dominion, and come to save us.
  Numbers 2:18-24 — Specific three-tribe grouping: Ephraim, Benjamin and Manasseh form the prayer's named group; Numbers places Manasseh and Benjamin in the camp formation led by Ephraim. This explains a concrete tribal grouping without dating the psalm to the march.

Psalms 79:4
God, turn us back; make Thy face shine, and we shall be saved.
  Numbers 6:25 — The divine face shining for favor: The plea that God's face appear brightly so the people will be saved corresponds to the priestly blessing asking Him to make His face shine upon the recipient and show mercy.

Psalms 79:5
Lord, God of powers, how long art Thou angry at the prayer of Thy servant,
  Lamentations 3:8 — Prayer encountering divine opposition: The psalm asks how long God is angry at His servant's prayer; Lamentations describes crying out and calling while God blocks the prayer. Both lament prayer itself encountering divine opposition.

Psalms 79:6
wilt Thou feed us bread of tears and give us tears to drink by measure?
  Psalms 41:4 — Tears as food: The bread of tears and tears given to drink by measure correspond to the personal lament that tears became the speaker's bread day and night. The measured drinking image is an additional feature here.

Psalms 79:7
Thou madest us an object of contradiction for our neighbors, and our enemies sneered at us.
  Psalms 43:14 — Communal ridicule by neighbors: Being made an object of contradiction for neighbors while enemies sneer corresponds to being made a reproach to neighbors and scorn and ridicule to surrounding people. Both address God as the One who has placed the community in this humiliation.

Psalms 79:8
Lord, God of powers, turn us back; make Thy face shine, and we shall be saved. Diapsalma.
  Psalms 79:4 — Same-chapter restoration refrain: The request to turn the people back and make God's face shine so they will be saved repeats verse4 after the tear-food and enemy-ridicule complaints, now adding powers to God's title.

Psalms 79:9
Thou didst transplant a vine from Egypt; Thou didst cast out nations and plant her.
  Psalms 43:3 — Nations displaced and a people planted: The vine's transplantation from Egypt, the casting out of nations and its planting correspond to Psalm43's statement that God's hand destroyed nations and planted the ancestors. Both use planting for the people's settlement, without rearranging the psalm's stated actions.

Psalms 79:10
Thou madest a road before her and didst plant her roots, and the land was filled.
  Jeremiah 12:2 — Planted growth taking root: The vine's rooting and filling the land corresponds to Jeremiah's description of people planted by God, taking root and producing growth. Jeremiah speaks of prosperous wicked people, not the psalm's collective vine identity.

Psalms 79:12
She stretched her branches as far as the sea, and her offshoots as far as the River.
  Exodus 23:31 — Extent described by sea and river: The vine's branches reaching sea and river correspond to Israel's promised boundaries running to the sea and river. The psalm's unnamed bodies of water are not assigned more precise identities than its text supplies.

Psalms 79:13
Why didst Thou tear down her fence, so that all who pass along the road harvest her?
  Isaiah 5:5 — Removed vineyard protection and plunder: The question why God tore down the vine's fence so passersby plunder it corresponds to His announced removal of His vineyard's fence so it becomes spoil and is trampled.

Psalms 79:15
God of powers, turn back now; look from heaven and see, and visit this vine,
  Isaiah 63:15 — Appeal to look down from heaven: The request that God turn back, look from heaven and see corresponds to Isaiah's appeal that He turn from heaven and see from His holy dwelling amid a complaint about withheld compassion.

Psalms 79:16
and restore her whom Thy right hand planted, and upon the son of man whom Thou didst strengthen for Thyself.
  Psalms 79:18 — Same-chapter strengthened-son repetition: The son of man whom God strengthened for Himself recurs with the same wording in verse18, where he is paired with the man of God's right hand. The repetition is recorded without settling the figure's identity.

Psalms 79:17
Burned with fire and dug up— at the rebuke of Thy face they will perish.
  John 15:6 — Burned vine imagery: The vine's burning in the psalm corresponds to the gathering and burning of branches in Jesus' vine discourse. The collective vine here and branches failing to abide in Jesus are different referents and explanations.

Psalms 79:18
Let Thy hand come upon the man of Thy right hand and upon the son of man whom Thou didst strengthen for Thyself.
  Psalms 79:16 — Same-chapter strengthened-son repetition: The petition for God's hand upon the man of His right hand repeats verse16's son-of-man clause, connecting the two petitions without identifying that person as a particular king or as Christ.

Psalms 79:19
And we shall certainly not depart from Thee; Thou wilt give us life, and we will call on Thy name.
  Psalms 84:7 — Renewed communal life and response to God: The assurance that God will give life and the people will call on His name corresponds to Psalm84's affirmation that He will turn back and give life and His people will rejoice in Him. The promised responses differ but both follow communal renewal.

Psalms 79:20
Lord, God of powers, turn us back; make Thy face shine, and we shall be saved.
  Psalms 79:4 — Same-chapter closing restoration refrain: The final appeal repeats verse4's turning, shining face and salvation clauses, closing the vine petitions with the fuller title Lord God of powers.

Psalms 80:1
For the end; concerning the winepresses. A psalm for Asaph.
  Psalms 8:1 — Specific winepress heading: Both headings give the direction For the end, concerning the winepresses. One says a psalm for Asaph and the other a psalm of David; the persons named and the prepositions are preserved without deciding authorship or the heading's musical or historical meaning.

Psalms 80:2
Exult in God our helper; raise a shout to the God of Jacob.
  Psalms 94:1 — Shared opening invitation to praise: Both openings invite exultation in the Lord or God and a shout to God as helper or Savior. The God-of-Jacob title is an additional specification in Psalm80.

Psalms 80:3
Take up a psalm and sound the tambourine, a delightful psaltery together with a lyre.
  Psalms 149:3 — Tambourine and psaltery in praise: The command to sound the tambourine and psaltery corresponds to praise of God's name with tambourine and psaltery. The lyre additionally named here is not supplied by the target.

Psalms 80:4
Sound the trumpet at the new moon, on the conspicuous day of our feast.
  Numbers 10:10 — Trumpets at new moons and festivals: The trumpet at the new moon and feast corresponds to the ordinance to sound trumpets on feast days and new moons, giving the same specific ceremonial occasions.

Psalms 80:5
For it is an ordinance for Israel and a judgment of the God of Jacob.
  Numbers 10:10 — Festival trumpet as an ordinance: Calling the preceding trumpet observance an ordinance for Israel corresponds to the divine instruction commanding trumpets at new moons and feasts as a memorial before God. The connection depends on the read verse4 context.

Psalms 80:7
He removed his back from burdens; his hands served at the basket.
  Exodus 6:6 — Removal of Egyptian burdens: The removal of the back from burdens corresponds to God's promise to bring Israel out from Egyptian oppression and slavery. The psalm's basket work remains its more particular labor image.

Psalms 80:8
In affliction you called on Me, and I delivered you; I heard you in the hidden place of the storm; I tested you at the water of contention. Diapsalma.
  Numbers 20:13 — Water-dispute descriptions: The psalm names God's testing at the water of contention; Numbers names the Water of Dispute, where Israel reviled the Lord and He was shown holy among them. The close water-dispute descriptions are compared without exchanging the parties' different actions.

Psalms 80:9
Hear, My people, and I solemnly testify to you; Israel, if you listen to Me,
  Deuteronomy 5:1 — Israel summoned to hear: God's address Hear, My people, Israel corresponds to Moses' summons for Israel to hear the statutes and judgments, in each case introducing covenant instruction.

Psalms 80:10
There shall be no new god among you, nor shall you bow down to a foreign god.
  Exodus 20:3-5 — Prohibition of other gods and their worship: The ban on a new or foreign god and bowing to one corresponds to the Decalogue's prohibition of other gods and bowing to or serving their images.

Psalms 80:11
For I am the Lord your God, who brought you up from the land of Egypt. Open your mouth wide, and I will fill it.
  Exodus 20:2 — Exodus-based divine self-identification: The Lord identifies Himself as Israel's God who brought it from Egypt, corresponding directly to the Decalogue's opening self-identification. The promise to fill an opened mouth is the psalm's additional wording.

Psalms 80:12
And My people did not listen to My voice, and Israel did not pay attention to Me.
  Jeremiah 7:13 — Divine speech met by refusal to listen: Israel's not listening to God's voice corresponds to His complaint that He spoke and called but the people did not listen or answer.

Psalms 80:13
So I sent them away according to the practices of their hearts; they will walk in their practices.
  Romans 1:24 — God handing people over to their desires: Sending the people away according to their hearts' practices corresponds to God's handing people over in their hearts' desires. The communities and specified subsequent conduct differ.

Psalms 80:14
If My people had listened to Me, if Israel had walked in My ways,
  Isaiah 48:18 — Unrealized blessing after refusal to listen: The conditional lament If My people had listened corresponds to Isaiah's If you had listened to My commandments, followed in both contexts by blessings that would have followed obedience.

Psalms 80:15
in no time I would have humbled their enemies and laid My hand upon those who afflict them.
  Deuteronomy 28:7 — Defeat of enemies under the obedience promise: God would have humbled Israel's enemies under the preceding obedience condition; Deuteronomy's blessing for obedience likewise promises that the Lord will hand opposing enemies over to be crushed.

Psalms 80:16
The Lord’s enemies lied to Him, and their appointed time will be for ever.
  Psalms 65:3 — Enemies lying to God: Both explicitly describe God's enemies lying to Him. Psalm65 addresses God and says the enemies will lie through His abundant power; Psalm80 says the Lord's enemies lied to Him. The appointed-time clause remains distinct and is not interpreted through this comparison.

Psalms 80:17
And He fed them with the finest wheat, and with honey from rock He satisfied them.
  Deuteronomy 32:13-14 — Honey from rock and wheat: Both passages combine honey supplied from rock with wheat provision. The psalm calls it finest wheat; Deuteronomy names the fat of the kernels of wheat. These particular foods and differing descriptions are preserved.
